Neural Networks Provably Learn Spectral Representations for Group Composition
Jianliang He, Leda Wang, Fengzhuo Zhang, Siyu Chen, Zhuoran Yang, Department of Statistics and Data Science, Yale University, {jianliang.he, leda.wang, fengzhuo.zhang, siyu.chen.sc3226, zhuoran.yang}@yale.edu

Abstract
Understanding how structured internal structure emerges during neural network training is central to the study of deep learning. We investigate this phenomenon through the group composition task, where a two-layer neural network is trained to predict g 1 ⋆ g 2 g_{1}\star g_{2} for elements of a finite group G G . By lifting the projected gradient flow to the Fourier domain, we demonstrate that the training dynamics are governed by a Riemannian gradient ascent on a representation-theoretic energy functional. We prove that, under random initialization, this flow drives each neuron to converge almost surely toward a single irreducible representation, while the cross-layer Fourier coefficients achieve a rotational rank-one alignment. This framework provides a representation-theoretic account of feature learning and characterizes a novel low-rank compression phenomenon for matrix-valued group representations. Moreover, for Abelian groups, we provide a complete population-level description: random initialization promotes uniform diversification across nontrivial representations and induces Haar-uniform phases, jointly approximating the indicator via a majority-vote mechanism. We further prove that both phase alignment and representation competition emerge with exponential convergence rates. Our code is available at github.com/Y-Agent/nn-group-representation-learning .



1
Introduction

Understanding how useful internal structure emerges during neural network training remains a central challenge in deep learning theory.
Two broad questions are especially persistent. The first concerns
representation learning
: when data contain latent regularities, what internal representations do neural networks extract from raw inputs, and how do those representations support computation and generalization
(
6
)
?
The second concerns
low intrinsic dimensionality
: despite optimization taking place in extremely high-dimensional parameter spaces, trained networks often concentrate on solutions with much lower-dimensional or low-rank structure, both in parameter space and in learned representations
(
19
;
3
;
2
)
.
These questions are distinct, but they point to a common problem:
How gradient-based training selects
structured, effective latent geometries
from massively overparameterized models?

A line of work in mechanistic interpretability seeks to answer this by directly reverse-engineering the features that emerge in trained networks.
Algebraic tasks provide a powerful lens for this goal, offering “minimal testbed” where structural emergence can be studied in a controlled manner.
In particular, data generated from groups possesses rigid symmetries and compositional structure, suggesting that well-trained networks may discover representations that reflect this underlying algebra.
For this reason, group-theoretic tasks have become an important paradigm in the literature
(
9
;
24
, e.g.,)
, with modular addition over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
serving as a canonical example.
Empirical studies on modular addition have shown that networks consistently extract trigonometric features aligned with the discrete Fourier basis
(
28
;
27
, e.g.,)
.
Recently,
13
provided the first rigorous theoretical characterization of this phenomenon, establishing the emergence of three distinct structural patterns:
(i) Each neuron specializes in a sinusoid defined by a single Fourier frequency
k
,
(ii) the phases of the first and second layer Fourier coefficients align according to a additive relation, and
(iii) the learned frequencies and phases are independently and uniformly distributed across neurons.
Together, these observations yield a closed-form ensemble predictor that assigns the correct label to the largest logit, and the growth in scale sharpens the predictions to achieve zero loss.

In modular addition, the Fourier modes discovered by the network are not accidental artifacts of training.
They have a precise mathematical interpretation: they are the
irreducible representations
(irreps) of the underlying cyclic group
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.
From this viewpoint, the phenomenon often described as “single-frequency specialization” is not merely a neuron fitting one sinusoidal pattern.
Rather, it reflects a representation-theoretic organization of computation, in which individual neurons concentrate their capacity on particular spectral components.
This also formalizes the
low-dimensional nature
of the learned solution: rather than relying on an arbitrary superposition of Fourier modes, each neuron isolates a single one-dimensional component of the group decomposition.
The natural question is whether the same principle persists beyond the cyclic case.
For non-Abelian groups, however, these representations are no longer scalar-valued frequencies, but
matrix-valued objects comprising multiple coupled coefficients
.
Thus, extending the modular-addition framework beyond
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
requires asking not only whether networks select individual irreps, but also how they organize the latent matrix structure within those irreps.
This leads to our central question:

When a neural network learns to compose elements of an arbitrary finite group
G
, does it discover the irreducible representations of
G
? And if so, do the alignment and low-rank principles observed in modular addition generalize to this broader setting?

Main Contributions.

We study the group composition task
g_{1}\star g_{2}
for arbitrary finite groups
(G,\star)
, using harmonic analysis on finite groups.
Concretely, we analyze the two-layer network with quadratic activation whose logits take the form

\displaystyle f_{\sf NN}(g_{1},g_{2};\Theta)=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}a_{m}\cdot\xi_{m}\cdot\sigma\left(\theta_{m,g_{1}}^{1}+\theta_{m,g_{2}}^{2}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{|G|},

where
\theta_{m}^{1},\theta_{m}^{2},\xi_{m}:G\to\mathbb{R}
are the two input embeddings and the output embedding for neuron
m
, and
a_{m}>0
is a neuron scaling factor.
Since a general group operation need not be commutative, the two inputs play distinct left and right roles.
This asymmetry motivates our use of separate input embeddings for the two operands.
We train on the complete composition table, comprising all pairs
(g_{1},g_{2})\in G\times G
with label
g_{1}\star g_{2}
, and analyze the projected gradient flow of the directional parameters under the cross-entropy risk.
By lifting the training dynamics into the spectral domain via the group Fourier transform, we uncover fundamental structural links between the observations from
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and the representation theory of
G
.
Our results provide an affirmative answer to the question above. These findings are summarized in the following informal theorem:

Informal Theorem

(Informal Version of Theorem
4.3
)
.

Consider the gradient flow under the population risk.
We write
\nu\in\{\theta_{m}^{1},\theta_{m}^{2},\xi_{m}\}
and view each
\nu
as a function

G\to\mathbb{R}
.
Then, for every neuron
m
, with probability one over the continuous random initialization, there exists a non-trivial irreducible representation
\check{\rho}_{m}
such that, as
t\to\infty
, the
gradient flow converges to a state satisfying the following:

(i)

(Single Representation).

In the Fourier domain, let
\widehat{\nu}[\rho]
denote the Fourier coefficient that measures the component of
\nu
along the
representation
\rho
. Each neuron keeps only
\check{\rho}_{m}
and its conjugate
\check{\rho}_{m}^{\vee}
:

\widehat{\nu}[\rho]\to{\bm{0}}_{d_{\rho}\times d_{\rho}},\qquad\forall\rho\notin\{\check{\rho}_{m},\check{\rho}_{m}^{\vee}\},~\nu\in\{\theta_{m}^{1},\theta_{m}^{2},\xi_{m}\},

where
d_{\rho}\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
represents the dimension of the corresponding representation.

(ii)

(Rank-one Rotational Alignment).

On the surviving representation
\rho\in\{\check{\rho}_{m},\check{\rho}_{m}^{\vee}\}
, the three
Fourier coefficients (as a matrix in general) become rank-one:

\mathrm{rank}(\widehat{\theta}_{m}^{1}[\rho])=\mathrm{rank}(\widehat{\theta}_{m}^{2}[\rho])=\mathrm{rank}(\widehat{\xi}_{m}[\rho])=1
.
Moreover, these coefficients have some alignment across layers, i.e., there exists
\lambda>0
such that

\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]=\lambda\cdot\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho]\,\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho],\qquad\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho]=\lambda\cdot\big(\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho]\big)^{*}\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho],\qquad\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho]=\lambda\cdot\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]\,\big(\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho]\big)^{*},

where
(\cdot)^{*}
denotes the Hermitian adjoint.
In other words, each of
\widehat{\theta}_{m}^{1}[\rho]
,
\widehat{\theta}_{m}^{2}[\rho]
and

\widehat{\xi}_{m}[\rho]
is positively proportional to the product formed by the other two
coefficients in a rotational order.

This theorem characterizes representation learning across arbitrary finite groups.
It establishes that the Fourier-feature mechanism discovered in modular addition extends far beyond cyclic groups: gradient flow selects irreducible representations, compresses surviving matrix-valued coefficients to rank one, and aligns them across layers according to the group-composition structure.

To establish this result, we show that each neuron’s weights approximately evolve as a Riemannian gradient flow on a constrained manifold (see Proposition
4.2
,
B.1
).
Moreover, this flow is driven by a specific energy functional
\Omega_{m}
(see Lemma
B.3
).
We then prove that every non-target equilibrium is either a measure-zero trap or a strict saddle.
To address the latter, we extend the saddle-avoidance result of
17
to continuous Riemannian gradient flows, establishing that the stable sets of strict saddles have measure zero under absolutely continuous random initialization (see Theorem
4.4
).
Consequently, the dynamics avoid all unstable critical points with probability one and converge to the desired single-representation, rank-one rotationally aligned equilibrium.

Refined Characterization for Abelian Groups.

For Abelian groups, we provide a comprehensive characterization of the learned parameters, the emergent mechanism, and the underlying training dynamics in §
5
.
Specifically, under uniform spherical initialization, the learned representation
\check{\rho}_{m}
is uniformly distributed over the set of non-trivial irreducible representations (see Theorem
5.1
).
Furthermore, the absolute phase
u_{m}
is Haar-distributed on the unit circle
\mathbb{D}
, independent of the surviving representation
\check{\rho}_{m}
.
This full diversification yields a closed-form ensemble predictor: by orthogonality of representations and averaging over the Haar measure, the noise terms cancel exactly while the signal terms combine into a “flawed indicator” that assigns the correct label
g_{1}\star g_{2}
the largest logit.
Finally, we establish explicit convergence rates, demonstrating that phase alignment occurs exponentially fast and a lottery-ticket mechanism governs that representation competition.
In the latter regime, the initially dominant representation wins almost surely, while all competing representations are exponentially suppressed (see Theorem
5.3
).

1.1
Related Work

Modular Arithmetic and Group Operations.

Modular arithmetic and finite group operations have become central testbeds for mechanistic interpretability, feature emergence, and grokking
(
28
;
27
;
38
;
21
;
26
;
29
;
22
;
20
)
. In modular addition, mechanistic studies show that trained networks often implement Fourier-structured algorithms, and that these circuits provide useful progress measures during training
(
27
;
38
)
. More generally, reverse-engineering studies of finite group operations show that representation theory gives a useful language for learned circuits
(
9
;
35
;
40
)
. A complementary theoretical perspective is provided by
23
, who show that finite-group invariance can force learned weights to recover the group Fourier transform. The closest predecessor to our Abelian analysis is
13
, who analyze modular addition over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and explain single-frequency learning, phase alignment, diversification, the flawed-indicator mechanism, and grokking in the cyclic setting.
39
broaden this picture by deriving scaling laws for feature emergence in group arithmetic. Our Abelian results extend this line of work to arbitrary finite Abelian groups, while our general-group results show which parts remain valid when scalar Fourier characters are replaced by matrix-valued irreducible representations.

24
study a different but closely related problem: sequential group composition with orbit-based embeddings, analyzed through Alternating Gradient Flow
(
16
, AGF,)
. Their emphasis is on the step-wise progression of representation learning. Instead, we study two-input composition with one-hot embeddings and standard gradient flow, which allows us to analyze the emergence of the structured spectral patterns.

Training Dynamics of Neural Networks.

A complementary line of work investigates feature learning directly through the dynamics of gradient-based optimization. Recent mechanics-inspired analyses highlight structural phenomena within these dynamics, such as symmetry breaking, directional-versus-radial learning phases, and alternating mechanisms for feature selection and growth
(
36
;
16
)
. In parallel, a broad literature studies these questions on structured low-dimensional targets, including single-index, multi-index, and more general latent-feature classes, where gradient methods recover task-relevant subspaces and exhibit staircase or multi-timescale learning dynamics
(
4
;
18
;
8
;
7
;
10
;
30
)
. Our work fits this dynamics perspective, but in a representation-theoretic setting where the relevant spectral features are not prescribed a priori.
Ultimately, by leveraging the tractable yet rich group composition task to uncover these underlying mechanisms, our work serves as a concrete realization of the “learning mechanics” paradigm
(
33
)
, moving beyond mere end-performance certification to provide exact, falsifiable predictions about the emergent internal organization.

1.2
Summary of Notation

General Notation.

For any positive integer
n\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, let
[n]=\{i\in\mathbb{Z}:1\leq i\leq n\}
.
Let
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
denote the set of integers modulo
p
.

\|\cdot\|_{p}
denotes the
\ell_{p}
-norm,
\|\cdot\|_{\rm F}
denotes the Frobenius norm, and
\|\cdot\|_{\rm op}
refers to the operator norm.
The softmax operator,
{\mathrm{smax}}(\cdot)
, maps a vector to a probability distribution, where the
i
-th component is given by
{\mathrm{smax}}(\upsilon)_{i}=\exp(\upsilon_{i})/\sum_{j}\exp(\upsilon_{j})
.
For two non-negative functions
f(x)
and
g(x)
defined on
x\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, we write
f(x)\lesssim g(x)
if there exists two constants
c>0
such that
f(x)\leq c\cdot g(x)
, and write
f(x)\gtrsim g(x)
if there exists two constants
c>0
such that
f(x)\geq c\cdot g(x)
. We write
f(x)\asymp g(x)
or
f(x)=\Theta(g(x))
if
f(x)\lesssim g(x)
and
g(x)\lesssim f(x)
.

Complex and Group-Theoretic Notation.

Let
\Re(\cdot)
and
\Im(\cdot)
denote the real and imaginary parts.
For any complex number
z=|z|\cdot e^{i\phi}\in\mathbb{C}
, we define
\arg(z)=\phi\in[0,2\pi)
.
For a matrix
A\in\mathbb{C}^{d\times d}
, denote by
A^{*}
the Hermitian adjoint.
We use
\mathbb{D}
to denote the unit circle in the complex plane
\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|z|=1\}
.
The notation
C_{1}\propto_{+}C_{2}
denotes that
C_{1}
is a real-proportional to
C_{2}
, that is,
C_{1}=\lambda\cdot C_{2}
for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.
We use
\simeq
to denote group isomorphism and
\rtimes
to denote the semi-direct product, where
N\rtimes H
is formed by a normal subgroup
N
and a subgroup
H
.

2
Learning Group Composition with Neural Network

In this section, we formalize the group composition task, describe the network architecture, and specify the training procedure.

Group Composition Task.

Let
(G,\star)
denote a
group
,
which is defined as a set
G
equipped with a binary operation
\star:G\times G\to G
satisfying the following three properties:

\triangleright

Associativity.

(a\star b)\star c=a\star(b\star c)
for all
a,b,c\in G
.

\triangleright

Identity.
There exists an identity element
{\mathrm{Id}}\in G
such that
a\star{\mathrm{Id}}={\mathrm{Id}}\star a=a
for all
a\in G
.

\triangleright

Inversion.
There exists an inverse
a^{-1}\in G
for every
a\in G
such that
a\star a^{-1}=a^{-1}\star a={\mathrm{Id}}
.

Moreover, the group is called
Abelian
if it additionally satisfies
commutativity
, i.e.,
a\star b=b\star a
. In the task of learning group composition, we want to predict
g_{1}\star g_{2}
for any
(g_{1},g_{2})\in G\times G
.
A canonical example is modular addition on
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, which is an Abelian group with
g_{1}\star g_{2}=(g_{1}+g_{2})\bmod p
. This task has been extensively studied in the literature
(
27
;
13
, e.g.,)
.

Network Architecture.

We consider a standard two-layer fully connected neural network
f(\cdot,\cdot;\Theta):\mathbb{R}^{|G|}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{|G|}
parametrized by
\Theta=\{(a_{m},\xi_{m},\theta_{m}^{1},\theta_{m}^{2})\}_{m\in[M]}
of the following form:

\displaystyle f_{\sf NN}(g_{1},g_{2};\Theta)=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}a_{m}\cdot\xi_{m}\cdot\sigma\big(\langle\theta_{m}^{1},e_{g_{1}}\rangle+\langle\theta_{m}^{2},e_{g_{2}}\rangle\big)\in\mathbb{R}^{|G|}.

(2.1)

The network parameters comprise: (i) two positional input embeddings
\theta_{m}^{\tau}\in\mathbb{R}^{|G|}
(
\tau\in\{1,2\}
), one for each operand, (ii) output embeddings
\xi_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{|G|}
mapping activations to logits over
G
, and (iii) neuron scaling factors
a_{m}>0
. The use of two separate input embeddings
\theta_{m}^{1},\theta_{m}^{2}
is necessitated by non-Abelian groups: since the group operation is non-commutative, i.e.,
g_{1}\star g_{2}\neq g_{2}\star g_{1}
, the network must distinguish between the left and right operands.
Besides,
\sigma(\cdot)
denotes the activation function, and we choose
\sigma(x)=x^{2}
throughout this paper.

Since the input is a one-hot vector, each weight vector
\nu\in\{\theta_{m}^{1},\theta_{m}^{2},\xi_{m}\}
admits two equivalent views: as a vector in
\mathbb{R}^{|G|}
with entries indexed by group elements, or as a function
\nu:G\to\mathbb{R}
with
\nu(g)=\langle\nu,e_{g}\rangle
.
We freely switch between these two perspectives throughout the paper.
Moreover,
f_{\sf NN}
in (
2.1
) is the
logit
, and the prediction probability is obtained by passing
f_{\sf NN}
through the softmax function.
We adopt mean-field parameterization
a_{m}/M=\Theta(1/M)
in accordance with
25
;
11
;
1
.

Remark 2.1
.

We make two remarks on the architecture.
First, prior work
(
13
)
has shown that the learned features are robust across activation choices (e.g., quadratic, ReLU), with the quadratic component being essential.
We adopt
\sigma(x)=x^{2}
because it is more amenable to analysis, enabling a clean spectral decomposition.
Second, the factorization of neuron weights into a scalar
a_{m}
and unit-norm directions
(\theta_{m}^{1},\theta_{m}^{2},\xi_{m})
does not change the expressivity of the network, but it decouples the training into two stages.
In the feature-learning stage, training primarily changes the directions, thereby learning the spectral features used by each neuron. In the margin-maximization stage, the learned directions are fixed and the scalars
a_{m}
are optimized, thereby refining the logits by increasing the margin of the correct class.

Training Data and Loss Function.

To gain a clean understanding of the learned representations and the mechanism by which the network solves group composition, we focus on full-data training over the complete composition table: the network observes every pair
(g_{1},g_{2})\in G\times G
with label
g_{1}\star g_{2}
.
We train the network by minimizing the cross-entropy (CE) loss over this complete dataset:

\displaystyle{\mathscr{R}}(\Theta)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{g_{1},g_{2}\in G}\log\left(\frac{\exp(f_{\sf NN}(g_{1},g_{2};\Theta)_{g_{1}\star g_{2}})}{\sum_{j\in G}\exp(f_{\sf NN}(g_{1},g_{2};\Theta)_{j})}\right)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{g_{1},g_{2}\in G}f_{\sf NN}(g_{1},g_{2};\Theta)_{g_{1}\star g_{2}}+\sum_{g_{1},g_{2}\in G}\log\bigg(\sum_{j\in G}\exp\big(f_{\sf NN}(g_{1},g_{2};\Theta)_{j}\big)\bigg).

(2.2)

The decomposition in the second line separates the loss into two parts: the first term maximizes logits at the correct label
g_{1}\star g_{2}
, while the log-partition function penalizes large logits across all classes. Here,
f_{\sf NN}(g_{1},g_{2};\Theta)_{j}
denotes the
j
-th entry of the logit vector in (
2.1
) for all
j\in G
.

Training Algorithm.

We adopt a two-stage training procedure that separates feature learning from scale optimization: in Stage I, the directional parameters
(\theta_{m}^{1},\theta_{m}^{2},\xi_{m})
are constrained to the unit sphere and trained via projected gradient flow with the scaling factors
a_{m}
held fixed; in Stage II, the learned directions are frozen and only
a_{m}
is optimized.
The model is initialized as follows:

a_{m}=a>0,\qquad(\theta_{m}^{1}(0),\theta_{m}^{2}(0),\xi_{m}(0))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\rm i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}{\rm Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{|G|-1})^{\otimes 3},\qquad\forall m\in[M].

Here, we fix
a=\Theta(1)
in Stage I as a sufficiently small constant that controls the initial output scale.
The small choice of
a
places the network in a “small-logit regime” where the softmax output is approximately uniform, which simplifies the analysis of early-phase dynamics.
With
a_{m}
fixed, we train only
(\theta_{m}^{1},\theta_{m}^{2},\xi_{m})
via projected gradient flow on the unit sphere:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\theta_{m}^{\tau}=-(I-\theta_{m}^{\tau}{\theta_{m}^{\tau}}^{\top})\nabla_{\theta_{m}^{\tau}}{\mathscr{R}}(\Theta),\qquad\partial_{t}\xi_{m}=-(I-\xi_{m}\xi_{m}^{\top})\nabla_{\xi_{m}}{\mathscr{R}}(\Theta),

(2.3)

for all
m\in[M]
and
\tau\in\{1,2\}
. Here, the projection operator
\mathbf{P}_{\nu}^{\perp}=I-\nu\nu^{\top}
projects the Euclidean gradient onto the tangent space of the sphere, ensuring that parameters remain on the sphere.
In Stage II, we freeze
(\theta_{m}^{1},\theta_{m}^{2},\xi_{m})
at their Stage I values and optimize only the scaling factors via

\partial_{t}a_{m}=-\nabla_{a_{m}}{\mathscr{R}}(\Theta),

sharpening the softmax toward the correct prediction.

3
Warmup: Learning Generalized Modular Addition

The study of simple arithmetic tasks such as modular addition has emerged as a cornerstone of mechanistic interpretability
(
28
, e.g.,)
.
While recent efforts have focused extensively on reverse-engineering and theoretical interpretation of how transformers and MLPs solve
x+y\bmod p
with
p
being a prime or odd number
(
27
;
38
;
13
, e.g.,)
, these studies remain largely restricted to the analysis of single cyclic groups.
In this section, we generalize the modular addition task to arbitrary finite Abelian groups and revisit the mechanistic observations of
13
through the lens of group representation theory.
This reformulation provides a unified language for describing the learned features and serves as a conceptual warm-up for our subsequent analysis of general (non-Abelian) group learning in §
4
.

3.1
Generalized Modular Addition

By the Fundamental Theorem of Finite Abelian Groups
(
37
)
, every finite Abelian group
G
is isomorphic to a unique direct sum of cyclic groups
G\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{n_{1}}\oplus\dots\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{n_{d}}
.
This isomorphism reduces the abstract group operation to component-wise modular addition.
It thus suffices to study
G_{\mathcal{N}}=\mathbb{Z}_{n_{1}}\oplus\dots\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{n_{d}}
with
\mathcal{N}=(n_{1},\dots,n_{d})
, where each
n_{j}\geq 2
, equipped with the operation:

\displaystyle g\star h=\begin{pmatrix}(g_{1}+h_{1})\bmod n_{1}\\
\vdots\\
(g_{d}+h_{d})\bmod n_{d}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\forall g,h\in G_{\mathcal{N}}.

(3.1)

Understanding this
generalized modular addition
task thus provides a complete understanding of the Abelian setting, unifying several well-studied problems.
In particular, the standard modular addition
x+y\bmod n
corresponds to the cyclic case
d=1
, while the bitwise XOR task
(
5
;
12
, e.g.,)
is recovered by setting
n_{j}=2
for all
j\in[d]
.

Discrete Fourier Transform.

The product structure of
G_{\mathcal{N}}
induces a Discrete Fourier Transform (DFT) that decomposes functions on
G_{\mathcal{N}}
into harmonic components.
For each frequency tuple
k=(k_{1},\dots,k_{d})\in G_{\mathcal{N}}
, we define the one-dimensional representation
\rho_{k}
as:

\displaystyle\rho_{k}(g)=\prod_{j=1}^{d}\exp\left(\frac{2\pi{i}k_{j}}{n_{j}}\cdot g_{j}\right)\in\mathbb{C},\qquad\forall g=(g_{1},\dots,g_{d})\in G_{\mathcal{N}}.

(3.2)

For any function
\nu:G_{\mathcal{N}}\to\mathbb{C}
, the Fourier coefficient
\widehat{\nu}[\rho_{k}]
and the reconstruction formula are

\displaystyle\widehat{\nu}[\rho_{k}]=\frac{1}{|G_{\mathcal{N}}|}\sum_{h\in G_{\mathcal{N}}}\nu(h)\cdot\overline{\rho_{k}(h)}\in\mathbb{C},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\nu(g)=\sum_{k\in G_{\mathcal{N}}}\widehat{\nu}[\rho_{k}]\cdot\rho_{k}(g).

(3.3)

This decomposition enables a spectral analysis of the network’s parameters by expressing the weights in the group’s harmonic basis.
Please refer to Figure
3
for an illustration of the representations.

Conjugate Representations.

Since the network parameters are real-valued, their Fourier coefficients come in conjugate pairs.
For any
k\in G_{\mathcal{N}}
, we define its
conjugate

k^{\vee}
with
k_{j}^{\vee}=(n_{j}-k_{j})\bmod n_{j}
such that
\rho_{k^{\vee}}(g)=\overline{\rho_{k}(g)}
.
A representation
\rho_{k}
is called
self-conjugate
if
k=k^{\vee}
, which occurs when
k_{j}\in\{0,n_{j}/2\}
for every
j
.
When all
n_{j}
are odd, no non-trivial self-conjugate representation exists.For any real-valued function
\nu
, the Fourier coefficients satisfy
\widehat{\nu}[\rho_{k^{\vee}}]=\overline{\widehat{\nu}[\rho_{k}]}
(see Lemma
D.4
).
In this section, we focus on the non-self-conjugate case where all
n_{j}
are odd, which admits a cleaner theory.
The self-conjugate case requires a separate treatment and is deferred to §
F.2
.

3.2
Learned Patterns for Generalized Modular Addition

We now describe the empirical patterns that emerge when the two-layer network (
2.1
) is trained on all
|G|^{2}
input pairs of the generalized modular addition task (
3.1
) using the training procedure described in §
2
.
After Stage I training converges, we project each neuron’s parameters
(\theta_{m}^{1},\theta_{m}^{2},\xi_{m})
onto the Fourier basis
\{\rho_{k}\}_{k\in G_{\mathcal{N}}}
via (
3.3
) and examine the resulting spectral structure.
As a running example, we use
G_{\mathcal{N}}=\mathbb{Z}_{3}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{5}
, equivalently
\mathbb{Z}_{15}
with
1024
neurons.
The observations below mirror those reported by
13
for
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
,with the primary difference being that the scalar frequency
k\in\{1,\dots,p-1\}
is replaced by a frequency
tuple

k=(k_{1},\dots,k_{d})\in G_{\mathcal{N}}
.

(a)

Visualization of the Single-Frequency Structure.

(b)

Phase Alignment.

Figure 1
:
Empirical verification of Observations
3.2
and
3.2
.
(a)
DFT heatmaps of the learned parameter
\xi_{m}
for the top 15 neurons on
G=\mathbb{Z}_{3}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{5}
. Each row corresponds to a neuron and each column to a frequency
k
.
\theta_{m}^{1}
and
\theta_{m}^{2}
exhibit identical sparsity (see Figure
9
for full experimental results in §
F.2
).
(b)
Scatter plot comparing the sum of input-layer phases against the output-layer phase for all
m
.

During the gradient training, the network undergoes a
frequency concentration
process where the Fourier coefficients
\widehat{\nu}[\rho_{k}]
of each parameter vector
\nu\in\{\theta_{m}^{1},\theta_{m}^{2},\xi_{m}\}
gradually become sparse, with only a single non-trivial frequency and its conjugate surviving.
We denote by
\check{k}_{m}\in G_{\mathcal{N}}\backslash\{0\}
the surviving frequency tuple for neuron
m
, and write
\check{\rho}_{m}:=\rho_{\check{k}_{m}}
for the corresponding representation.

Observation 1 (Single Frequency).

For every neuron
m
, there exists a single non-trivial frequency tuple
\check{k}_{m}\in G_{\mathcal{N}}\backslash\{0\}
such that all other Fourier coefficients vanish:

\widehat{\nu}[\rho_{k}]=0\text{~~for all~~}\rho_{k}\notin\{\check{\rho}_{m},\check{\rho}_{m}^{\vee}\},

where
\nu
denotes any of the learned parameters and
\check{\rho}_{m}^{\vee}
is the conjugate representation.

As shown in Figure
1
, Observation
3.2
shows that each neuron selects a single non-trivial frequency
\check{k}_{m}
together with its conjugate
\check{k}_{m}^{\vee}
.
The real and imaginary parts in the DFT heatmap provide evidence of Hermitian symmetry: for each
\nu\in\{\theta_{m}^{1},\theta_{m}^{2},\xi_{m}\}
, the two surviving coefficients satisfy
\widehat{\nu}[\check{\rho}_{m}^{\vee}]=\overline{\widehat{\nu}[\check{\rho}_{m}]}
.
Next, we study how the phases of the surviving coefficients at the representative frequency
\check{k}_{m}
align across the input and output layers.

Observation 2 (Phase Alignment).

For a nonzero complex number
z=|z|\cdot e^{{i}\phi}
, let
\arg(z)=\phi\bmod 2\pi
denote its phase.
For every neuron
m
, consider the complex Fourier coefficients of the three parameter vectors
\theta_{m}^{1},\theta_{m}^{2},\xi_{m}
at the surviving frequency
\check{k}_{m}
.
Their phases satisfy:

\arg(\widehat{\xi}_{m}[\check{\rho}_{m}])=\big\{\arg(\widehat{\theta}_{m}^{1}[\check{\rho}_{m}])+\arg(\widehat{\theta}_{m}^{2}[\check{\rho}_{m}])\big\}\bmod 2\pi.

Figure
1
verifies this alignment: for each neuron, we plot the output phase against the sum of input phases, and the points concentrate tightly along the diagonal, confirming the additive relationship.
In the following, we denote the two input phases by

\phi_{m}^{\tau}:=\arg(\widehat{\theta}_{m}^{\tau}[\check{\rho}_{m}])
for
\tau\in\{1,2\}
.
The output phase is then determined by Observation
3.2
.

While Observations
3.2
and
3.2
characterize individual spectral support and coupling, they do not explain how the neuron
ensemble
achieves the correct prediction.
This requires a third observation regarding the
distribution
of neurons.
Following Observations
3.2
and
3.2
, each neuron
m
is characterized by its frequency
\check{k}_{m}\in G_{\mathcal{N}}\backslash\{0\}
and input phases
\phi_{m}^{\tau}
’s, which in turn determine the phase of
\xi_{m}
.
Our third observation reveals that these frequencies and phases are
uniformly distributed
.

Observation 3 (Diversification).

The surviving frequencies
\{\check{k}_{m}\}
and phases
\{\phi_{m}\}
satisfy:

(i)

Frequency Uniformity
:
\check{k}_{m}
is uniformly distributed over all non-trivial frequencies in
G_{\mathcal{N}}\backslash\{0\}
.

(ii)

Phase Uniformity
:
\phi_{m}^{\tau}
’s are independently and uniformly distributed on
[0,2\pi)
across neuron
m
and position index
\tau
.

Figure
2
provides empirical verification: panel (a) plots the phases
\{\phi_{m}^{\tau}\}
on the unit circle and their joint distribution, illustrating uniform distribution and mutual independence. Panel (b) shows a histogram of the surviving frequencies
\{\check{k}_{m}\}
across neurons, confirming the uniform occupancy over all conjugate pairs.
We prove Observation
3.2
rigorously as part of Theorem
5.1
in §
5
.

(a)

Distribution of phases
\{\phi_{m}^{\tau}\}
on the unit circle and their joint distribution.

(b)

Frequency Distribution.

Figure 2
:
Empirical verification of Observation
3.2
.
(a)
Polar plots showing the distribution of phases
\{\phi_{m}^{\tau}\}
on the unit circle (left/middle) and their joint distribution (right), demonstrating uniform coverage and mutual independence.
(b)
Frequency histogram of surviving frequencies
\check{k}_{m}
’s across neurons, confirming that each non-trivial conjugate pair is represented with nearly equal frequency.

Implications for the Learned Predictor.

Combining the three observations reveals the network’s computational mechanism.
Observations
3.2
and
3.2
together determine the form of each neuron’s contribution to the logit: neuron
m
computes an estimator that depends on the inputs
g_{1},g_{2}
only through the representation
\check{\rho}_{m}
evaluated at
g_{1},g_{2}
, and output
j
.
Observation
3.2
then ensures that all non-trivial representations contribute equally to the ensemble, so that the network’s overall predictor takes a closed-form expression.
As shown by
13
for
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and generalized in our Lemma
C.4
for
arbitrary representation
beyond product form in (
3.2
), the ensemble logit under full diversification approximately produces the following
indicator function
:

f(g_{1},g_{2})_{j}\;\propto\;2\cdot\ind(j=g_{1}\star g_{2})+\ind(j=g_{1}^{2})+\ind(j=g_{2}^{2})+\mathsf{const},

which peaks at the correct answer
j=g_{1}\star g_{2}
and exhibits secondary “ghost” peaks at the squaring elements
g_{1}^{2}
and
g_{2}^{2}
.
Here,
\mathsf{const}
is ignorable due to the softmax operation.

4
Main Results for General Group Learning

This section analyzes the spectral patterns learned by neural networks on general finite groups.
After providing background on group harmonic analysis (see §
4.1
), we present our main theoretical results: each neuron converges to a single irreducible representation with rank-1 rotational alignment (see §
4.2
), while scaling factors
a_{m}
in (
2.1
) grow to ensure perfect accuracy (see §
4.4
).

4.1
Background: Harmonic Analysis on Finite Group

Group Representation.

We provide a brief introduction to irreducible representations of finite groups. See
31
for a comprehensive treatment.

Definition 4.1

(Irreducible Representation)
.

Let
G
be a finite group.
An irreducible representation of
G
is a homomorphism
\rho:(G,\star)\mapsto({\rm GL}(V_{\rho}),\cdot)
where
V_{\rho}
is a finite-dimensional vector space over
\mathbb{C}
and
{\rm GL}(V_{\rho})
denotes the group of invertible linear maps on
V_{\rho}
, satisfying

\rho(g_{1}\star g_{2})=\rho(g_{1})\cdot\rho(g_{2}),\qquad\forall g_{1},g_{2}\in G.

Let
{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)
denote a complete set of irreducible unitary representations of
G
. For each
\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)
,
\rho:G\to{\rm GL}(V_{\rho})
is a homomorphism and
\rho(g)^{*}\rho(g)=I_{d_{\rho}}
for all
g\in G
.
For each
\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)
, we fix an orthonormal basis of
V_{\rho}
, identifying
\rho(g)
with its matrix representation in
\mathbb{C}^{d_{\rho}\times d_{\rho}}
where
d_{\rho}=\dim V_{\rho}
satisfying
\sum_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)}d_{\rho}^{2}=|G|
.
The unitary dual
{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)
satisfies the following properties:

(i)

There exists a one-dimensional representation

\rho_{\mathsf{triv}}\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)
defined by
\rho_{\mathsf{triv}}(g)=1
for all
g\in G
.

(ii)

For any
\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)
, there exists a dual representation
\rho^{\vee}\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)
defined by
\rho^{\vee}(g)=\rho(g^{-1})^{\top}
.

(iii)

Let
\rho_{ij}(g)\in\mathbb{C}
denote the
(i,j)
-th entry of the matrix
\rho(g)\in\mathbb{C}^{d_{\rho}\times d_{\rho}}
given
g\in G
.
Then, the collection

\big\{\sqrt{d_{\rho}}\,\rho_{ij}(\cdot)\in\mathbb{C}^{|G|}:\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G),\;i,j=1,\dots,d_{\rho}\big\},

forms an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(G)
with respect to the inner product

\langle f,h\rangle_{L^{2}(G)}=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{g\in G}f(g)\,\overline{h(g)}
.

Representation converts the abstract group operation
\star
into matrix multiplication: the property
\rho(g_{1}\star g_{2})=\rho(g_{1})\cdot\rho(g_{2})
means that
\rho
is a
structure-preserving map
from the group into the space of invertible matrices.
This “linearizes” the group, enabling the use of linear algebraic tools.
While irreducible representations for Abelian groups are one-dimensional, which recovers the scalar Fourier characters
\rho_{k}(g)\in\mathbb{C}
from §
3.2
, non-Abelian groups necessitate irreps with
d_{\rho}>1
, where the Fourier coefficients become
d_{\rho}
-by-
d_{\rho}
complex matrices.

(a)

Abelian Group: Cyclic Group
\mathbb{Z}_{12}
.

(b)

Non-abelian Group: Alternating Group
A_{4}
.

Figure 3
:
Visual introduction to group structure and spectral representations. In each panel, the Cayley graph (left) illustrates the group’s algebraic structure, where nodes represent unique group elements and edges denote the action of specific generators. The spectral basis heatmaps (right) visualize the irreducible representations. While
\mathbb{Z}_{12}
is characterized by twelve 1D irreps,
A_{4}
exhibits a more complex spectral structure, including a 3D irrep. The heatmaps clearly reveal the underlying symmetry and conjugate relationships inherent within the irrep decomposition.

Discrete Fourier Transform.

The orthogonality of irreducible representations, i.e., property (iii) in Definition
4.1
, constitutes the foundation of harmonic analysis on finite groups. It allows us to decompose any function
\nu:G\mapsto\mathbb{R}
into its spectral components via DFT.
Recall from §
2
that each weight vector
\nu\in\mathbb{R}^{|G|}
is equivalently viewed as a function
\nu:G\to\mathbb{R}
, placing it in
L^{2}(G)
.
The Fourier coefficients are defined as:

\displaystyle\widehat{\nu}[\rho]=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{g\in G}\nu(g)\rho(g^{-1})\in\mathbb{C}^{d_{\rho}\times d_{\rho}},\qquad\forall\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G).

According to the Fourier inversion theorem
(
37
, see, e.g.,)
, the original function
\nu
can be exactly reconstructed from these coefficients via:

\displaystyle\nu(g)=\sum_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)}d_{\rho}\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\big(\widehat{\nu}[\rho]\rho(g)\big)=\sum_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)}\sum_{i,j=1}^{d_{\rho}}d_{\rho}\cdot(\widehat{\nu}[\rho])_{ij}\cdot\rho_{ji}(g)\in\mathbb{R},\qquad\forall g\in G.

This is the direct generalization of the DFT in (
3.3
) for the Abelian group.
While the Abelian Fourier transform decomposes a function into
|G|
scalar components, the general group DFT decomposes it into blocks of size
d_{\rho}^{2}
, with one block per irreducible representation.

4.2
Stage I: Group Representation Learning

Recall from §
2
that in Stage I, the scaling factors
a_{m}
are held fixed at their initialization value
a>0
, and only the directional parameters
(\theta_{m}^{1},\theta_{m}^{2},\xi_{m})
evolve under projected gradient flow on the unit sphere (
2.3
).
In the sequel, we first approximate the CE loss in the small-logit regime, then state our main convergence result (see Theorem
4.3
), which shows that gradient flow drives each neuron to a single irreducible representation with rank-1 rotational alignment.

Risk Approximation.

Since the scaling factor
a
is chosen to be sufficiently small (see §
2
), the network logits
f_{\sf NN}(g_{1},g_{2};\Theta)
remain close to zero during early training.
In this small-logit regime,
we apply the
Taylor expansion
\log(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\exp(s_{j}))\approx\log n+n^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{n}s_{j}
to
the cross-entropy loss (
2.2
), which yields an approximate loss:

\displaystyle{\mathscr{R}}_{\sf ap}(\Theta)=-\sum_{g_{1},g_{2}\in G}f_{\sf NN}(g_{1},g_{2};\Theta)_{g_{1}\star g_{2}}+\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{g_{1},g_{2}\in G}\sum_{j\in G}f_{\sf NN}(g_{1},g_{2};\Theta)_{j}+|G|^{2}\log|G|.

(4.1)

Let
\{\theta_{m}^{1,{\sf ap}},\theta_{m}^{2,{\sf ap}},\xi_{m}^{\sf ap}\}_{m=1}^{M}
denote the solution to the gradient flow ODE in (
2.3
) with respect to
{\mathscr{R}}_{\sf ap}(\Theta)
.
The following proposition shows that the trajectories induced by the approximate loss
{\mathscr{R}}_{\sf ap}
and the original risk
{\mathscr{R}}
remain close throughout training over any finite time horizon.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
(\theta_{m}^{1}(t),\theta_{m}^{2}(t),\xi_{m}(t))
be the solution of the gradient flow ODE in (
2.3
) associated with the risk
{\mathscr{R}}
in (
2.2
).
Similarly, let
(\theta_{m}^{1,\sf ap}(t),\theta_{m}^{2,\sf ap}(t),\xi_{m}^{\sf ap}(t))
be the solution of the gradient flow ODE in (
2.3
) associated with the approximate risk
{\mathscr{R}}_{\sf ap}
in (
4.1
).
Assuming identical initialization
\theta_{m}^{\tau}(0)=\theta_{m}^{\tau,\sf ap}(0)
and
\xi_{m}(0)=\xi_{m}^{\sf ap}(0)
, then for any fixed time
T\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, the following bound holds for all time
t\in[0,T]

\displaystyle\max_{m\in[M]}\max\big\{\|\theta_{m}^{1}(t)-\theta_{m}^{1,\sf ap}(t)\|_{2}^{2},\|\theta_{m}^{2}(t)-\theta_{m}^{2,\sf ap}(t)\|_{2}^{2},\|\xi_{m}(t)-\xi_{m}^{\sf ap}(t)\|_{2}^{2}\}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq a\cdot\big\{\exp\big(\Theta(a|G|^{1/2}M^{-1})\cdot t\big)-1\big\}.

The proof of Proposition
4.2
is deferred to §
A.2
.
This result ensures that the trajectories induced by the approximate risk
\mathscr{R}_{\mathsf{ap}}
remain uniformly close to those of the original risk
\mathscr{R}
within a finite time horizon
T
, given that the initialization scale
a
is sufficiently small.
To interpret this estimate, write
x=\Theta(a|G|^{1/2}M^{-1})\cdot t
. When
x\ll 1
, the Taylor expansion
\exp(x)-1=x+O(x^{2})
gives

a\{\exp(x)-1\}=O(a^{2}|G|^{1/2}M^{-1}\cdot t).

Thus, in this early-time regime, the discrepancy between the exact and approximate gradient-flow trajectories grows at most linearly in time, with slope proportional to
a^{2}|G|^{1/2}/M
. Consequently, to keep the squared trajectory discrepancy below a target level
\varepsilon>0
, the linearized estimate permits time horizons of order
t=O(M|G|^{-1/2}a^{-2}\varepsilon)
, provided the corresponding exponent remains small.

Global Convergence of the Spectral Patterns.

In view of Proposition
4.2
, we henceforth analyze the gradient flow under
{\mathscr{R}}_{\sf ap}
and use
(\theta_{m}^{1},\theta_{m}^{2},\xi_{m})
to replace
(\theta_{m}^{1,{\sf ap}},\theta_{m}^{2,{\sf ap}},\xi_{m}^{\sf ap})
for brevity.
Moreover, we decompose each parameter into its spectral components via the Group DFT:

\theta_{m}^{\tau}(g)=\sum_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)}d_{\rho}\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\big(\widehat{\theta_{m}^{\tau}}[\rho]\rho(g)\big),\qquad\xi_{m}(g)=\sum_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)}d_{\rho}\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\big(\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]\rho(g)\big).

We define the orbit of
\rho
as
{\rm Orb}(\rho)=\{\rho,\rho^{\vee}\}
, which reduces to a singleton if
\rho
is self-dual.

The following theorem establishes that, under random initialization, gradient flow almost surely drives parameters to develop structural spectral patterns analogous to the Abelian case in §
3.2
.

Theorem 4.3
.

Consider the gradient flow (
2.3
) under the approximate risk
{\mathscr{R}}_{\sf ap}
in (
4.1
).
For every neuron
m
, there exists a non-trivial irreducible representation
\check{\rho}_{m}\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}
such that as
t\to\infty
, almost surely, the following properties hold:

(i)

(Single Representation)
. For all representation
\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)\setminus{\rm Orb}(\check{\rho}_{m})
, the Fourier coefficients vanish such that
\widehat{\nu}[\rho]\to 0_{d_{\rho}\times d_{\rho}}
for any parameter
\nu\in\{\xi_{m},\theta_{m}^{1},\theta_{m}^{2}\}
. Then, the parameters take the form:

\nu(g)=d_{\check{\rho}_{m}}\cdot|{\rm Orb}(\check{\rho}_{m})|\cdot\Re\big(\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(\widehat{\nu}[\check{\rho}_{m}]\check{\rho}_{m}(g))\big),\qquad\forall\nu\in\{\theta_{m}^{1},\theta_{m}^{2},\xi_{m}\}.

(ii)

(Rank-one Rotational Alignment)
. For active representations
\rho\in{\rm Orb}(\check{\rho}_{m})
, the Fourier coefficients of the parameters are of rank one, satisfying

\mathrm{rank}(\widehat{\theta}_{m}^{1}[\rho])=\mathrm{rank}(\widehat{\theta}_{m}^{2}[\rho])=\mathrm{rank}(\widehat{\xi}_{m}[\rho])=1.

Furthermore, they exhibit mutual alignment via the following proportionality relations:

\displaystyle\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]\propto_{+}\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho]\,\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho],\qquad\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho]\propto_{+}\big(\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho]\big)^{*}\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho],\qquad\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho]\propto_{+}\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]\,\big(\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho]\big)^{*}.

(4.2)

Here, “almost surely” is with respect to the random Stage I initialization
(\theta_{m}^{1}(0),\theta_{m}^{2}(0),\xi_{m}(0))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\rm i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}{\rm Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{|G|-1})^{\otimes 3}
.
After initialization, the gradient flow is deterministic.
We now discuss the two parts of Theorem
4.3
and explain their meaning.

\bullet

Discussion of (i): Single Representation.

Part (i) asserts that each neuron
m
selects exactly one non-trivial irreducible representation
\check{\rho}_{m}
and its dual
\check{\rho}_{m}^{\vee}
, and all other spectral components vanish.
In other words, while the parameters could theoretically span all
|{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)|
representations under random initialization, gradient training drives them to collapse onto a single
{\rm Orb}(\check{\rho}_{m})
.
This extends the single frequency pattern from §
3.2
of the Abelian group: each neuron learns to encode a specific group representation rather than a generic mixture (see Figure
5
in §
4.5
).

\bullet

Discussion of (ii): Rank-one Rotational Alignment.
Part (ii) reveals two structures within the Fourier coefficients of surviving
\check{\rho}_{m}
.
First, despite having
d_{\check{\rho}_{m}}
available degree of freedom,
\widehat{\nu}[\check{\rho}_{m}]\in\mathbb{C}^{d_{\check{\rho}_{m}}\times d_{\check{\rho}_{m}}}
are all rank-one. Second, these matrices are mutually aligned according to the (positive) proportionality relations in (
4.2
).
In the Abelian case, Fourier coefficients are scalars, and the alignment reduces to a single phase relation

\arg(\widehat{\xi}_{m})=\arg(\widehat{\theta}^{1}_{m})+\arg(\widehat{\theta}^{2}_{m})
.
Because scalar multiplication is commutative, the remaining relations in (
4.2
) are satisfied automatically.
In the non-Abelian case, however, matrix multiplication is non-commutative.
Therefore, these three relations are no longer equivalent and must be characterized separately.
Please refer to Figure
6
and
6
for experimental verification.

4.3
Proof Sketch for the Emergence of Spectral Patterns

For real-valued parameters
\nu\in\{\theta_{m}^{1},\theta_{m}^{2},\xi_{m}\}
, the projected gradient flow in(
2.3
) preserves the
L^{2}(G)
-norm, restricting the optimization trajectory to the product of spheres
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{S}^{3}
, where
\mathbb{S}
is a sphere in
L^{2}(G)
.
Lifting the dynamics to the Fourier domain via the group DFT reveals that the projected gradient flow of (
4.1
) coincides with the Riemannian gradient ascent of the energy functional (see Lemma
B.3
):

\displaystyle\Omega_{m}=\sum_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}}d_{\rho}\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\big((\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho])^{*}\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho]\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho]\big)\in\mathbb{R},

(4.3)

\displaystyle\text{subject to~}|G|\cdot\sum_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)}d_{\rho}\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}((\widehat{\nu}[\rho])^{*}\widehat{\nu}[\rho])=1\text{~for all~}\nu\in\{\theta_{m}^{1},\theta_{m}^{2},\xi_{m}\}.

This lifting can be viewed as a Riemannian mirror flow, with the group DFT acting as the mirror map between the parameter and Fourier spaces.
We further remark that a broader equivalence can be established via fourier analysis: the approximate risk
{\mathscr{R}}_{\sf ap}
is equivalent to the negative energy
-\Omega_{m}
up to an additive constant and a multiplicative factor, i.e.,
{\mathscr{R}}_{\sf ap}(\Theta)=-{2a|G|^{2}}/{M}\cdot\Omega_{m}+\text{const}
.
However, as we are only interested in the gradient flow dynamics constrained on
\mathcal{M}
, the Riemannian gradient ascent equivalence is sufficient for our analysis.
Without loss of generality, dropping the time-scaling factor
2a|G|^{2}/M
, we can analyze the dynamics entirely on the spectral manifold via:

\partial_{t}\widehat{\Theta}_{m}={\rm grad}_{\mathcal{M}}\Omega(\widehat{\Theta}_{m}),\qquad\widehat{\Theta}_{m}=(\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho],\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho],\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho])_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)}\in{\mathcal{M}}.

Here,
\text{grad}_{\mathcal{M}}\Omega_{m}
denotes the Riemannian gradient on
{\mathcal{M}}
, defined as the orthogonal projection of the Fréchet derivative
\mathrm{D}\Omega_{m}
onto the tangent space.
The complete proof of Theorem
4.3
is provided in §
B.2
, and we provide a proof sketch below.

Step 1: Spectral Dynamics and Equilibrium (§
B.2.1
).

Because the spectral dynamics are Riemannian gradient ascent of the energy
\Omega_{m}
in (
4.3
), Proposition
B.1
gives the following evolution equation for each non-trivial Fourier block, with one coupling term from the other two layers and one projection term enforcing the sphere constraint:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho]

\displaystyle=\frac{2a|G|}{M}\cdot\underbrace{(\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho])^{*}\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]}_{\displaystyle\text{\small driving term}}-\frac{2a|G|^{2}}{M}\cdot\underbrace{\Omega_{m}\cdot\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho]}_{\displaystyle\text{\small projection term}},\qquad\forall\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}.

The evolution equations for
\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho]
and
\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]
have the same structure, obtained by cyclically permuting the three layers.
The
driving term
couples the per-layer updates via the product of the other two layers’ fourier coefficients, while the
projection term
is proportional to the total energy.
Define the set of critical points for this dynamical system as

{\rm Crit}(\Omega)=\{\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{\dagger}\in{\mathcal{M}}:{\rm grad}_{\mathcal{M}}\Omega(\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{\dagger})=\mathbf{0}\}
.
We write
\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}:=\Omega_{m}(\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{\dagger})
for the energy value at the equilibrium.
For the equilibrium points with
\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}>0
, solving the equilibrium equations already gives the alignment relations specified in (
4.2
). In the following, we discuss broader critical-point classes and show that, under generic initialization, the flow avoids all classes except the positive-energy rank-one equilibria.

Step 2: Critical Point Classification
(§
B.2.2
).

We classify the critical points into five cases according to the sign of the equilibrium energy and the representation support of
\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{\dagger}
.
The appendix proof then rules out the first four cases by two mechanisms: a null-set initialization condition for Cases 1–2, and a strict-saddle argument for Cases 3–4.

\triangleright

Cases 1 & 2: Negative Energy
\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}<0
and Degenerate Zero-Energy
\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}=0
.

For the negative-energy equilibria and the zero-energy equilibria with trivial representation, we can show that they are supported only for measure-zero initializations.
Lemma
B.5
first shows that the pairwise norm gaps for any irreps
\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)
between the three Fourier components evolve only by a common scalar factor:

\Delta_{m}[\rho](t)=\Delta_{m}[\rho](0)\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{4a|G|^{2}}{M}\cdot\int_{0}^{t}\Omega_{m}(s)\,{\rm d}s\Big),\qquad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}.

If
\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}<0
, starting from nonzero gaps
\Delta^{\tau}_{m}[\rho](0)\neq 0
, if the dynamics converge to negative-energy equilibria, the whole term would unavoidably grow to infinity.
By the Parseval’s identity, this would imply that the
L^{2}(G)
-norm of the parameters diverges, which is contradicting the boundedness of the manifold
\mathcal{M}
.
Hence, if the trajectory converges to one of these equilibria, the relevant gaps must already vanish at initialization.
Lemmas
B.6
and
B.7
formalize this implication: convergence to these equilibria in requires
\widehat{\Theta}_{m}(0)
to lie in the exceptional set

{\mathcal{M}}_{\sf init}=\big\{\widehat{\Theta}_{m}\in{\mathcal{M}}:\|\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho]\|_{\rm F}^{2}=\|\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho]\|_{\rm F}^{2}=\|\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]\|_{\rm F}^{2},\quad\forall\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)\big\}.

Since
{\mathcal{M}}_{\sf init}
is a proper submanifold of
{\mathcal{M}}
and therefore has Riemannian volume zero.

\triangleright

Cases 3 & 4: Higher-Rank
\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}>0
and Non-Degenerate
\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}=0
.

These cases are excluded by the Hessian analysis.
For positive-energy equilibria, Lemma
B.9
shows that the equilibrium equations force the three Fourier coefficients at each representation
\rho
to share the same rank
r_{\rho}
and an aligned partial-isometry factorization.
If
\sum_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}}r_{\rho}\geq 2
, then Lemma
B.10
constructs an explicit tangent direction
\Xi_{m}
on the tangent space and acting as a positive-eigenvalue direction of
\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{M}}\Omega(\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{\dagger})
, i.e.,
\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{M}}\Omega(\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{\dagger})[\Xi]\propto_{+}\Xi
where
\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{M}}\Omega(\cdot):T_{(\cdot)}{\mathcal{M}}\mapsto T_{(\cdot)}{\mathcal{M}}
denotes the Hessian operator.
For zero-energy equilibria with at least one active non-trivial representation, Lemma
B.8
shows that the equilibrium equations become triple-annihilation relations.
This gives a mutually orthogonal block structure and again yields a tangent direction with strictly positive Hessian eigenvalue.
Therefore, all critical points in Cases 3–4 are
strict saddles
2
2

2

In this paper, strict saddle points include local maximizers.
in the set defined by

{\rm Sad}(\Omega)=\{\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{\dagger}\in{\rm Crit}(\Omega):\lambda(\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{M}}\Omega(\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{\dagger}))\cap\mathbb{C}_{+}\neq\emptyset\},

\triangleright

Case 5: Rank-One
\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}>0
.

With Cases 1-4 excluded, the only remaining equilibria have positive energy and a single active non-trivial irrep
\check{\rho}_{m}
with
r_{\check{\rho}_{m}}=1
. These are precisely the single-representation, rank-one patterns with positive proportion characterized in Theorem
4.3
.

Step 3: Saddle Avoidance (§
B.2.3
).

We extend the saddle-avoidance results for first-order methods
(
17
)
to continuous Riemannian gradient flow by leveraging an analogous version of the center-stable manifold theorem in
(
32
)
. The main statement is formalized as follows.

Lemma 4.4

(Informal)
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}
be a compact Riemannian manifold, and let
{\mathcal{F}}\in C^{2}({\mathcal{M}})
. Consider the gradient flow with a random initialization:

\partial_{t}x(t)=\mathrm{grad}_{{\mathcal{M}}}{\mathcal{F}}(x(t)),\qquad x(0)=X_{0}\sim{\sf P}_{0},

where
{\sf P}_{0}
is absolutely
continuous with respect to the Riemannian volume measure.
For each
t\in\mathbb{R}
, let
\phi_{t}(x)
denote the value at time
t
of the solution starting from
x
.
Define the global stable set

W^{s}:=\{x\in{\mathcal{M}}:\exists\,p\in{\rm Sad}({\mathcal{F}}),~\phi_{t}(x)\to p\text{ \rm~as~ }t\to\infty\}.

Then
W^{s}
has zero Riemannian volume, and thus
\mathbb{P}(X_{0}\in W^{s})=0
.

The proof proceeds as follows.
For a strict saddle
p
, the tangent space can be decomposed as
T_{p}{\mathcal{M}}=E_{p}^{\sf sc}\oplus E_{p}^{\sf u}
, where
E_{p}^{\sf sc}
is the eigenspace corresponding to non-positive Hessian eigenvalues, and
E_{p}^{\sf u}
to strictly positive eigenvalues.
The center-stable manifold theorem (see Theorem
B.13
) then guarantees the existence of a local center-stable manifold
W_{\rm loc}^{\sf sc}(p)
and a neighborhood
U_{p}
such that

\displaystyle{\rm(i)}~\dim(W_{\rm loc}^{\sf sc}(p))=\dim(E_{p}^{\sf sc}),\qquad{\rm(ii)~}\phi_{t}(x)\in U_{p}\text{~for all~}t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}~\Rightarrow~x\in W^{\sf sc}_{\rm loc}(p).

(4.4)

For any saddle point
p\in{\rm Sad}({\mathcal{F}})
, by definition, we have
\dim(W_{\rm loc}^{\sf sc}(p))<\dim({\mathcal{M}})
, and hence has zero Riemannian volume.
Next, we extract a countable subcover
\{U_{p_{j}}\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
such that
{\rm Sad}({\mathcal{F}})\subseteq\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}U_{p_{i}}
.
Since any point
w\in W^{\sf s}
converges to a saddle in some
U_{p_{j}}
, its forward trajectory must eventually remain within
U_{p_{j}}
for all sufficiently large times.
By property (ii) in (
4.4
), there exists an integer
N\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0}
such that
\phi_{N}(w)\in W_{\rm loc}^{\sf sc}(p_{j})
.
Hence, we can get a countable cover of
W^{\sf s}
by

W^{\sf s}\subseteq\bigcup_{j=1}^{\infty}\bigcup_{N=0}^{\infty}\phi_{-N}(W^{\sf sc}_{\mathrm{loc}}(p_{j})),

where every piece is a smooth image of a volume zero set. Therefore, the global stable set of saddle points
W^{\sf s}
has Riemannian volume zero, which yields the desired result.

(a)

Manifold, Tangent Space and Flow.

(b)

Local Stable-Center Manifold.

Figure 4
:
Illustration of the geometric concepts used in the center-stable manifold theorem for the saddle-avoidance argument.

(a)
The Riemannian gradient flow evolves intrinsically on the manifold
\mathcal{M}
.

(b)
Near a strict saddle
p
, the tangent space decomposes as

T_{p}\mathcal{M}=E_{p}^{\sf sc}\oplus E_{p}^{\sf u}
, where
E_{p}^{\sf sc}
contains the non-expanding directions and
E_{p}^{\sf u}
contains the non-expanding directions.
The center-stable manifold theorem yields a local center-stable manifold

W^{\sf sc}_{\rm loc}(p)
inside a neighborhood
U_{p}
around
p
.

Step 4: All Critical Points in Cases 1-4 are Avoided Almost Surely (§
B.2.4
).

\triangleright

Cases 1 & 2.
Convergence to these equilibria occurs only if the initial state lies within
{\mathcal{M}}_{\sf init}
as established in Step 2. Since
{\mathcal{M}}_{\sf init}
is a set of zero Riemannian volume, these points are reached with probability zero under any absolutely continuous random initialization.

\triangleright

Cases 3 & 4.
By Theorem
4.4
and the analysis in Step 3, the Riemannian gradient flow almost surely escapes all strict saddle points. Consequently, the dynamics avoid these higher-rank and non-degenerate equilibria with probability one.

After excluding these measure-zero basins and unstable equilibria, the only remaining attractors are the positive-energy equilibria with a total effective rank of one in Case 5.

4.4
Stage II: Growth of Scaling Parameter

In Stage II, the directional parameters
(\theta_{m}^{1},\theta_{m}^{2},\xi_{m})
are frozen at their Stage I values and only the scaling factors
a_{m}
are optimized.
For theoretical tractability, we tie these factors
a_{m}
, reducing the Stage II dynamics to a single scalar ODE for
a(t)
. While this simplification ensures equal magnitude contributions for our analytical convenience, experiments demonstrate that the network converges successfully when scaling factors are optimized independently.
For a sufficiently wide network, we analyze the dynamics in the mean-field limit.
Under the tied constraint, the network output (
2.1
) becomes
f_{\sf NN}(g_{1},g_{2};\Theta)=a\cdot{f}_{\sf NN}^{\widehat{\mu}}(g_{1},g_{2})
, where the
mean-field predictor

{f}_{\sf NN}^{\widehat{\mu}}
is defined by

{f}_{\sf NN}^{\widehat{\mu}}(g_{1},g_{2}):=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\xi_{m}\cdot\sigma\big(\langle\theta_{m}^{1},e_{g_{1}}\rangle+\langle\theta_{m}^{2},e_{g_{2}}\rangle\big)=\int\xi\cdot\sigma\big(\langle\theta^{1},e_{g_{1}}\rangle+\langle\theta^{2},e_{g_{2}}\rangle\big)\,{\rm d}\widehat{\mu}(\theta^{1},\theta^{2},\xi),

with
\widehat{\mu}:=M^{-1}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\delta_{(\xi_{m},\theta_{m}^{1},\theta_{m}^{2})}
representing the empirical measure over the frozen directional parameters. In this stage, the functional direction of the predictor is fixed by the spectral structures learned in Stage I, leaving the scalar
a(t)
as the sole trainable parameter.

Sufficient Condition for Scale Growth.

While Stage I establishes the correct spectral structure, the loss remains large due to the small
a
.
To achieve zero loss, it suffices that the mean-field predictor
{f}_{\sf NN}^{\widehat{\mu}}
already assigns the highest logit to the correct group product for every input pair; growing
a
then sharpens the softmax toward the correct label.
Let
\mu
denote the limiting measure such that
\widehat{\mu}\to\mu
as
M\to\infty
.
We define the
perfect accuracy
condition with respect to
\mu
as

\displaystyle{f}_{\sf NN}^{\mu}(g_{1},g_{2})_{g_{1}\star g_{2}}>\max_{j\in G\backslash\{g_{1}\star g_{2}\}}{f}_{\sf NN}^{\mu}(g_{1},g_{2})_{j},\qquad\forall g_{1},g_{2}\in G.

(
\mu
-
PA
)

In words, (

μ

-PA
) requires that the mean-field predictor already aligns with the ground truth, i.e.,
\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{j\in G}{f}_{\sf NN}^{\mu}(g_{1},g_{2})_{j}=g_{1}\star g_{2}
for every input pair.
Since
G
is finite, the strict inequality over finitely many pairs automatically implies a positive logit margin.

We prove (

μ

-PA
) for Abelian groups in Theorem
5.1
, while experiments confirm it also holds for non-Abelian cases.
Under this condition, we establish the following convergence result.

Theorem 4.5
.

Suppose the condition (

μ

-PA
) holds and the neurons’ scales are tied such that
a_{j}=a
for all
j\in[M]
.
For any
\delta\in(0,1)
, if
M\gtrsim\log(|G|^{3}/\delta)
, then with probability at least
1-\delta
the following hold:

(i)

(Logarithmic Scale Growth). The shared scale satisfies
a(t)\gtrsim\log(1+|G|\cdot(|G|-1)\cdot t)
for
t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.

(ii)

(Loss Convergence). For any
\epsilon>0
, the cross-entropy loss satisfies
{\mathscr{R}}(\Theta(T))\leq\epsilon
provided the training time
T\gtrsim|G|/\epsilon\cdot(1+(|G|-1)^{-2})
.

The proof of Theorem
4.5
is deferred to §
B.3
.
Together, Theorems
4.3
and
4.5
establish a complete two-stage mechanism: Stage I learns the correct spectral structure, i.e., single representation, rank-1 alignment, and Stage II amplifies the scale
a(t)
logarithmically, sharpening the softmax and driving the cross-entropy loss to zero at rate
O(1/T)
.
The convergence rate in Theorem
4.5
depends on the logit margin implied by (

μ

-PA
), which is entirely determined by Stage I and absorbed into the
\gtrsim
notation.
We remark that this dynamics closely mimics the implicit bias of gradient flow training on separable data under exponential-type losses
(
34
, e.g.,)
.

Takeaway for General Group.

For any finite group
G
, training provably proceeds in two stages:

\triangleright

Stage I
(Theorem
4.3
): The gradient flow drives each neuron to encode a single irreps
\check{\rho}_{m}
with rank-1 Fourier coefficients and rotational alignment.
This generalizes the Abelian “single frequency + phase alignment” (Observations
3.2
and
3.2
) to general groups, while introducing rank-one compression as a novel feature unique to matrix-valued representations.

\triangleright

Stage II
(Theorem
4.5
): The scaling factor grows logarithmically, sharpening the softmax distribution and driving the CE loss to zero at an
O(1/T)
rate, provided the mean-field predictor satisfies the perfect accuracy condition in (

μ

-PA
).

4.5
Experimental Results

Data Generation.

Our simulations utilize the Frobenius group
C_{7}\rtimes C_{3}
, defined by

\langle x,y\mid x^{7}=y^{3}=1,\;yxy^{-1}=x^{2}\rangle.

Here
x
and
y
are generators, meaning that all elements of
G
can be obtained by multiplying powers of
x
and
y
.
Specifically, every element has a unique form
x^{i}y^{j}
.
The relation
yxy^{-1}=x^{2}
specifies the interaction between the two generators and makes the group non-abelian.
We choose this group because it provides a simple test case where the representation structure is richer than that of commonly used groups.

C_{7}\rtimes C_{3}
necessitates learning
multi-dimensional
irreducible representations unlike abelian groups, and features
non-self-conjugate
representations unlike symmetric groups.

Figure 5
:
Empirical verification of the spectral pattern (i) in Theorem
4.3
for Stage I. The heatmaps display the learned parameters for the top 20 neurons after applying the group DFT. Each row corresponds to one neuron. Along the horizontal axis, the coefficients are grouped by irreducible representations of the Frobenius group: the
1
-D representations
\rho_{\rm triv}
,
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{1}^{\vee}
each contribute a single column, while the
3
-dimensional representations
\rho_{2}
and
\rho_{2}^{\vee}
contribute
3\times 3
matrix blocks whose entries are indexed by
(i,j)
. The vertical separators mark the boundaries between these irrep blocks. Thus, a neuron that selects a single representation should exhibit two active conjugate blocks and near-zero values elsewhere, which is precisely the block-sparse pattern in the figure.

Accuracy Growth.

(b)

Rotational Alignment.

(c)

Rank-1 Compression.

:

μ

-PA
) and the spectral patterns (ii) and (iii) in Theorem
4.3
.
(a)
Accuracy curves across training, showing that the classifier reaches accuracy
1
and then remains there.
(b)
Evolution of the rotational alignment metric
{\sf dist}_{\rm al}
for the active Fourier blocks. The trajectories approach
1
and their variance reduces to
0
, which means these matrices become asymptotically proportional as predicted by (
4.2
).
(c)
Evolution of the low-rank metric
{\sf dist}_{\rm r1}
. The trajectories and their variances decay toward
0
, indicating that the second singular value vanishes relative to the first and the Fourier coefficient matrices become rank one.

Experimental Results for Stage I.

To quantify the rational alignment and rank-one structure predicted by Theorem
4.3
, we introduce two metrics to measure the alignment and spectral decay of matrices
C_{1},C_{2}\in\mathbb{C}^{d_{\rho}\times d_{\rho}}
as

{\sf dist}_{\rm al}(C_{1},C_{2})={|\langle{\rm vec}(C_{1}),{\rm vec}(C_{2})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}|}/({\|C_{1}\|_{\rm F}\cdot\|C_{2}\|_{\rm F}})
and
{\sf dist}_{\rm r1}(C_{1})={\sigma_{(2)}(C_{1})}/{\sigma_{(1)}(C_{1})},

where
{\rm vec}(\cdot)
flattens a matrix into a vector, and
\sigma_{(n)}(\cdot)
denotes the
n
-th largest singular value.
The alignment metric
{\sf dist}_{\rm al}
measures the cosine similarity between matrices, where
1
indicates the proportionality, i.e.,
C_{1}\propto_{+}C_{2}
, and
0
indicates the orthogonality.
Moreover,
{\sf dist}_{\rm r1}
captures spectral decay and a value near
0
indicates a perfectly rank-one structure.

The experimental results in Figures
5
and
6
corroborate the theoretical predictions established in Theorem
4.3
.
First, the structured sparsity observed in the Fourier-domain heatmaps (Figure
5
) directly validates the single-representation structure predicted by spectral pattern (i). In particular, Figure
5
shows that, for each of the top 20 neurons, the Fourier coefficients are concentrated in a single irreducible-representation block, while the remaining blocks stay nearly zero. This block-sparse pattern is exactly the evidence expected from pattern (i): each neuron selects one representation channel rather than spreading its mass across multiple irreps.
Furthermore, the training dynamics depicted in Figure
6
confirm the remaining theoretical claims.
Throughout training, the model successfully achieves perfect accuracy (see Figure
6
), which validates the perfect alignment condition (

μ

-PA
).
Moreover, the rotational alignment metric steadily converges to
1
(see Figure
6
), demonstrating the cross-layer synchronization dynamics.
Concurrently, the singular value ratio strictly decays toward
0
(see Figure
6
), providing clear evidence for the rank-one compression predicted by pattern (iii).
Together, these results verify that the practical optimization trajectory naturally collapses into the exact theoretical equilibrium.

.

, before undergoing a rapid drop toward

in Stage II.

). For a fair comparison, the gradient of the untied case is amplified by a factor of

to match the gradient accumulation across neurons inherent to the tied case.

Experimental Results for Stage II.

Figure
7
offers empirical validation for Theorem
4.5
. Consistent with the mechanism where phase alignment strictly precedes the final loss minimization, the cross-entropy loss initially plateaus during Stage I due to the small, frozen scaling factor.
Subsequently, the loss undergoes a rapid, dynamic drop toward
0
(see Figure
7
), substantiating the loss behavior claimed in part (ii) of the theorem.
Furthermore, Figures
7
and
7
track the evolution of the scaling factors.
Both the tied configuration, i.e., analyzed under the perfect alignment condition in (

μ

-PA
), and the untied configuration demonstrate the clear logarithmic growth over time predicted by part (i).
Together, these observations confirm that once the necessary structural alignment is achieved, the network naturally transitions into a scale-driven regime that drives the loss to zero.

5
Mechanism and Training Dynamics of Abelian Group

In this section, we specialize in Abelian groups and provide a complete picture of the converged model, its mechanistic interpretation, and its training dynamics.
Throughout this section, we adopt a shared input embedding
\theta^{1}_{m}=\theta_{m}^{2}=:\theta_{m}
, which is natural for the Abelian group since the group operation is commutative.
Under this convention, the Stage I projected gradient flow becomes

\partial_{t}\theta_{m}=-\frac{1}{2}(I-\theta_{m}{\theta_{m}}^{\top})\nabla_{\theta_{m}}{\mathscr{R}}(\Theta),\qquad\partial_{t}\xi_{m}=-(I-\xi_{m}\xi_{m}^{\top})\nabla_{\xi_{m}}{\mathscr{R}}(\Theta),

where the factor
1/2
compensates for the shared embedding, which would otherwise cause
\theta_{m}
to evolve twice as fast.
We further assume that
G
has no self-conjugate irreducible representations.

This section is organized as follows.
In §
5.1
, we prove the diversification property in Observation
3.2
by deriving the mean-field limit
\mu
of the trained network.
Moreover,
\mu
naturally satisfies (

μ

-PA
), which yields a closed-form
flawed indicator
that reveals the network’s computational mechanism.
In §
5.2
, we establish the explicit convergence rates for both phase alignment and representation competition, providing quantitative bounds on the emergence time of the spectral patterns.

5.1
Diversification, Perfect Accuracy, and Mechanics

We begin by proving Observation
3.2
: under uniform spherical initialization, the trained network’s mean-field limit exhibits full diversification over representations and phases.
Moreover, we show that the resulting predictor satisfies (

μ

-PA
) and admits a closed-form mechanistic interpretation.
Recall that
\mathbb{D}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|z|=1\}
denotes the unit circle in the complex plane.

Theorem 5.1
.

Consider an Abelian group
G
that possesses no self-conjugate representations.
Let the network parameters be initialized as
\theta_{m},\xi_{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\rm i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}{\rm Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{|G|-1})
.
Then the limiting measure
\mu
on
(\theta,\xi)
is defined as the push-forward of
\pi
under the mapping
{\mathscr{T}_{\sf idft}}:{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}\times\mathbb{D}\mapsto(\mathbb{S}^{|G|-1})^{\otimes 2}
:

\displaystyle\mu={\mathscr{T}_{\sf idft}}_{\#}\pi,\qquad{\mathscr{T}_{\sf idft}}:(\check{\rho},u)\mapsto\sqrt{2/|G|}\cdot\big(\Re(u\check{\rho}(\cdot)),\Re(u^{2}\check{\rho}(\cdot))\big).

(5.1)

Here,
\pi={\rm Unif}({\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1})\otimes{\rm Haar}(\mathbb{D})
is a product measure, and
{\rm Haar}(\mathbb{D})
denotes the Haar measure, i.e., uniform distribution, over the unit circle
\mathbb{D}
.
Furthermore,
\mu
satisfies the (

μ

-PA
).

The proof of Theorem
5.1
is deferred in §
C.2
.
Below, we interpret the theorem’s implications, describe the resulting majority-vote mechanism, and provide a sketch of the proof.

Anatomy of the Push-Forward Mapping.

The mapping
{\mathscr{T}_{\sf idft}}
converts a
spectral coordinate

(\check{\rho},u)\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}\times\mathbb{D}
into a pair of weight functions
(\theta_{m},\xi_{m})\in(\mathbb{S}^{|G|-1})^{\otimes 2}
.
Under this parametrization, each trained neuron is defined by
(i) A
learned representation

\check{\rho}\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}
, the single non-trivial irrep that survives the representation competition, and (ii) An
absolute phase

u\in\mathbb{D}
, a unit complex number that parametrizes the rotational degree of freedom within the chosen irrep.
The phase doubling
u\mapsto u^{2}
of the output embedding
\xi_{m}
in (
5.1
) follows directly from the phase alignment condition (Observation
3.2
), while the
\sqrt{2/|G|}
factor ensures both functions lie on the unit sphere.
The source measure
\pi=\text{Unif}({\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1})\otimes\text{Haar}(\mathbb{D})
formalizes the diversification in Observation
3.2
: every non-trivial irrep is represented equally, phases are distributed Haar-uniformly within each irrep, and the choice of representation is independent of the phase.

Mechanistic Interpretation: The Flawed Indicator via Majority Vote.

We now explain how the diversified network solves the group composition task via a
majority-vote mechanism
.
Specifically, each neuron
m
, parametrized by
(\check{\rho}_{m},u_{m})
, contributes a
biased vote
to the output logit.
As shown in Lemma
C.4
, for input
(g_{1},g_{2})
and output entry
j
, the neuron’s contribution is proportional to

\displaystyle\underbrace{2\cdot\Re\big(\check{\rho}_{m}(j(g_{1}g_{2})^{-1})\big)}_{\displaystyle\text{\small singal term}}+\underbrace{\Re\big(\check{\rho}_{m}(jg_{1}^{-2})\big)+\Re\big(\check{\rho}_{m}(jg_{2}^{-2})\big)}_{\displaystyle\text{\small ghost singal term}}+\underbrace{\sum_{\kappa\in\{-4,-2,2,4\}}C_{\kappa,\check{\rho}_{m}}\cdot(u_{m})^{\kappa}}_{\displaystyle\text{\small noise term}},

where
C_{\kappa,\check{\rho}_{m}}
are complex-valued constants depending on
j,g_{1},g_{2}
.
The key insight is that averaging over a diversified ensemble cancels individual biases.
Since phases
u_{m}
are Haar-uniform and representations
\check{\rho}_{m}
are distributed uniformly, the noise terms vanish in expectation while the signal accumulates coherently.
Moreover, we can show that the mean-field predictor takes the form:

{f}_{\sf NN}^{\mu}(g_{1},g_{2})_{j}\;\propto\;2\cdot\ind(j=g_{1}\star g_{2})+\ind(j=g_{1}^{2})+\ind(j=g_{2}^{2})+{\sf const}.

We characterize this as a
flawed indicator
: it fails to be a perfect delta function due to architectural limitations.
Nevertheless, the correct label always dominates, ensuring a positive logit margin.

Combining the results established so far, we arrive at the following takeaway.

Takeaway of Mechanistic Interpretability.

For any Abelian group
G
without self-conjugate representations, three properties hold simultaneously after the two-stage gradient training:

\triangleright

Single-Frequency and Phase Alignment
(Theorem
4.3
): Each neuron encodes a single rank-1 irreducible representation Fourier coefficients and aligned phases.

\triangleright

Spectral Uniformity
(Theorem
5.1
): The ensemble of neurons achieves full diversification: representations are uniform over
{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}
and phases are Haar-uniform on
\mathbb{D}
.

\triangleright

Scale Explosion
(Theorem
4.5
): The scaling parameter
a
logarithmically grows to infinity.

Together, (i) and (ii) imply that the network becomes a
flawed indicator
predictor via majority vote, and (iii) sharpens the softmax output toward a one-hot prediction.

5.2
Convergence Rate of the Spectral Patterns

By Theorem
4.3
, we know that each neuron eventually learns a single irreducible representation with aligned input-output phases.
But this raises two natural questions:
which
representation does each neuron select, and
how fast
do these spectral patterns emerge?

At initialization, every neuron has energy spread across all representations with unaligned phases.
Training then drives two simultaneous processes:

(i)

Phase Alignment
: The relative phase between the input and output embeddings locks into the relation required by Observation
3.2
.

(ii)

Representation Competition
: The Fourier magnitudes of different irreps compete for dominance until a single winner
\check{\rho}_{m}
together with its conjugate emerges.

We show that both processes converge exponentially fast and, notably, that representation selection follows a
lottery ticket
mechanism: the winning irrep is determined by the random initialization.
To track these processes, we decompose the Fourier coefficient into its magnitude and phase:

\alpha_{\nu,m}[\rho](t)=|\widehat{\nu}[\rho](t)|\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},\qquad\phi_{\nu,m}[\rho](t)=\widehat{\nu}[\rho](t)/|\widehat{\nu}[\rho](t)|\in\mathbb{D}.

We measure phase alignment by the variable
\varphi_{m}[\rho]=\overline{\phi_{\xi,m}[\rho]}\cdot\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho]^{2}\in\mathbb{D}
, which equals one when phases are perfectly aligned.
We adopt the following mild assumption for analytical convenience.

Definition 5.2

(Scaling-Matching Initialization)
.

Consider a coupled random initialization for
\theta_{m}
and
\xi_{m}
with identical Fourier magnitudes:

\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho](0)=\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho](0)
for all
\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)
.

This assumption requires only that
\theta_{m}
and
\xi_{m}
share initial scales per irrep, allowing for arbitrary phasors and non-uniform scales.
This scale-matching property is preserved by the gradient flow (see Lemma
C.5
), justifying the unambiguous notation
\alpha_{m}[\rho](t)
for all
t\geq 0
.

The theorem below decouples these two dynamics, analyzing phase alignment within a fixed representation and representation competition under pre-aligned phases.

Theorem 5.3
.

Let
G
be an Abelian group with no self-conjugate representations, and adopt the scale-matching initialization in Definition
5.2
.
Then the following hold:

(i)

(Phase Alignment). Suppose neuron
m
is initialized with a single representation
\check{\rho}_{m}\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}
, i.e.,
\alpha_{m}[\rho](0)=0
for all
\rho\notin{\rm Orb}(\check{\rho}_{m})
.
Then the phase alignment level
\varphi_{m}[\check{\rho}_{m}]
converges to one. That is, for any
\epsilon>0
, we have
\Re(\varphi_{m}[\check{\rho}_{m}](T))\geq 1-\varepsilon
once

T\gtrsim\frac{M}{a|G|^{1/2}}\cdot\log\left(\frac{2}{\varepsilon}\cdot\frac{1-\Re(\varphi_{m}[\check{\rho}_{m}](0))}{1+\Re(\varphi_{m}[\check{\rho}_{m}](0))}\right).

(ii)

(Representation Competition). Suppose all phases are initially aligned, i.e.,
\varphi_{m}[\rho](0)=1
.
In this case, the irrep with the largest initial magnitude wins,

\check{\rho}_{m}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}}\alpha_{m}[\rho](0)
.
Define the scale ratio
r_{\check{\rho}_{m},\rho}=\alpha_{m}[\check{\rho}_{m}]/\alpha_{m}[\rho]
and let
r_{\min}=\min_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}\backslash{\rm Orb}(\check{\rho}_{m})}r_{\check{\rho}_{m},\rho}(0)
.
Then for any
\varepsilon>0
and training time

T\gtrsim\frac{M}{a|G|}\cdot\max\left\{\frac{\log\!\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{-1}-1}{r_{\min}-1}\right)}{\alpha_{m}[\check{\rho}_{m}](0)},\frac{\log\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{-1}}{r_{\check{\rho}_{m},\rho_{\sf triv}}(0)}\right)}{|G|\cdot\Omega_{m}(0)}\right\}

the scale ratio satisfies
r_{\check{\rho}_{m},\rho}(T)\geq 1/\varepsilon
for all non-winning representations
\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)\backslash{\rm Orb}(\check{\rho}_{m})
.

The proof is provided in §
C.3
.
We discuss the theoretical implications of convergence results.

(a)

Training Dynamics of Phase Alignment.

(b)

Representation Competition within Neuron.

Figure 8
:
Training dynamics of
\mathbb{Z}_{3}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{5}
under the initializations in Theorem
5.3
.
(a)
Phase alignment: the alignment level
\Re(\varphi_{m})
converges to
1
at different speeds depending on the initial phase.
(b)
Representation competition: the magnitude of the winning irrep grows while all competitors decay.

\bullet

Discussion of (i): Phase Alignment.

The first part isolates phase dynamics by assuming the representation competition is resolved.
The objective is for the input and output phases to lock into the relation
\phi_{\xi,m}=\phi_{\theta,m}^{2}
.
This alignment proceeds exponentially, with
1-\Re(\varphi_{m})
reaching
\epsilon
-accuracy in time
\widetilde{O}({M}/(a|G|^{1/2})\cdot\log(1/\epsilon))
.
The closer the initial phase
\Re(\varphi_{m}(0))
is to
1
