be the partial isometries and
r_{\rho}
be the ranks from the equilibrium factorization.
Consider the tangent directions of the form

\displaystyle\Xi_{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho]=

\displaystyle V_{m}[\rho]\Sigma_{m}[\rho](W_{m}[\rho])^{*},\qquad\qquad\Xi_{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho]=U_{m}[\rho]\Sigma_{m}[\rho](V_{m}[\rho])^{*}

\displaystyle\Xi_{\xi_{m}}[\rho]=U_{m}[\rho]\Sigma_{m}[\rho](W_{m}[\rho])^{*},\qquad\forall\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)^{\sharp}_{\neq 1},

(B.24)

where
\Sigma_{m}[\rho]\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{\rho}\times r_{\rho}}
is a diagonal matrix.
Let
\mathcal{T}_{m}
be the linear subspace defined by
\{\Xi_{m}=(\Xi_{\theta_{m}^{1}},\Xi_{\theta_{m}^{2}},\Xi_{\xi_{m}})\\
\in T_{\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{\dagger}}{\mathcal{M}}:\Xi_{\nu}[\rho_{\sf triv}]=0\}
, subject to tangent condition
\sum_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)^{\sharp}_{\neq 1}}d_{\rho}^{\sharp}\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma_{m}[\rho])=0
.
If
\sum_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}}r_{\rho}\geq 2
, then
\mathcal{T}_{m}
is non-trivial with
\dim(\mathcal{T}_{m})\geq 1
.
Then, we have

\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{M}}\Omega(\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{\dagger})[\Xi_{m}]=\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}\cdot\Xi_{m},\qquad\forall\Xi_{m}\in\mathcal{T}_{m}.

Proof of Lemma
B.10
.

Please refer to §
B.2.8
for a detailed proof.
∎

Lemma
B.10
shows that every
\Xi_{m}\in\mathcal{T}_{m}
acts as an eigenvector of the Hessian with positive eigenvalue
\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}>0
.
Therefore, positive-energy equilibria with total rank at least two belong to
{\rm Sad}(\Omega)
.
Only the rank-one, single-representation positive-energy equilibria remain.

B.2.3
Step 3: Saddle Avoidance for Riemannian Gradient Flow

The final ingredient is a general result showing that Riemannian gradient flow avoids all saddle points from random initialization almost surely.

Lemma B.11
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}
be a compact Riemannian manifold with a Riemannian metric
g
, and let

{\mathcal{F}}:{\mathcal{M}}\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth functional satisfying
{\mathcal{F}}\in C^{r}({\mathcal{M}})
with
r\geq 2
.
Consider the Riemannian gradient flow of
{\mathcal{F}}
defined by

\partial_{t}x(t)=\mathrm{grad}_{{\mathcal{M}}}{\mathcal{F}}(x(t)),\qquad x(0)=X_{0}.

For a time
t\in\mathbb{R}
, denote
\phi_{t}(x):{\mathcal{M}}\to{\mathcal{M}}
to be the flow mapping from
x
, and define the global stable set as

W^{\sf s}=\{x\in{\mathcal{M}}:\exists\,p\in{\rm Sad}({\mathcal{F}}),\;\phi_{t}(x)\to p\text{\rm~as~}t\to\infty\},

Then
W^{\sf s}
has Riemannian volume measure zero.
Consequently, if
X_{0}
has a probability measure absolutely continuous with respect to the Riemannian volume measure, then

\mathbb{P}(X_{0}\in W^{\sf s})=0
.

Proof of Lemma
B.11
.

Please refer to §
B.2.9
for a detailed proof.
∎

This theorem extends the classical saddle-avoidance results for these discrete first-order methods
(
17
)
to the continuous Riemannian gradient flows on arbitrary manifolds.
The key idea is that the center-stable manifold of each saddle point is a lower-dimensional submanifold of
\mathcal{M}
, and hence has Riemannian volume zero.
A countable union of such null sets remains null, so a randomly initialized flow avoids all saddle points with probability one.

B.2.4
Assembly: Proof of Theorem
4.3

Proof of Theorem
4.3
.

Since the dynamics in Proposition
B.1
decouple across neurons, it suffices to analyze each neuron
m
separately.
By Lemma
B.3
, the projected GF for neuron
m
is the Riemannian gradient ascent flow of
\Omega
on
{\mathcal{M}}
, evaluated at the Fourier coefficients
\widehat{\Theta}_{m}
(see Step 1 in §
B.2.1
).
As established in
(
15
, Lemma 8.4.4)
, for the Riemannian gradient flow, we have either

\mathrm{grad}_{\mathcal{M}}{\mathcal{F}}(x(t))\to 0\text{~~or~~}|{\mathcal{F}}(x(t))|\to+\infty\text{~~when~~}t\rightarrow\infty.

Since
\mathcal{M}
is compact and
\Omega
is continuous on
\mathcal{M}
, the second possibility is excluded.
Thus the flow converges to some critical point, i.e.,
x(\infty)\in{\rm Crit}(\Omega)
.
It remains to identify which critical points can be reached.
In Step 2 (see §
B.2.2
), we classify all
{\rm Crit}(\Omega)
into five cases.
Notably, we have

\triangleright

Case 1 & 2
. By Lemma
B.6
and
B.7
, convergence to such an equilibrium requires
\widehat{\Theta}_{m}(0)\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\sf init}
. Since
\mathrm{vol}({\mathcal{M}}_{\sf init})=0
and the law of
\widehat{\Theta}_{m}(0)
is absolutely continuous,
\mathbb{P}\big(\widehat{\Theta}_{m}(0)\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\sf init}\big)=0
.

\triangleright

Case 3 & 4
. Lemmas
B.8
,
B.9
, and
B.10
establish that these critical points are strict saddles. By Theorem
B.11
, the gradient flow avoids the stable manifolds of such saddles almost surely.

Therefore, almost surely, only Case 5 remains: positive-energy, rank-one, single-representation equilibria.
These equilibria give the single-representation property
(i)
and the rank alignment with positive proportion
(ii)
.
Furthermore, the equilibrium conditions in (
B.21
) imply:

(\widehat{\theta^{2}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger})^{*}\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}=|G|\cdot\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}\cdot\widehat{\theta^{1}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger},

with analogous identities holding for
\widehat{\theta}^{2}_{m}[\rho]^{\dagger}
and
\widehat{\xi}_{m}[\rho]^{\dagger}
. These relations ensure rotational alignment, while
\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}>0
establishes the positive proportionality, thereby completing the proof.
∎

We now prove the supporting results stated in §
B.2.1
–§
B.2.3
.

B.2.5
Proofs of Lemma
B.3
and
B.4

Proof of Lemma
B.3
.

The Riemannian gradient on
{\mathcal{M}}=\mathbb{S}(\mathcal{H})^{3}
is obtained by projecting the ambient gradient onto the tangent space.
The orthogonal projection is given by

\displaystyle\Pi_{\widehat{\nu}}(U)=U-\langle U,\widehat{\nu}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}\cdot\widehat{\nu}\in T_{\widehat{\nu}}\mathbb{S}(\mathcal{H}),\qquad\forall\widehat{\nu}\in\mathbb{S}(\mathcal{H}).

(B.25)

Since
{\mathcal{M}}
is a product of three sphere factors, the projection acts block-wise:

\displaystyle\mathrm{grad}_{{\mathcal{M}}}\Omega(\widehat{\Theta}_{m})=\Pi_{\widehat{\Theta}_{m}}\big(\nabla_{\mathcal{H}^{3}}\Omega\big)=\big(\Pi_{{\widehat{\nu}}}(\nabla_{\widehat{\nu}}\Omega)\big)_{\nu\in\{\theta_{m}^{1},\theta_{m}^{2},\xi_{m}\}},\quad\Pi_{\widehat{\Theta}_{m}}=\bigoplus_{\nu\in\{\theta_{m}^{1},\theta_{m}^{2},\xi_{m}\}}\Pi_{\widehat{\nu}}.

(B.26)

The proof proceeds in two steps: we first compute the ambient gradient
\nabla_{\mathcal{H}^{3}}\Omega
, and then apply the projection
\Pi_{\widehat{\Theta}_{m}}
to obtain the Riemannian gradient.
Recall that the ambient gradient
\nabla_{\widehat{\nu}}\Omega\in\mathcal{H}
is the Riesz representative of the Fréchet derivative
{\rm D}_{\widehat{\nu}}\Omega
, defined by

{\rm D}_{\widehat{\nu}}\Omega(\Xi_{\nu})=\big\langle\nabla_{\widehat{\nu}}\Omega,\Xi_{\nu}\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}
for all
\Xi_{\nu}\in\mathcal{H}
.
We take
\nu=\theta_{m}^{1}
as an example.
Since
\Omega
is linear in
\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}
, the Fréchet derivative is given by

\displaystyle{\rm D}_{\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}}\Omega(\Xi_{\theta_{m}})=\sum_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)^{\sharp}_{\neq 1}}d_{\rho}^{\sharp}\cdot\Re\big(\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\big((\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho])^{*}\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho]\,\Xi_{\theta_{m}}[\rho]\big)\big).

(B.27)

Following this, we can identify the
\rho
-th block of
\nabla_{\widehat{\theta}_{m}^{1}}\Omega
by matching (
B.27
) with the inner product form defined in (
B.18
).
This gives the ambient gradient:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho]}\Omega=1/|G|\cdot(\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho])^{*}\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]\cdot\ind(\rho\neq\rho_{\sf triv}),\qquad\forall\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}^{\sharp}.

(B.28)

We now apply the projection to the ambient gradient in (
B.28
).
Based on (
B.26
), we need to compute
\Pi_{\widehat{\theta}_{m}^{1}}(\nabla_{\widehat{\theta}_{m}^{1}}\Omega)
.
Substituting (
B.25
) gives that

\displaystyle\Pi_{\widehat{\theta}_{m}^{1}}(\nabla_{\widehat{\theta}_{m}^{1}}\Omega)=\nabla_{\widehat{\theta}_{m}^{1}}\Omega-\langle\nabla_{\widehat{\theta}_{m}^{1}}\Omega,\widehat{\theta}_{m}^{1}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}\cdot\widehat{\theta}_{m}^{1}.

(B.29)

It remains to evaluate the inner product
\langle\nabla_{\widehat{\theta}_{m}^{1}}\Omega,\widehat{\theta}_{m}^{1}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}
.
Substituting (
B.28
), we obtain

\displaystyle\langle\nabla_{\widehat{\theta}_{m}^{1}}\Omega,\widehat{\theta}_{m}^{1}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}

\displaystyle=|G|\cdot\sum_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)^{\sharp}_{\neq 1}}d_{\rho}^{\sharp}\cdot 1/|G|\cdot\Re\big(\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\big((\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho])^{*}\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho]\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho]\big)\big)=\Omega(\widehat{\Theta}_{m}).

(B.30)

Substituting (
B.30
) back into (
B.29
) yields

\displaystyle\Pi_{\widehat{\theta}_{m}^{1}}(\nabla_{\widehat{\theta}_{m}^{1}}\Omega)=1/|G|\cdot\nabla_{\widehat{\theta}_{m}^{1}}\Omega-\Omega(\widehat{\Theta}_{m})\cdot\widehat{\theta}_{m}^{1}.

(B.31)

Analogous identities hold for
\widehat{\theta}_{m}^{2}
and
\widehat{\xi}_{m}
by symmetry.
Comparing (
B.31
) and its counterparts with the dynamics in Proposition
B.1
, we see that scaling
\mathrm{grad}_{\mathcal{M}}\Omega
by the constant
2a|G|^{2}/M
recovers the exact dynamics, which completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
B.4
.

The proof proceeds in two steps: we first compute the ambient Hessian
\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{H}^{3}}\Omega
, then derive the Riemannian Hessian by accounting for the sphere constraint.

Step 1: Ambient Hessian.

The ambient Hessian is defined as
\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{H}^{3}}\Omega(\widehat{\Theta}_{m})[\Xi_{m}]={\rm D}(\nabla_{\mathcal{H}^{3}}\Omega(\widehat{\Theta}_{m}))[\Xi_{m}]
.
From (
B.28
) and its cyclic counterparts, the ambient gradient is

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathcal{H}^{3}}\Omega(\widehat{\Theta}_{m})

\displaystyle=1/|G|\cdot\bigg(\bigoplus_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)^{\sharp}}(\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho])^{*}\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]\cdot\ind(\rho\neq\rho_{\sf triv}),

\displaystyle\qquad\bigoplus_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)^{\sharp}}\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho](\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho])^{*}\cdot\ind(\rho\neq\rho_{\sf triv}),\;\bigoplus_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)^{\sharp}}\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho]\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho]\cdot\ind(\rho\neq\rho_{\sf triv})\bigg).

Each block is a bilinear product of the other two layers’ Fourier coefficients.
Differentiating the
\theta_{m}^{1}
-block via the product rule gives

\displaystyle{\rm D}\big((\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho])^{*}\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]\big)[\Xi_{m}]

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\varepsilon}\,(\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho]+\varepsilon\Xi_{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho])^{*}(\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]+\varepsilon\Xi_{\xi_{m}}[\rho])\right|_{\varepsilon=0}

\displaystyle=(\Xi_{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho])^{*}\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]+(\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho])^{*}\Xi_{\xi_{m}}[\rho].

Assembling across all
\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)^{\sharp}
yields the
\theta_{m}^{1}
-block of the ambient Hessian in (
B.20a
).
The remaining blocks (
B.20b
) and (
B.20c
) follow by the same product rule applied cyclically.

Step 2: Riemannian Hessian.

Since
\mathrm{grad}_{\mathcal{M}}\Omega=\Pi_{\widehat{\Theta}_{m}}(\nabla_{\mathcal{H}^{3}}\Omega)
, the Riemannian Hessian involves differentiating both the projection and the ambient gradient:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{M}}\Omega(\widehat{\Theta}_{m})[\Xi_{m}]

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\widehat{\Theta}_{m}}\Big({\rm D}\big(\Pi_{\widehat{\Theta}_{m}}(\nabla_{\mathcal{H}^{3}}\Omega(\widehat{\Theta}_{m}))\big)[\Xi_{m}]\Big)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\Pi_{\widehat{\Theta}_{m}}\big({\rm D}_{1}\Pi_{\widehat{\Theta}_{m}}[\Xi_{m}]\big(\nabla_{\mathcal{H}^{3}}\Omega(\widehat{\Theta}_{m})\big)\big)}_{\displaystyle\text{\small curvature term}}+\underbrace{\Pi_{\widehat{\Theta}_{m}}\big(\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{H}^{3}}\Omega(\widehat{\Theta}_{m})[\Xi_{m}]\big)}_{\displaystyle\text{\small projected ambient Hessian}},

where
{\rm D}_{1}\Pi_{\widehat{\Theta}_{m}}[\Xi_{m}]
denotes the derivative of the projection with respect to the base point
\widehat{\Theta}_{m}
in the direction
\Xi_{m}
.
We now compute the curvature term.
For a single sphere factor, the projection is
\Pi_{X}(Y)=Y-\langle Y,X\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}\cdot X
, and differentiating with respect to
X
in the direction
\Xi
gives

\displaystyle{\rm D}_{1}\Pi_{X}[\Xi](Y)

\displaystyle=-\langle Y,\Xi\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}\cdot X-\langle Y,X\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}\cdot\Xi.

We apply
\Pi_{X}
to this expression.
Then, the first term vanishes and for the second term
\Pi_{X}(\Xi)=\Xi
since
\Xi\in T_{X}\mathbb{S}(\mathcal{H})
implies
\langle\Xi,X\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}=0
.
Therefore, we have

\Pi_{X}\big({\rm D}_{1}\Pi_{X}[\Xi](Y)\big)=-\langle Y,X\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}\cdot\Xi.

Since
{\mathcal{M}}=\mathbb{S}(\mathcal{H})^{3}
is a product manifold, the projection
\Pi_{\widehat{\Theta}_{m}}
acts block-wise on the three factors. Applying the identity above to each factor gives

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{M}}\Omega(\widehat{\Theta}_{m})[\Xi_{m}]=\Pi_{\widehat{\Theta}_{m}}\big(\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{H}^{3}}\Omega(\widehat{\Theta}_{m})[\Xi_{m}]\big)-\big(\langle\nabla_{\widehat{\nu}}\Omega,\widehat{\nu}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}\cdot\Xi_{\nu}\big)_{\nu\in\{\theta_{m}^{1},\theta_{m}^{2},\xi_{m}\}}.

(B.32)

It remains to show that all three inner products equal
\Omega(\widehat{\Theta}_{m})
.
By the same calculation as in (
B.30
), substituting the ambient gradient (
B.28
) into the
L^{2}(\mathcal{H})
inner product yields

\displaystyle\big\langle\nabla_{\widehat{\nu}}\Omega,\widehat{\nu}\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}=\Omega(\widehat{\Theta}_{m}),\qquad\forall\nu\in\{\theta_{m}^{1},\theta_{m}^{2},\xi_{m}\}.

(B.33)

Substituting (
B.33
) into (
B.32
) gives the claimed decomposition (
B.19
) and completes the proof.
∎

B.2.6
Proof of Lemma
B.5
,
B.6
,
B.7
: Cases 1 & 2

Proof of Lemma
B.5
.

According to the dynamics in Proposition
B.1
and the chain rule, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\|\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho]\|_{\rm F}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{4a|G|}{M}\cdot\Re\big(\operatorname{tr}\big((\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho])^{*}(\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho])^{*}\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]\big)\big)\cdot\ind(\rho\neq\rho_{\sf triv})-\frac{4a|G|^{2}}{M}\cdot\Omega_{m}\cdot\|\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho]\|_{\rm F}^{2},

(B.34)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\|\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho]\|_{\rm F}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{4a|G|}{M}\cdot\Re\big(\operatorname{tr}\big((\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho])^{*}\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho](\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho])^{*}\big)\big)\cdot\ind(\rho\neq\rho_{\sf triv})-\frac{4a|G|^{2}}{M}\cdot\Omega_{m}\cdot\|\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho]\|_{\rm F}^{2},

(B.35)

and similarly for

\|\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]\|_{\rm F}^{2}
.
Subtracting (
B.35
) from
(
B.34
) gives that for all
\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)^{\sharp}
:

\partial_{t}\Delta^{1}_{m}[\rho]=-\frac{4a|G|^{2}}{M}\cdot\Omega_{m}\cdot\Delta^{1}_{m}[\rho],\qquad\Delta^{1}_{m}[\rho]=\|\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho]\|_{\rm F}^{2}-\|\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho]\|_{\rm F}^{2}.

Solving this scalar linear equation explicitly yields

\Delta^{1}_{m}[\rho](t)=\Delta^{1}_{m}[\rho](0)\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{4a|G|^{2}}{M}\int_{0}^{t}\Omega_{m}(s)\,{\rm d}s\Big),\qquad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}.

The same argument, subtracting the evolution of

\|\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]\|_{\rm F}^{2}
from that of

\|\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho]\|_{\rm F}^{2}
, gives the corresponding formula for

\Delta^{2}_{m}[\rho]
.
This completes the proof of (
B.23
).
∎

Proof of Lemma
B.6
.

By Lemma
B.3
, the dynamics in Proposition

B.1
is the Riemannian gradient flow of
\Omega_{m}
.
As a result,
\Omega_{m}(t)
is a non-decreasing function in
t
and thus

\Omega_{m}(t)\leq\Omega_{m}(\infty)=\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}<0,\qquad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}.

As a consequence, for all
t\geq 0
and
\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}^{\sharp}
, (
B.23
) gives

|\Delta^{1}_{m}[\rho](t)|=|\Delta^{1}_{m}[\rho](0)|\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{4a|G|^{2}}{M}\int_{0}^{t}\Omega_{m}(s)\,{\rm d}s\Big)\geq|\Delta^{1}_{m}[\rho](0)|\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{4a|G|^{2}}{M}\cdot\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}\cdot t\Big).

Since
\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}<0
, we have the exponential term goes to infinity as
t\to+\infty
.
If
\Delta^{1}_{m}[\rho](0)\neq 0
, then
we have
|\Delta^{1}_{m}[\rho](t)|\to+\infty
,
contradicts the boundness of the
{\mathcal{M}}
. This contradiction shows that

\Delta^{1}_{m}[\rho](0)=0\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\|\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho](0)\|_{\rm F}^{2}=\|\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho](0)\|_{\rm F}^{2}.

Applying the same argument to the corresponding equation comparing

\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho]
and
\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]
, we also obtain

\|\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho](0)\|_{\rm F}^{2}=\|\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho](0)\|_{\rm F}^{2}
.
Hence,
\widehat{\Theta}_{m}(0)
satisfies the defining equal-norm constraints of

{\mathcal{M}}_{\sf init}
. Therefore, convergence to
\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{\dagger}
specified in Case 1 is possible
only if
\widehat{\Theta}_{m}(0)\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\sf init}
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
B.7
.

The proof follows an argument analogous to that of Lemma
B.6
.
Note that if
\widehat{\Theta}_{m}(t)
converges to
\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{\dagger}
as
t\to+\infty
, then

\Omega_{m}(t)\to\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}=0
. Therefore,

\Omega_{m}(t)\leq 0
for every
t\geq 0
.
Using (
B.23
), for every
\rho
and every
t\geq 0
, we can obtain that

|\Delta^{1}_{m}[\rho](t)|=|\Delta^{1}_{m}[\rho](0)|\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{4a|G|^{2}}{M}\int_{0}^{t}\Omega_{m}(s)\,{\rm d}s\Big)\geq|\Delta^{1}_{m}[\rho](0)|.

On the other hand, we have

\widehat{\theta_{m}^{\tau}}[\rho](\infty)=\widehat{\theta_{m}^{\tau}}[\rho]^{\dagger}=0\text{~~if~~}\rho\neq\rho_{\sf triv}\text{~~for all~~}\tau\in\{1,2\}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\Delta^{1}_{m}[\rho](\infty)=0.

The preceding lower bound forces
\Delta^{1}_{m}[\rho](0)=0
, or equivalently,
\|\widehat{\theta}_{m}^{2}[\rho](0)\|_{\text{F}}^{2}=\|\widehat{\theta}_{m}^{1}[\rho](0)\|_{\text{F}}^{2}
.
An analogous argument yields the constraint
\|\widehat{\theta}_{m}^{2}[\rho](0)\|_{\text{F}}^{2}=\|\widehat{\xi}_{m}[\rho](0)\|_{\text{F}}^{2}
.
Consequently, we have the convergence to
\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{\dagger}
in Case 2 is possible only if
\widehat{\Theta}_{m}(0)\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\sf init}
, which completes the proof.
∎

B.2.7
Proof of Lemma
B.8
: Case 3

Proof of Lemma
B.8
.

The proof includes three steps: we first establish the orthogonal block structure of the equilibrium, then simplify the Riemannian Hessian, and finally construct an explicit ascent direction that is a positive eigenvector of the Hessian.

Step 1: Orthogonal Block Structure.

We start by showing that the three equilibrium coefficient matrices have mutually orthogonal column and row spaces, and derive their block SVD factorization.
Fix
\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)^{\sharp}_{\neq 1}
.
Since
\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}=0
, the equilibrium conditions reduce to

\displaystyle(\widehat{\theta^{2}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger})^{*}\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}=0,\qquad\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}(\widehat{\theta^{1}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger})^{*}=0,\qquad\widehat{\theta^{2}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}\widehat{\theta^{1}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}=0.

which implies that the column spaces and row spaces of these matrices are mutually orthogonal.
Let
r_{1,\rho},r_{2,\rho},r_{3,\rho}
denote the ranks of
\widehat{\theta^{1}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger},\widehat{\theta^{2}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}
, and
\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}
, respectively.
These constraints imply that the total dimension
d_{\rho}
can accommodate the ranks pairwise:

\displaystyle r_{i,\rho}+r_{j,\rho}\leq d_{\rho},\qquad\forall(i,j)\in\{S\subseteq\{1,2,3\}:|S|=2\}.

(B.36)

To represent these constraints, we construct partial isometries
with orthonormal columns such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}(V_{1,m}[\rho])

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Im}(\widehat{\theta^{1}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}),\qquad

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}(U_{2,m}[\rho])

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Im}(\widehat{\theta^{2}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}),\qquad

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}(U_{3,m}[\rho])

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Im}(\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}),

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}(W_{1,m}[\rho])

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Im}\bigl((\widehat{\theta^{1}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger})^{*}\bigr),\qquad

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}(V_{2,m}[\rho])

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Im}\bigl((\widehat{\theta^{2}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger})^{*}\bigr),\qquad

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}(W_{3,m}[\rho])

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Im}\bigl((\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger})^{*}\bigr),

By the orthogonality relations established above,

\displaystyle(U_{2,m}[\rho])^{*}U_{3,m}[\rho]=0,\qquad(V_{2,m}[\rho])^{*}V_{1,m}[\rho]=0,\qquad(W_{3,m}[\rho])^{*}W_{1,m}[\rho]=0.

(B.37)

Take
\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho]^{\dagger}
as an example.
Define

P_{m}[\rho]=(V_{1,m}[\rho])^{*}\,\widehat{\theta^{1}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}\,W_{1,m}[\rho]\in\mathbb{C}^{r_{\rho}\times r_{\rho}},

which is invertible.
By the singular value decomposition, there exist unitary matrices

Q_{m}[\rho],Q^{\prime}_{m}[\rho]
and a diagonal matrix

\Sigma_{1,m}[\rho]

such that
P_{m}[\rho]=Q_{m}[\rho]\Sigma_{1,m}[\rho](Q^{\prime}_{m}[\rho])^{*}
.
Rotating the isometries by

V_{1,m}[\rho]\mapsto V_{1,m}[\rho]Q_{m}[\rho],\qquad W_{1,m}[\rho]\mapsto W_{1,m}[\rho]Q^{\prime}_{m}[\rho],

we diagonalize
\widehat{\theta^{1}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}
.
Since these rotations are unitary, they preserve the relevant column spaces and hence do not affect the orthogonality relations. Relabelling the rotated isometries, and applying the same argument to the other two coefficients, gives

\displaystyle\widehat{\theta^{1}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}=

\displaystyle V_{1,m}[\rho]\Sigma_{1,m}[\rho](W_{1,m}[\rho])^{*},\qquad\widehat{\theta^{2}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}=U_{2,m}[\rho]\Sigma_{2,m}[\rho](V_{2,m}[\rho])^{*},

\displaystyle\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}=U_{3,m}[\rho]\Sigma_{3,m}[\rho](W_{3,m}[\rho])^{*},\qquad\forall\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}^{\sharp}.

(B.38)

Take
U_{2,m}[\rho]
and
U_{3,m}[\rho]
as an example.
(
B.36
) and (
B.37
) imply that
{\rm Im}(U_{2,m}[\rho])\perp{\rm Im}(U_{3,m}[\rho])
with
r_{2,\rho}+r_{3,\rho}\leq d_{\rho}
.
Therefore, we can extend these bases by appending
d_{\rho}-r_{2,\rho}-r_{3,\rho}
orthonormal columns to form a full unitary matrix
U_{m}[\rho]\in\mathbb{C}^{d_{\rho}\times d_{\rho}}
.
The same construction applies to
V_{m}[\rho]
and
W_{m}[\rho]
.
Under these basis transformations, each coefficient occupies its own diagonal block:

\displaystyle\widehat{\theta^{1}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}=V_{m}[\rho]\begin{pmatrix}\Sigma_{1,m}[\rho]&&\\
&\mathbf{0}&\\
&&\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle(W_{m}[\rho])^{*},\qquad\widehat{\theta^{2}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}=U_{m}[\rho]\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{0}&&\\
&\Sigma_{2,m}[\rho]&\\
&&\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}(V_{m}[\rho])^{*},

\displaystyle\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}=U_{m}

\displaystyle[\rho]\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{0}&&\\
&\mathbf{0}&\\
&&\Sigma_{3,m}[\rho]\end{pmatrix}(W_{m}[\rho])^{*}.

Step 2: Hessian Simplification.

In the second step, we show that when
\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}=0
, the Riemannian Hessian coincides with the ambient Hessian.
According to Lemma
B.4
, when
\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}=0
, we have

\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{M}}\Omega(\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{\dagger})[\Xi_{m}]=\Pi_{\widehat{\Theta}_{m}}\big(\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{H}^{3}}\Omega(\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{\dagger})[\Xi_{m}]\big).

Since projection acts as
\Pi_{\widehat{\nu}}({\rm HS}_{\nu})={\rm HS}_{\nu}-\langle{\rm HS}_{\nu},\widehat{\nu}^{\dagger}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}\cdot\widehat{\nu}^{\dagger}
, it suffices to show the inner products vanish.
By (
B.20a
), (
B.20b
) and (
B.20c
), each
{\rm HS}_{\nu}
is a bilinear product of the tangent direction and equilibrium blocks from the other two layers.
Hence, the equilibrium conditions in (
B.21
) ensure that these cross-layer products are orthogonal to the equilibrium itself such that

\langle\widehat{\theta^{1}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger},{\rm HS}_{\theta_{m}^{1}}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}=\langle\widehat{\theta^{2}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger},{\rm HS}_{\theta_{m}^{2}}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}=\langle\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger},{\rm HS}_{\xi_{m}}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}=0.

Therefore, we have
\Pi_{\widehat{\Theta}_{m}}({\rm HS})={\rm HS}
, and thus

\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{M}}\Omega(\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{\dagger})[\Xi_{m}]=\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{H}^{3}}\Omega(\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{\dagger})[\Xi_{m}].

Step 3: Eigenvector Construction.

In the final step, we construct an explicit tangent vector
\Xi_{m}
and show it is an eigenvector of the Hessian with a positive eigenvalue.
It suffices to assume that at least one coefficient is nonzero by symmetry.
Without loss of generality, assume for some
\rho_{\circ}\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)^{\sharp}_{\neq 1}
,
r_{1,\rho_{\circ}}>0
, i.e.,
\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho_{\circ}]
is non-zero.
Recall that
\widehat{\theta^{1}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}=V_{1,m}[\rho]\Sigma_{1,m}[\rho](W_{1,m}[\rho])^{*}
for all
\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}^{\sharp}
.
Let
v_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}]
and
w_{1,m}[\rho]_{\circ}
denote the leading left and right singular vectors of
\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho_{\circ}]^{\dagger}
.
Then,

\displaystyle\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho_{\circ}]^{\dagger}w_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}]=s_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}]\cdot v_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}],\qquad(\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho_{\circ}]^{\dagger})^{*}v_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}]=s_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}]\cdot w_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}],

(B.39)

where
s_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}]
denotes the corresponding singular value with
s_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}]>0
.
As established in (
B.37
) and (
B.38
), the blocks within each basis are mutually orthogonal.
Therefore, we can show that

\displaystyle\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho_{\circ}]^{\dagger}w_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}]={\bm{0}},\qquad\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho_{\circ}]^{\dagger}v_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}]={\bm{0}}.

(B.40)

Our goal is to construct a tangent direction
\Xi_{m}
that corresponds to an eigenvector of the Hessian with a positive eigenvalue.
We analyze this in two scenarios based on the available degrees of freedom:
r_{2,\rho_{\circ}}+r_{3,\rho_{\circ}}<d_{\rho_{\circ}}
and the case where
r_{2,\rho_{\circ}}+r_{3,\rho_{\circ}}=d_{\rho_{\circ}}
.

Case 1:
r_{2,\rho_{\circ}}+r_{3,\rho_{\circ}}<d_{\rho_{\circ}}
.

In this case, there exists a unit vector
\widetilde{u}_{m}
in the remaining free block of
U_{m}[\rho_{\circ}]
.
Since this vector is orthogonal to both the
\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}
-block and the
\widehat{\xi_{m}}
-block, we have

\displaystyle({\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho_{\circ}]^{\dagger}})^{*}\widetilde{u}_{m}={\bm{0}},\qquad(\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho_{\circ}]^{\dagger})^{*}\widetilde{u}_{m}={\bm{0}}.

(B.41)

Define the tangent direction
\Xi_{m}
by setting
\Xi_{\nu}[\rho]={\bm{0}}
for all
\rho\neq\rho_{\circ}
and
\nu\in\{\theta_{m}^{1},\theta_{m}^{2},\xi_{m}\}
, and

\Xi_{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho_{\circ}]={\bm{0}},\qquad\Xi_{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho_{\circ}]=\widetilde{u}_{m}(v_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}])^{*},\qquad\Xi_{\xi_{m}}[\rho_{\circ}]=\widetilde{u}_{m}(w_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}])^{*}.

We first verify that
\Xi_{m}\in T_{\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{\dagger}}\mathcal{M}
.
By direct calculation and using (
B.41
), we have

\langle\Xi_{\theta^{2}_{m}},\widehat{\theta^{2}_{m}}^{\dagger}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}=d_{\rho_{\circ}}\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\big((\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho_{\circ}]^{\dagger})^{*}\Xi_{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho_{\circ}]\big)=d_{\rho_{\circ}}\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\big((\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho_{\circ}]^{\dagger})^{*}\widetilde{u}_{m}(v_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}])^{*}\big)=0.

Similarly, we have

\langle\Xi_{\xi_{m}},\widehat{\xi_{m}}^{\dagger}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}=\langle\Xi_{\theta^{1}_{m}},\widehat{\theta^{1}_{m}}^{\dagger}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}=0
and therefore
\Xi_{m}\in T_{\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{\dagger}}\mathcal{M}
.
By (
B.20a
) and (
B.41
), the
\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}
-block of the ambient Hessian for representation
\rho_{\circ}
is given by

\displaystyle|G|\cdot{\rm HS}_{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho_{\circ}]

\displaystyle=(\Xi_{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho_{\circ}])^{*}\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho_{\circ}]+(\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho_{\circ}])^{*}\Xi_{\xi_{m}}[\rho_{\circ}]

\displaystyle=v_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}](\widetilde{u}_{m})^{*}\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho_{\circ}]+(\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho_{\circ}])^{*}\widetilde{u}_{m}(w_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}])^{*}={\bm{0}}.

Furthermore, by (
B.20b
) and (
B.39
), we have

\displaystyle|G|\cdot{\rm HS}_{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho_{\circ}]

\displaystyle=\Xi_{\xi_{m}}[\rho_{\circ}](\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho_{\circ}])^{*}+\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho_{\circ}](\Xi_{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho_{\circ}])^{*}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{u}_{m}(w_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}])^{*}(\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho_{\circ}])^{*}=s_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}]\cdot\widetilde{u}_{m}v_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}]^{*}=s_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}]\cdot\Xi_{\theta_{m}^{2}},

and similarly we have
|G|\cdot{\rm HS}_{\xi_{m}}[\rho_{\circ}]=s_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}]\cdot\Xi_{\xi_{m}}
.
Combining all three blocks and
{\rm HS}_{\nu}[\rho]=\Xi_{\nu}={\bm{0}}
for all
\rho\neq\rho_{\circ}
and
\nu\in\{\theta_{m}^{1},\theta_{m}^{2},\xi_{m}\}
, based on Lemma
B.4
, we obtain

\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{M}}\Omega(\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{\dagger})[\Xi_{m}]=\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{H}^{3}}\Omega(\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{\dagger})[\Xi_{m}]=({\rm HS}_{\theta_{m}^{1}},{\rm HS}_{\theta_{m}^{2}},{\rm HS}_{\xi_{m}})=|G|^{-1}\cdot s_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}]\cdot\Xi_{m}.

Therefore,
\Xi_{m}
is an eigenvector with positive eigenvalue
\lambda_{m}^{+}=s_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}]/|G|>0
.

Case 2:
r_{2,\rho_{\circ}}+r_{3,\rho_{\circ}}=d_{\rho_{\circ}}
.

In this case, there is no free block in
U_{m}[\rho_{\circ}]
to pair with.
However, since
r_{1,\rho_{\circ}}+r_{2,\rho_{\circ}}\leq d_{\rho_{\circ}}
and
r_{2,\rho_{\circ}}=d_{\rho_{\circ}}-r_{3,\rho_{\circ}}
, it forces
r_{2,\rho_{\circ}}=d_{\rho_{\circ}}-r_{3,\rho_{\circ}}\geq r_{1,\rho_{\circ}}>0
.
Similar to (
B.39
), let
u_{2,m}[\rho_{\circ}]
and
v_{2,m}[\rho_{\circ}]
denote the leading left and right singular vectors of
\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho_{\circ}]^{\dagger}
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho_{\circ}]^{\dagger}v_{2,m}[\rho_{\circ}]=s_{2,m}[\rho_{\circ}]\cdot u_{2,m}[\rho_{\circ}],\qquad(\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho_{\circ}]^{\dagger})^{*}u_{2,m}[\rho_{\circ}]=s_{2,m}[\rho_{\circ}]\cdot v_{2,m}[\rho_{\circ}],

(B.42)

where
s_{2,m}[\rho_{\circ}]>0
denotes the corresponding positive singular value.
Similar to (
B.40
), we have

\displaystyle(\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho_{\circ}]^{\dagger})^{*}u_{2,m}[\rho_{\circ}]={\bm{0}},\qquad(\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho_{\circ}]^{\dagger})^{*}v_{2,m}[\rho_{\circ}]={\bm{0}}.

(B.43)

Let
\lambda_{m}^{+}=\big\{(s_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}])^{2}+(s_{2,m}[\rho_{\circ}])^{2}\big\}^{1/2}
.
Similar to Case 1, we define the tangent direction
\Xi_{m}
by setting
\Xi_{\nu}[\rho]={\bm{0}}
for all
\rho\neq\rho_{\circ}
and
\nu\in\{\theta_{m}^{1},\theta_{m}^{2},\xi_{m}\}
, while
\Xi_{\xi_{m}}[\rho_{\circ}]=u_{2,m}[\rho_{\circ}](w_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}])^{*}
and

\Xi_{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho_{\circ}]={s_{2,m}[\rho_{\circ}]}/{\lambda_{m}^{+}}\cdot v_{2,m}[\rho_{\circ}](w_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}])^{*},\qquad\Xi_{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho_{\circ}]={s_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}]}/{\lambda_{m}^{+}}\cdot u_{2,m}[\rho_{\circ}](v_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}])^{*}.

We again verify the tangent condition for
\Xi_{m}\in T_{\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{\dagger}}\mathcal{M}
.
By direct calculation, (
B.43
) implies that

\displaystyle\langle\Xi_{\theta^{1}_{m}},\widehat{\theta^{1}_{m}}^{\dagger}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}

\displaystyle=d_{\rho_{\circ}}\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\big((\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho_{\circ}]^{\dagger})^{*}\Xi_{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho_{\circ}]\big)

\displaystyle=d_{\rho_{\circ}}\cdot{s_{2,m}[\rho_{\circ}]}/{\lambda_{m}^{+}}\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\big((\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho_{\circ}]^{\dagger})^{*}v_{2,m}[\rho_{\circ}](w_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}])^{*}\big)=0,

and
\langle\Xi_{\xi_{m}},\widehat{\xi_{m}}^{\dagger}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}=0
.
Analogously, the orthogonal relationship in (
B.40
) ensures that

\displaystyle\langle\Xi_{\theta^{2}_{m}},\widehat{\theta^{2}_{m}}^{\dagger}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}

\displaystyle=d_{\rho_{\circ}}\cdot{s_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}]}/{\lambda_{m}^{+}}\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\big((\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho_{\circ}]^{\dagger})^{*}u_{2,m}[\rho_{\circ}](v_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}])^{*}\big)=0,

where the last equality uses the cyclicity of the trace operator.
Combining these results, we conclude that
\Xi_{m}\in T_{\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{\dagger}}\mathcal{M}
, as desired.
Next, we compute the ambient Hessian.
Substituting the definitions of
\Xi_{\theta_{m}^{2}}
and
\Xi_{\xi_{m}}
into (
B.20a
) yields that

\displaystyle|G|\cdot{\rm HS}_{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho_{\circ}]

\displaystyle=(\Xi_{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho_{\circ}])^{*}\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho_{\circ}]+(\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho_{\circ}])^{*}\Xi_{\xi_{m}}[\rho_{\circ}]

\displaystyle={s_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}]}/{\lambda_{m}^{+}}\cdot v_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}](u_{2,m}[\rho_{\circ}])^{*}\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho_{\circ}]^{\dagger}+(\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho]^{\dagger})^{*}u_{2,m}[\rho_{\circ}](w_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}])^{*}

\displaystyle=s_{2,m}[\rho_{\circ}]\cdot v_{2,m}[\rho_{\circ}](w_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}])^{*}=\lambda_{m}^{+}\cdot\Xi_{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho_{\circ}].

where the third inequality results from the orthogonal relationship in (
B.43
) and the singular form in (
B.42
).
Applying the same argument to
\theta_{m}^{2}
yields that
|G|\cdot{\rm HS}_{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho_{\circ}]=\lambda_{m}^{+}\cdot\Xi_{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho_{\circ}]
.
Furthermore, for
\xi_{m}
-block, we can show that

\displaystyle|G|\cdot{\rm HS}_{\xi_{m}}[\rho_{\circ}]

\displaystyle=\Xi_{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho_{\circ}]\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho_{\circ}]+\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho_{\circ}]\Xi_{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho_{\circ}]

\displaystyle={s_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}]}/{\lambda_{m}^{+}}\cdot u_{2,m}[\rho_{\circ}](v_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}])^{*}\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho_{\circ}]+{s_{2,m}[\rho_{\circ}]}/{\lambda_{m}^{+}}\cdot\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho_{\circ}]v_{2,m}[\rho_{\circ}](w_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}])^{*}

\displaystyle={s_{1,m}^{2}[\rho_{\circ}]}/{\lambda_{m}^{+}}\cdot u_{2,m}[\rho_{\circ}](w_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}])^{*}+{s_{2,m}^{2}[\rho_{\circ}]}/{\lambda_{m}^{+}}\cdot u_{2,m}[\rho_{\circ}](w_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}])^{*}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{m}^{+}\cdot u_{2,m}[\rho_{\circ}](w_{1,m}[\rho_{\circ}])^{*}=\lambda_{m}^{+}\cdot\Xi_{\xi_{m}}[\rho_{\circ}].

and
{\rm HS}_{\nu}[\rho]=\Xi_{\nu}={\bm{0}}
for all
\rho\neq\rho_{\circ}
and
\nu\in\{\theta_{m}^{1},\theta_{m}^{2},\xi_{m}\}
, based on Lemma
B.4
, we have

\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{M}}\Omega(\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{\dagger})[\Xi_{m}]=\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{H}^{3}}\Omega(\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{\dagger})[\Xi_{m}]=|G|^{-1}\cdot\lambda_{m}^{+}\cdot\Xi_{m},

and therefore
\Xi_{m}
is an eigenvector with positive eigenvalue
\lambda_{m}^{+}/|G|>0
.
In both cases, we have constructed a tangent vector
\Xi_{m}\in T_{\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{\dagger}}\mathcal{M}
that is an eigenvector of
\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{M}}\Omega(\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{\dagger})
with a positive eigenvalue, confirming that the equilibrium is a saddle point, which completes the proof.
∎

B.2.8
Proof of Lemma
B.9
and
B.10
: Case 4

Proof of Lemma
B.9
.

Fix
\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)^{\sharp}_{\neq 1}
.
The proof proceeds in three steps: we first show that all three Fourier coefficients share the same rank, then identify their shared column/row spaces, and finally show the core matrices are proportional to unitaries.
Recall the equilibrium conditions in (
B.21
).
From (
B.21
) and the inequalities

\mathrm{rank}(AB)\leq\min\{\mathrm{rank}(A),\mathrm{rank}(B)\}
,
we obtain

\mathrm{rank}\bigl(\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}\bigr)=\mathrm{rank}\bigl(\widehat{\theta^{2}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}\widehat{\theta^{1}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}\bigr)\leq\min\{\mathrm{rank}\bigl(\widehat{\theta^{2}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}\bigr),\mathrm{rank}\bigl(\widehat{\theta^{1}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}\bigr)\},

Applying the same argument cyclically to the first and second identities yields that each rank is bounded by every other. Therefore, all three ranks are equal:

\mathrm{rank}\bigl(\widehat{\theta^{1}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}\bigr)=\mathrm{rank}\bigl(\widehat{\theta^{2}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}\bigr)=\mathrm{rank}\bigl(\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}\bigr)=r_{\rho}.

We now show that the coefficients share consistent column spaces.
From the third identity in (
B.21
),

\mathrm{Im}\bigl(\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}\bigr)\subseteq\mathrm{Im}\bigl(\widehat{\theta^{2}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}\bigr),\qquad\mathrm{Im}\bigl((\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger})^{*}\bigr)\subseteq\mathrm{Im}\bigl((\widehat{\theta^{1}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger})^{*}\bigr),

where
{\rm Im}(\cdot)
denotes the column space.
Since all three matrices share the same rank
r_{\rho}
, the subspaces on both sides have equal dimension, so the inclusions become equalities.
By applying the same logic to the cyclic permutations of (
B.21
), we find that

\mathrm{Im}\bigl(\widehat{\theta^{2}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}\bigr)=\mathrm{Im}\bigl(\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}\bigr),\quad\mathrm{Im}\bigl(\widehat{\theta^{1}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}\bigr)=\mathrm{Im}\bigl((\widehat{\theta^{2}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger})^{*}\bigr),\quad\mathrm{Im}\bigl((\widehat{\theta^{1}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger})^{*}\bigr)=\mathrm{Im}\bigl((\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger})^{*}\bigr).

We define partial isometries
U_{m}[\rho],V_{m}[\rho],W_{m}[\rho]\in\mathbb{C}^{d_{\rho}\times r_{\rho}}
as the orthonormal bases for these three
r_{\rho}
-dimensional subspaces.
Therefore, each equilibrium coefficient admits the factorization

\displaystyle\widehat{\theta^{1}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}=V_{m}[\rho]M_{1,m}

\displaystyle[\rho](W_{m}[\rho])^{*},\qquad\widehat{\theta^{2}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}=U_{m}[\rho]M_{2,m}[\rho](V_{m}[\rho])^{*},

\displaystyle\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}=U_{m}[\rho]M_{3,m}[\rho][\rho](W_{m}[\rho])^{*},

(B.44)

where the core matrices
M_{1,m}[\rho],M_{2,m}[\rho],M_{3,m}[\rho]\in\mathbb{C}^{r_{\rho}\times r_{\rho}}
are invertible, each with rank
r_{\rho}
.
Substituting (
B.44
) into (
B.21
), the equilibrium conditions reduce to

\displaystyle(M_{2,m}[\rho])^{*}M_{3,m}[\rho]=|G|\cdot

\displaystyle\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}\cdot M_{1,m}[\rho],\qquad M_{3,m}[\rho](M_{1,m}[\rho])^{*}=|G|\cdot\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}\cdot M_{2,m}[\rho],

\displaystyle M_{2,m}[\rho]M_{1,m}[\rho]=|G|\cdot\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}\cdot M_{3,m}[\rho].

(B.45)

Eliminating
M_{3,m}[\rho]
by substituting the third equation of (
B.2.8
) into the first, we have

(M_{2,m}[\rho])^{*}M_{2,m}[\rho]M_{1,m}[\rho]=|G|^{2}\cdot(\Omega_{m}^{\dagger})^{2}\cdot M_{1,m}[\rho]~\Rightarrow~(M_{2,m}[\rho])^{*}M_{2,m}[\rho]=|G|^{2}\cdot(\Omega_{m}^{\dagger})^{2}\cdot I_{r_{\rho}},

since
M_{1,m}[\rho]
is invertible.
A symmetric argument gives that
(M_{1,m}[\rho])^{*}M_{1,m}[\rho]=|G|^{2}\cdot(\Omega_{m}^{\dagger})^{2}\cdot I_{r_{\rho}}
.
Therefore, both
M_{1,m}[\rho]
and
M_{2,m}[\rho]
are proportional to unitary matrices:

M_{1,m}[\rho]=|G|\cdot\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}\cdot Q^{1}_{m}[\rho],\quad M_{2,m}[\rho]=|G|\cdot\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}\cdot Q^{2}_{m}[\rho],\quad\text{for some unitary~}Q^{1}_{m}[\rho],Q^{2}_{m}[\rho]\in\mathbb{C}^{r_{\rho}\times r_{\rho}}.

Substituting into the third equation of (
B.2.8
) gives

M_{3,m}[\rho]=|G|\cdot\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}\cdot Q^{2}_{m}[\rho]Q^{1}_{m}[\rho]
.
Finally, we absorb the unitary factors into the bases by defining

\widetilde{W}_{m}[\rho]=W_{m}[\rho](Q^{1}_{m}[\rho])^{*},\qquad\widetilde{U}_{m}[\rho]=U_{m}[\rho]Q^{2}_{m}[\rho].

Since
Q^{1}_{m}[\rho],Q^{2}_{m}[\rho]
are unitary,
\widetilde{U}_{m}[\rho],\widetilde{W}_{m}[\rho]
keep partial isometries. Substituting into (
B.44
) gives

\widehat{\theta^{1}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}=|G|\cdot\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}\cdot V_{m}[\rho](\widetilde{W}_{m}[\rho])^{*},~\widehat{\theta^{2}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}=|G|\cdot\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}\cdot\widetilde{U}_{m}[\rho](V_{m}[\rho])^{*},~\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}=|G|\cdot\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}\cdot\widetilde{U}_{m}[\rho](\widetilde{W}_{m}[\rho])^{*}.

Relabeling the bases completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
B.10
.

The proof proceeds in three steps: first verify the tangent-space condition, then compute the ambient Hessian, and finally obtain the Riemannian Hessian.

Step 1: Tangent-Space Verification.

We start by showing
{\mathcal{T}}_{m}\subseteq T_{\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{\dagger}}{\mathcal{M}}
.
Recall that each tangent direction
\Xi_{m}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{m}
has the form in (
B.24
).
Therefore, for each
\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)^{\sharp}_{\neq 1}
, we have

\displaystyle\Xi_{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho]=V_{m}[\rho]\Sigma_{m}

\displaystyle[\rho](W_{m}[\rho])^{*},\qquad\Xi_{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho]=U_{m}[\rho]\Sigma_{m}[\rho](V_{m}[\rho])^{*},

\displaystyle\Xi_{\xi_{m}}[\rho]=U_{m}[\rho]\Sigma_{m}[\rho](W_{m}[\rho])^{*}.

(B.46)

From Lemma
B.9
, the equilibrium coefficients factorize as

\displaystyle\widehat{\theta^{1}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}=|G|\cdot\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}\cdot

\displaystyle V_{m}[\rho](W_{m}[\rho])^{*},\qquad\widehat{\theta^{2}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}=|G|\cdot\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}\cdot U_{m}[\rho](V_{m}[\rho])^{*}

\displaystyle\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}=|G|\cdot\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}\cdot U_{m}[\rho](W_{m}[\rho])^{*}.

(B.47)

We verify that
{\mathcal{T}}_{m}
belongs to the tangent space by checking the inner product
\langle\Xi_{\nu},\widehat{\nu}^{\dagger}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}=0
for each layer
\nu\in\{\theta_{m}^{1},\theta_{m}^{2},\xi_{m}\}
. For
\nu=\theta_{m}^{1}
, by combining (
B.46
) and (
B.47
), we have

\displaystyle\langle\Xi_{\theta^{1}_{m}},\widehat{\theta^{1}_{m}}^{\dagger}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}=|G|^{2}\cdot\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}\cdot\sum_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)^{\sharp}_{\neq 1}}d_{\rho}^{\sharp}\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma_{m}[\rho])=0.

The same argument applies for directions
\Xi_{\theta^{2}_{m}},\Xi_{\xi_{m}}
.

Step 2: Ambient Hessian.

We substitute the tangent form (
B.46
) and the factorization form (
B.47
) into the ambient Hessian formula from Lemma
B.4
.
We illustrate the computation for the
\theta_{m}^{1}
-block.
By (
B.20a
), (
B.46
) and (
B.47
), the
\theta_{m}^{1}
-block of the ambient Hessian at
\rho
is

\displaystyle{\rm HS}_{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho]

\displaystyle=|G|^{-1}\cdot\{(\Xi_{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho])^{*}\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger}+(\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho]^{\dagger})^{*}\Xi_{\xi_{m}}[\rho]\}=2\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}\cdot\Xi_{\theta_{m}^{1}}[\rho],

where we use the orthonormality
(U_{m}[\rho])^{*}U_{m}[\rho]=I_{r_{\rho}}
.
The same computation applies cyclically to
{\rm HS}_{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho]=2\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}\cdot\Xi_{\theta_{m}^{2}}[\rho]
and
{\rm HS}_{\xi_{m}}[\rho]=2\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}\cdot\Xi_{\xi_{m}}[\rho]
.
Therefore, we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{H}^{3}}\Omega(\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{\dagger})[\Xi_{m}]=({\rm HS}_{\theta_{m}^{1}},{\rm HS}_{\theta_{m}^{2}},{\rm HS}_{\xi_{m}})=2\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}\cdot\Xi_{m}.

(B.48)

Step 3: Riemannian Hessian.

Step 2 shows that
\Xi_{m}
is an eigenvector of the
ambient
Hessian, but we need it to be an eigenvector of the
Riemannian
Hessian on
\mathcal{M}
.
From Lemma
B.4
, the two are related by a projection and a curvature correction:

\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{M}}\Omega(\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{\dagger})[\Xi_{m}]=\Pi_{\widehat{\Theta}_{m}}\big(\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{H}^{3}}\Omega(\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{\dagger})[\Xi_{m}]\big)-\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}\cdot\Xi_{m}.

Moreover, by calculation, we can check that

\displaystyle\langle{\rm HS}_{\theta_{m}^{1}},\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}^{\dagger}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H})}

\displaystyle=2\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}\cdot|G|\cdot\sum_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)^{\sharp}_{\neq 1}}d_{\rho}^{\sharp}\cdot\Re\big(\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\big((\widehat{\theta^{1}_{m}}[\rho]^{\dagger})^{*}V_{m}[\rho]\Sigma_{m}[\rho](W_{m}[\rho])^{*}\big)\big)=0

where we use the trace-zero constraint. The same holds for
\theta_{m}^{2}
and
\xi_{m}
by the cyclic symmetry.
Therefore, we have
\Pi_{\widehat{\Theta}_{m}}({\rm HS})={\rm HS}
. Combining with (
B.48
) yields that

\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{M}}\Omega(\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{\dagger})[\Xi_{m}]=\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{H}^{3}}\Omega(\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{\dagger})[\Xi_{m}]-\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}\cdot\Xi_{m}=\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}\cdot\Xi_{m},\qquad\forall\,\Xi_{m}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{m}.

This identity shows that every tangent vector
\Xi_{m}\in\mathcal{T}_{m}
serves as an eigenvector with eigenvalue
\lambda=\Omega_{m}^{\dagger}>0
.
Then
\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{M}}\Omega(\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{\dagger})
has a positive eigenvalue once
\dim(\mathcal{T}_{m})\geq 1
, that is,
\sum_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)^{\sharp}_{\neq 1}}r_{\rho}\geq 2
.
Otherwise, the tangent condition imposes a
{\mathcal{T}}_{m}=\{0\}
, which completes the proof.
∎

B.2.9
Proof of Theorem
B.11
: Saddle Avoidance

To prove Theorem
B.11
, we apply the center-stable manifold theorem from the dynamical systems theory directly to the Riemannian gradient flow
(
32
)
.
Consistent with the previous section, we consider the Riemannian gradient flow defined by

\partial_{t}x_{t}=\mathrm{grad}_{\mathcal{M}}{\mathcal{F}}(x_{t}),

Note that while standard literature often uses a negative sign, our formulation is easily reconciled by replacing
{\mathcal{F}}
with
-{\mathcal{F}}
and inverting the signs of the corresponding results in this section.
In the following, we establish the regularity of the gradient flow mapping under standard smoothness conditions, showing that it constitutes a diffeomorphism.

Lemma B.12
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a compact Riemannian manifold, and let

{\mathcal{F}}\in C^{r}(\mathcal{M})
,
r\geq 2
. Let
\phi_{t}:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}
be the flow mapping of the Riemannian gradient
flow of
{\mathcal{F}}
on
\mathcal{M}
. Then the flow is defined for all

t\in\mathbb{R}
and all
x\in\mathcal{M}
. Moreover, for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
\phi_{t}
is a diffeomorphism, i.e.,
\phi_{t}
is bijective and smooth.

Proof of Lemma
B.12
.

Since
{\mathcal{F}}\in C^{r}(\mathcal{M})
with
r\geq 2
and the Riemannian metric is smooth, the vector field
\mathrm{grad}_{\mathcal{M}}{\mathcal{F}}
is at least
C^{1}
on
\mathcal{M}
.
Since every
C^{1}
vector field on a compact manifold is complete, i.e. there exists a unique integral curve
\gamma_{x}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathcal{M}
satisfying
\partial_{t}\gamma_{x}(t)=\mathrm{grad}_{\mathcal{M}}{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{x}(t))
with the initialization
\gamma_{x}(0)=x
, the flow mapping
\phi_{t}
is defined for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
and every
x\in\mathcal{M}
.
To see that
\phi_{t}
is a diffeomorphism, note that the uniqueness of solutions, the flow satisfies
\phi_{t}\circ\phi_{s}=\phi_{t+s}
for all
s,t\in\mathbb{R}
.
In particular, we have

\phi_{t}\circ\phi_{-t}=\phi_{-t}\circ\phi_{t}=\phi_{0}={\mathrm{Id}}_{\mathcal{M}},

which establishes that
\phi_{t}
is bijective with a well-defined inverse
\phi_{-t}
.
Finally, since
\mathrm{grad}_{\mathcal{M}}{\mathcal{F}}
is a
C^{1}
vector field, the flow mapping depends
C^{1}
-smoothly on the initial condition.
Thus both
\phi_{t}
and its inverse
\phi_{-t}
are
C^{1}
maps.
Hence,
\phi_{t}
is a diffeomorphism of
\mathcal{M}
, which completes the proof.
∎

Building upon this, we are now ready to establish the center-stable manifold theorem for the Riemannian gradient flow.

Theorem B.13
.

Let
p\in{\mathcal{M}}
be a critical point of
{\mathcal{F}}\in C^{r}
with
r\geq 2
, and let
\phi_{t}
be the Riemannian gradient flow of
{\mathcal{F}}
on
\mathcal{M}
satisfying the regularity conditions of Lemma
B.12
.
Let
T_{p}{\mathcal{M}}=E_{p}^{\sf sc}\oplus E_{p}^{\sf u}
where
E_{p}^{\sf sc}
and
E_{p}^{\sf u}
are the spans of the eigenvectors of
\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{M}}{\mathcal{F}}(p)
corresponding to non-positive and strictly positive eigenvalues.
Then, there exists a neighborhood
U_{p}
of
p
and a
C^{r-1}
embedded submanifold
W^{\sf sc}_{\rm loc}(p)
tangent to
E_{p}^{\sf sc}
at
p
, called the local center-stable manifold, such that

(i)

\phi_{t}(W^{\sf sc}_{\rm loc}(p))\cap U_{p}\subset W^{\sf sc}_{\rm loc}(p)
for all time
t\geq 0
,

(ii)

If the forward orbit of
x
remains in
U_{p}
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, i.e.,
\phi_{t}(x)\in U_{p}
for all
t\geq 0
, then
x\in W^{\sf sc}_{\rm loc}(p)
.

Proof of Theorem
B.13
.

By Lemma
B.12
, the flow
\phi_{t}
is a diffeomorphism along all time
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
After this,
32
has the flow
invariant analogues for vector fields as stated above, the proof follows as suggested by
32
.
∎

Theorem
B.13
describes the local forward-time dynamics of the Riemannian gradient flow near a critical point
p
.
It shows that the non-positive eigenspace of
\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{M}}{\mathcal{F}}(p)
integrates to a
C^{r-1}
local center-stable manifold
W^{\sf sc}_{\rm loc}(p)
, and that every point whose forward orbit remains in a sufficiently small neighborhood of
p
must lie on this manifold.
The local invariance property in (i) means that, under forward flow, the points in
W^{\sf sc}_{\rm loc}(p)
remain in this manifold as long as the trajectory stays inside
U_{p}
.
Conversely, (ii) implies that any point whose forward orbit stays in
U_{p}
for all future time must lie on
W^{\sf sc}_{\rm loc}(p)
. Thus, if a trajectory enters
U_{p}
and then remains in
U_{p}
thereafter, it must lie in the local center-stable manifold from then on.

Local Flow Organization via Hessian Splitting.

We provide an intuitive interpretation of the theorem statement.
Consider the Euclidean gradient flow
\partial_{t}x_{t}=\nabla{\mathcal{F}}(x_{t})
.
Let
p
be a nondegenerate critical point.
Taylor expanding the vector field near
p
yields

\partial_{t}(x_{t}-p)=\nabla{\mathcal{F}}(x_{t})\approx\nabla{\mathcal{F}}(p)+\nabla^{2}{\mathcal{F}}(p)\cdot(x_{t}-p)=\nabla^{2}{\mathcal{F}}(p)\cdot(x_{t}-p),

(B.49)

where the last equation uses
\nabla{\mathcal{F}}(p)=0
.
To first order, the nonlinear flow is approximated by the linear system in (
B.49
).
Analogously, for the Riemannian gradient flow, working in the tangent space, the linearized dynamic
\Xi_{t}\in T_{p}{\mathcal{M}}
is governed by

\partial_{t}\Xi_{t}=\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{M}}{\mathcal{F}}(p)[\Xi_{t}]
.
Specifically, the stable subspace
E_{p}^{\sf sc}
, spanned by eigenvectors corresponding to nonnegative eigenvalues of
\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{M}}{\mathcal{F}}(p)
is the directions where the flow is attracted or not repelled towards
p
.
Conversely, the unstable subspace
E_{p}^{\sf u}
associated with positive eigenvalues, represents directions along which the flow repels from
p
.

Comparison with
(
32
, Theorem III.7)
.

Theorem
B.13
is the center-stable part of

(
32
, Theorem III.7)
, adapted to the Riemannian gradient flow.
The theorem in
32
is formulated for
a local
C^{r}
diffeomorphism
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
, which defines a discrete-time system governed by the iterations of
f
.
Its linearization at a fixed point is given by the linear map
Df(0)
.
In that discrete-time framework, the center-stable subspace is the invariant subspace of
Df(0)
corresponding to eigenvalues of magnitude at most
1
, while the unstable subspace corresponds to eigenvalues of magnitude strictly greater than
1
.
By contrast, Theorem
B.13
concerns a continuous-time flow
\phi_{t}
generated by the Riemannian gradient vector field
\mathrm{grad}_{\mathcal{M}}{\mathcal{F}}
.
Thus, the analogue of the discrete orbit
\{f^{k}(x_{0})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is the continuous trajectory
t\mapsto\phi_{t}(x_{0})
.
To reconcile the two formulations, one can fix a small enough time
t>0
and consider the time-
t
map
f=\phi_{t}
.
The solution of the linearized ODE at
p
within a small time period
t
gives an exact matrix exponential mapping, namely

Df(p)=D\phi_{t}(p)\approx\exp\bigl(t\,\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{M}}{\mathcal{F}}(p)\bigr).

This identity clarifies why eigenvalues of
Df(0)
with magnitude greater than
1
in the discrete-time setting correspond to the strictly positive eigenvalues of
\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{M}}{\mathcal{F}}(p)
in our gradient flow setting.

Proof of Theorem
B.11
.

By Lemma
B.12
, the flow
\phi_{t}
is a diffeomorphism along all time
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
Consider a strict saddle point
p\in{\rm Sad}({\mathcal{F}})
, where by definition,
\mathrm{Hess}_{{\mathcal{M}}}{\mathcal{F}}(p)
possesses at least one positive eigenvalue.
Theorem
B.13
gives a linear subspace
E^{\sf sc}_{p}\subsetneq T_{p}{\mathcal{M}}
, a local center-stable manifold
W^{\sf sc}_{\rm loc}(p)
and a related open neighborhood
U_{p}
, satisfying

\dim(W^{\sf sc}_{\rm loc}(p))=\dim(E^{\sf sc}_{p})<\dim({\mathcal{M}}).

The first equality results from
W^{\sf sc}_{\rm loc}(p)
is an embedded disk tangent to
E_{p}^{\sf sc}
at
p
and the inequality follows from the existence of a non-empty unstable subspace
E_{p}^{\sf u}
, i.e., the eigenspace corresponding to positive eigenvalues, which is guaranteed because
p
is a strict saddle.
Thus, the manifold has zero volume measure on
{\mathcal{M}}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle{\rm vol}_{\mathcal{M}}(W^{\sf sc}_{\rm loc}(p))=0,\qquad\forall p\in{\rm Sad}({\mathcal{F}}).

(B.50)

Furthermore, since
\mathcal{M}
is complete separable, and
\{U_{p}\}_{p\in{\rm Sad}({\mathcal{F}})}
is an open cover of
{\rm Sad}({\mathcal{F}})
, we can extract a countable subcover indexed by
\{p_{j}\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
yielding

{\rm Sad}({\mathcal{F}})\subseteq\bigcup_{p\in{\rm Sad}({\mathcal{F}})}U_{p}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}U_{p_{i}}.

Recall that we define the global stable set as

W^{\sf s}=\{x\in{\mathcal{M}}:\exists\,p\in{\rm Sad}({\mathcal{F}}),\;\phi_{t}(x)\to p\text{\rm~as~}t\to\infty\}.

By definition, for any
w\in W^{\sf s}
, the trajectory converges to a specific saddle point, i.e.,
\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\phi_{t}(w)=p^{*}\in{\rm Sad}({\mathcal{F}})
.
Since
p^{*}
must belong to at least one neighborhood in our countable subcover, there exists an index
j\in\mathbb{N}
such that
p^{*}\in U_{p_{j}}

Hence, there exists a sufficiently large integer time
N\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0}
such that
\phi_{t}(w)\in U_{p_{j}}
for all
t\geq N
.
Applying (ii) of Theorem
B.13
to the shifted trajectory yields

\displaystyle\phi_{N}(w)\in U_{p_{j}},\quad\phi_{t}(\phi_{N}(w))\in U_{p_{j}}\text{~for all~}t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\phi_{N}(w)\in W_{\rm loc}^{\sf sc}(p_{j}).

(B.51)

Since
\phi_{t}
is bijective, we can apply the inverse mapping
\phi_{-N}
to both sides, which implies
w\in\phi_{-N}(W^{\sf sc}_{\mathrm{loc}}(p_{j}))
.
Therefore, we can bound the global stable set by the countable union

W^{\sf s}\subset\bigcup_{j=1}^{\infty}\bigcup_{N=0}^{\infty}\phi_{-N}\bigl(W^{\sf sc}_{\mathrm{loc}}(p_{j})\bigr).

Since
\phi_{-N}
is a diffeomorphism of
{\mathcal{M}}
, each set
\phi_{-N}(W^{\sf sc}_{\mathrm{loc}}(p_{j}))
is again an embedded
C^{r-1}
submanifold of dimension
\dim(E_{p_{j}}^{\sf sc})
.
Combining (
B.50
) and (
B.51
) gives that

{\rm vol}_{\mathcal{M}}(W^{\sf s})\leq{\rm vol}_{\mathcal{M}}\left(\bigcup_{j=1}^{\infty}\bigcup_{N=0}^{\infty}\phi_{-N}(W^{\sf sc}_{\mathrm{loc}}(p_{j}))\right)\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}{\rm vol}_{\mathcal{M}}\big(\phi_{-N}(W^{\sf sc}_{\mathrm{loc}}(p_{j}))\big)=0.

Recall that
X_{0}
is randomly initialized whose law is absolutely continuous with respect to the Riemannian volume measure.
Since
W^{\sf s}
has zero volume,
\mathbb{P}(X_{0}\in W^{\sf s})=0
.
Hence, with probability
1
, the gradient flow does not converge to
any saddle point, which completes the proof.
∎

B.3
Proof of Theorem
4.5
: Growth Rate of Scaling Factor in Stage II

Proof of Theorem
4.5
.

The proof proceeds in three steps.
First, we derive the scalar gradient flow for
a(t)
in terms of the logit margin.
Second, we show that the finite-width predictor
{f}_{\widehat{\mu}}
inherits the population margin, yielding a positive lower bound on
\partial_{t}a
and hence logarithmic growth of
a(t)
.
Third, we translate this scale growth into an upper bound on the cross-entropy loss.

Based on (

μ

-PA
), the population predictor
{f}_{\mu}
assigns the highest logit to the correct label for every input pair.
Since
G
is finite, this strict inequality implies a positive
logit margin
:

\Delta{f}_{\mu}:=\min_{g_{1},g_{2}\in G}\big\{{f}_{\mu}(g_{1},g_{2})_{g_{1}\star g_{2}}-\max_{j\in G\backslash\{g_{1}\star g_{2}\}}{f}_{\mu}(g_{1},g_{2})_{j}\big\}>0.

Step 1: Scalar Gradient Flow.

Under the tied constraint
a_{j}=a
, the network output factorizes as
f(g_{1},g_{2};a)=a\cdot{f}_{\widehat{\mu}}(g_{1},g_{2})
.
Differentiating the CE loss (
2.2
) with respect to
a
gives

\displaystyle\partial_{t}a=-\nabla_{a}{\mathscr{R}}(a)=\sum_{g_{1},g_{2}\in G}{f}_{\widehat{\mu}}(g_{1},g_{2})_{g_{1}\star g_{2}}-\sum_{g_{1},g_{2}\in G}\big\langle{p}_{g_{1}g_{2}},\,{f}_{\widehat{\mu}}(g_{1},g_{2})\big\rangle,

(B.52)

where
({p}_{g_{1}g_{2}})_{\ell}:={\mathrm{smax}}(a\cdot{f}_{\widehat{\mu}}(g_{1},g_{2}))_{\ell}
is the softmax probability.
Rearranging (
B.52
) yields

\displaystyle\partial_{t}a

\displaystyle=\sum_{g_{1},g_{2}\in G}\sum_{\ell\neq g_{1}\star g_{2}}({p}_{g_{1}g_{2}})_{\ell}\cdot\big\{{f}_{\widehat{\mu}}(g_{1},g_{2})_{g_{1}\star g_{2}}-{f}_{\widehat{\mu}}(g_{1},g_{2})_{\ell}\big\}.

(B.53)

This expression shows that
\partial_{t}a
is a weighted sum of logit gaps between the correct label and each incorrect label, with weights given by the softmax probabilities on incorrect labels.

Step 2: Logit Margin and Logarithmic Scale Growth.

To control the logit gaps in (
B.53
), we decompose each into its population value plus a finite-width approximation error:

\displaystyle{f}_{\widehat{\mu}}(g_{1},g_{2})_{g_{1}\star g_{2}}-{f}_{\widehat{\mu}}(g_{1},g_{2})_{\ell}

\displaystyle\qquad=\big\{{f}_{\mu}(g_{1},g_{2})_{g_{1}\star g_{2}}-{f}_{\mu}(g_{1},g_{2})_{\ell}\big\}+\big\{({f}_{\widehat{\mu}}-{f}_{\mu})(g_{1},g_{2})_{g_{1}\star g_{2}}-({f}_{\widehat{\mu}}-{f}_{\mu})(g_{1},g_{2})_{\ell}\big\}

\displaystyle\qquad\geq\Delta{f}_{\mu}-2\|{f}_{\widehat{\mu}}-{f}_{\mu}\|_{\infty,\infty}.

(B.54)

We now bound the approximation error
\|{f}_{\widehat{\mu}}-{f}_{\mu}\|_{\infty,\infty}
.
Let
\zeta_{m\ell}:=(\xi_{m})_{\ell}\cdot\sigma\big(\langle\theta_{m}^{1},e_{g_{1}}\rangle+\langle\theta_{m}^{2},e_{g_{2}}\rangle\big)
denote the logit contribution of the
m
-th neuron.
Since parameters lie on the unit sphere, we have

|\zeta_{m\ell}|\leq\|\xi_{m}\|_{2}\cdot(\|\theta_{m}^{1}\|_{2}+\|\theta_{m}^{2}\|_{2})^{2}\leq 4
.
By applying the Hoeffding’s inequality
(
14
)
to
{f}_{\widehat{\mu}}(g_{1},g_{2})_{\ell}=M^{-1}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\zeta_{m\ell}
and taking a union bound, with probability at least
1-\delta
, we have

\displaystyle\|{f}_{\widehat{\mu}}-{f}_{\mu}\|_{\infty,\infty}\leq\sqrt{\frac{32}{M}\cdot\log\left(\frac{2|G|^{3}}{\delta}\right)}.

(B.55)

Taking
M\geq 512\,\Delta{f}_{\mu}^{-2}\cdot\log(2|G|^{3}/\delta)
in (
B.55
) ensures
\|{f}_{\widehat{\mu}}-{f}_{\mu}\|_{\infty,\infty}\leq\Delta{f}_{\mu}/8
, so that (
B.54
) gives

\displaystyle{f}_{\widehat{\mu}}(g_{1},g_{2})_{g_{1}\star g_{2}}-{f}_{\widehat{\mu}}(g_{1},g_{2})_{\ell}\geq\Delta{f}_{\mu}/4,\qquad\forall(g_{1},g_{2})\in G^{2},\;\ell\neq g_{1}\star g_{2}.

(B.56)

It remains to lower bound the softmax weight on incorrect labels.
Note that

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\neq g_{1}\star g_{2}}({p}_{g_{1}g_{2}})_{\ell}

\displaystyle=1-({p}_{g_{1}g_{2}})_{g_{1}\star g_{2}}=1-\frac{\exp\big(a\cdot{f}_{\widehat{\mu}}(g_{1},g_{2})_{g_{1}\star g_{2}}\big)}{\sum_{j\in G}\exp\big(a\cdot{f}_{\widehat{\mu}}(g_{1},g_{2})_{j}\big)}

\displaystyle=1-\bigg(1+\sum_{j\neq g_{1}\star g_{2}}\exp\Big(a\cdot\big\{{f}_{\widehat{\mu}}(g_{1},g_{2})_{j}-{f}_{\widehat{\mu}}(g_{1},g_{2})_{g_{1}\star g_{2}}\big\}\Big)\bigg)^{-1}.

Combining with (
B.53
) and (
B.56
), we obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{t}a\geq\frac{\Delta{f}_{\mu}}{4}\cdot|G|^{2}\cdot\frac{(|G|-1)\cdot\exp(-8a)}{1+(|G|-1)\cdot\exp(-8a)}\geq\frac{\Delta{f}_{\mu}}{4}\cdot|G|\cdot(|G|-1)\cdot\exp(-8a).

Integration with respect to
t
yields

\displaystyle a(t)\geq\tfrac{1}{8}\log\big(\exp(8a(0))+2\Delta{f}_{\mu}\cdot|G|\cdot(|G|-1)\cdot t\big)\gtrsim\log(1+|G|\cdot(|G|-1)\cdot t).

(B.57)

This establishes part
(i)
of the theorem.

Step 3: Loss Convergence.

Using the empirical margin (
B.56
), the cross-entropy loss satisfies

\displaystyle{\mathscr{R}}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{g_{1},g_{2}\in G}\log\Big(1+\sum_{\ell\neq g_{1}\star g_{2}}\exp\big(a(t)\cdot\{{f}_{\widehat{\mu}}(g_{1},g_{2})_{\ell}-{f}_{\widehat{\mu}}(g_{1},g_{2})_{g_{1}\star g_{2}}\}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq|G|^{2}\cdot\log\big(1+(|G|-1)\cdot\exp(-a(t)\cdot\Delta{f}_{\mu}/4)\big)

\displaystyle\leq|G|^{2}\cdot(|G|-1)\cdot\exp\big(-a(t)\cdot\Delta{f}_{\mu}/4\big),

where the last step uses
\log(1+x)\leq x
for
x\geq 0
.
To achieve
{\mathscr{R}}(T)\leq\epsilon
, it suffices that
a(T)\gtrsim\log(|G|^{2}\cdot(|G|-1)/\epsilon)
.
Combining with (
B.57
), this is satisfied when

T\gtrsim|G|/\epsilon\cdot(1+(|G|-1)^{-2}),

which establishes part
(ii)
and completes the proof.
∎

Appendix C
Proof of Results for Abelian Group in §
5

In this appendix, we specialize the general analysis of §
B
to finite Abelian groups.
We begin in §
C.2
by deriving a magnitude-phase decomposition of the Fourier-domain ODE (see Lemmas
C.1
and
C.2
), which simplifies the system.
This decomposition facilitates the proof of Theorem
5.1
, which we establish by showing that the limiting distribution is the pushforward of a uniform product measure on
{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}\times\mathbb{D}
(see Lemma
C.3
) and verifying that this population measure achieves perfect accuracy (see Lemma
C.4
).
Finally, in §
C.3
, we prove Theorem
5.3
by analyzing the respective convergence rates of phase alignment and representation competition.

C.1
Spectral Dynamics for Abelian Groups

Recall from §
5
that we adopt a shared input embedding
\theta_{m}^{1}=\theta_{m}^{2}=:\theta_{m}
, which is natural since the group operation is commutative.
Under the shared embedding, we have the following dynamics:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\widehat{\theta}_{m}[\rho]

\displaystyle=\frac{2a|G|}{M}\cdot\overline{\widehat{\theta}_{m}[\rho]}\cdot\widehat{\xi}_{m}[\rho]-\frac{2a|G|^{2}}{M}\cdot\Omega_{m}\cdot\widehat{\theta}_{m}[\rho],

(C.1a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\widehat{\xi}_{m}[\rho]

\displaystyle=\frac{2a|G|}{M}\cdot\widehat{\theta}_{m}[\rho]^{2}-\frac{2a|G|^{2}}{M}\cdot\Omega_{m}\cdot\widehat{\xi}_{m}[\rho],

(C.1b)

where the energy functional simplifies to

\Omega_{m}=\sum_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}}\overline{\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]}\cdot\widehat{\theta_{m}}[\rho]^{2}=\sum_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}}\Re\big(\overline{\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]}\cdot\widehat{\theta_{m}}[\rho]^{2}\big).

(C.2)

Since the Fourier coefficients are complex scalars, it is natural to decompose each into its magnitude and phase.
For each
\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)
and
\nu\in\{\theta,\xi\}
, we define

\alpha_{\nu,m}[\rho](t)=|\widehat{\nu}_{m}[\rho](t)|\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},\qquad\phi_{\nu,m}[\rho](t)=\widehat{\nu}_{m}[\rho](t)/|\widehat{\nu}_{m}[\rho](t)|\in\mathbb{D},

and thus
\widehat{\nu}_{m}[\rho]=\alpha_{\nu,m}[\rho]\cdot\phi_{\nu,m}[\rho]
.
This polar decomposition separates the dynamics into magnitude evolution, i.e., how much energy each representation carries, and phase evolution, i.e., the complex direction of each Fourier coefficient, which we make precise in the following lemma.

Lemma C.1
.

The complex-valued dynamics in (
C.1a
)–(
C.1b
) are equivalent to the following magnitude–phase decomposition parameterized by magnitudes
\alpha_{\nu,m}[\rho]\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and phases
\phi_{\nu,m}[\rho]\in\mathbb{D}
.
Let

\varphi_{m}[\rho]=\overline{\phi_{\xi,m}[\rho]}\cdot\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho]^{2}\in\mathbb{D},

represent the relative phase alignment.
For all
\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}
, the evolution of the magnitudes is governed by:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho]

\displaystyle=\frac{2a|G|}{M}\cdot\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho]\cdot\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho]\cdot\Re\big(\varphi_{m}[\rho]\big)-\frac{2a|G|^{2}}{M}\cdot\Omega_{m}\cdot\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho],

(C.3a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho]

\displaystyle=\frac{2a|G|}{M}\cdot\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho]^{2}\cdot\Re\big(\varphi_{m}[\rho]\big)-\frac{2a|G|^{2}}{M}\cdot\Omega_{m}\cdot\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho].

(C.3b)

The evolution of the phases is given by

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho]

\displaystyle=-\frac{2a|G|}{M}\cdot\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho]\cdot\Im\big(\varphi_{m}[\rho]\big)\cdot\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho]\cdot{i},

(C.4a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\phi_{\xi,m}[\rho]

\displaystyle=\frac{2a|G|}{M}\cdot\frac{\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho]^{2}}{\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho]}\cdot\Im\big(\varphi_{m}[\rho]\big)\cdot\phi_{\xi,m}[\rho]\cdot{i}.

(C.4b)

Lemma
C.1
reveals a key structural property: the magnitudes (
C.3a
)–(
C.3b
) depend on the phases only through
\Re(\varphi_{m}[\rho])
, and the phases (
C.4a
)–(
C.4b
) depend on phases through
\Im(\varphi_{m}[\rho])
.
In both cases, the individual phases
\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho]
and
\phi_{\xi,m}[\rho]
enter only via the
relative phase alignment

\varphi_{m}[\rho]
.
This suggests that the system can be reduced to the variables
(\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho],\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho],\varphi_{m}[\rho])
without tracking the individual phases, which we confirm in Lemma
C.2
below.

Proof of Lemma
C.1
.

We derive the magnitude and phase equations separately.

Magnitude Equations.

To obtain the evolution of
\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho]
, we differentiate
\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho]^{2}=|\widehat{\theta}_{m}[\rho]|^{2}
using the chain rule and (
C.1a
):

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho]^{2}=\partial_{t}|\widehat{\theta_{m}}[\rho]|^{2}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\theta_{m}}[\rho]\cdot\partial_{t}\overline{\widehat{\theta_{m}}[\rho]}+\overline{\widehat{\theta_{m}}[\rho]}\cdot\partial_{t}\widehat{\theta_{m}}[\rho]

\displaystyle=\frac{2a|G|}{M}\cdot(\overline{\widehat{\theta_{m}}[\rho]}^{2}\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]+\widehat{\theta_{m}}[\rho]^{2}\overline{\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]})-\frac{4a|G|^{2}}{M}\cdot\Omega_{m}\cdot|\widehat{\theta_{m}}[\rho]|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{4a|G|}{M}\cdot\alpha_{\theta,m}^{2}[\rho]\cdot\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho]\cdot\Re\big(\varphi_{m}[\rho]\big)-\frac{4a|G|^{2}}{M}\cdot\Omega_{m}\cdot\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho]^{2}.

By dividing both sides by
2\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho]
, we obtain the desired result in (
C.3a
).
Similarly, based on (
C.1b
) and chain rule, we obtain that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho]^{2}=\partial_{t}|\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]|^{2}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]\cdot\partial_{t}\overline{\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]}+\overline{\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]}\cdot\partial_{t}\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]

\displaystyle=\frac{4a|G|}{M}\cdot\alpha_{\theta,m}^{2}[\rho]\cdot\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho]\cdot\Re\big(\varphi_{m}[\rho]\big)-\frac{4a|G|^{2}}{M}\cdot\Omega_{m}\cdot\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho]^{2},

which results in (
C.3b
). This concludes the derivations of the magnitude equations.

Phase Equations.

For the phase evolution, we use the product rule

\partial_{t}\widehat{\theta}_{m}[\rho]=(\partial_{t}\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho])\cdot\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho]+\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho]\cdot(\partial_{t}\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho])

and compare with (
C.1a
) to isolate
\partial_{t}\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho]
. Specifically, note that we have

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\widehat{\theta_{m}}[\rho]

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho]\cdot\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho]+\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho]\cdot\partial_{t}\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho]

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{eq:mag_theta_dyn}}{=}\frac{2a|G|}{M}\cdot\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho]\cdot\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho]\cdot\Re\big(\varphi_{m}[\rho]\big)\cdot\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho]-\frac{2a|G|^{2}}{M}\cdot\Omega_{m}\cdot\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho]\cdot\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho]

\displaystyle\qquad+\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho]\cdot\partial_{t}\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho],

(C.5)

On the other hand, we can obtain a second expression for
\partial_{t}\widehat{\theta}_{m}[\rho]
by substituting the polar forms
\widehat{\theta}_{m}[\rho]=\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho]\cdot\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho]
and
\widehat{\xi}_{m}[\rho]=\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho]\cdot\phi_{\xi,m}[\rho]
directly into the original ODE (
C.1a
):

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\widehat{\theta_{m}}[\rho]

\displaystyle=\frac{2a|G|}{M}\cdot\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho]\cdot\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho]\cdot\overline{\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho]}\cdot\phi_{\xi,m}[\rho]-\frac{2a|G|^{2}}{M}\cdot\Omega_{m}\cdot\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho]\cdot\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho].

(C.6)

Equating (
C.5
) and (
C.6
) and solving for
\partial_{t}\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho]
, the normalization terms
\Omega_{m}\cdot\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho]\cdot\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho]
cancel on both sides, and only the growth terms differ:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho]

\displaystyle=\frac{2a|G|}{M}\cdot\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho]\cdot\left(\overline{\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho]}\cdot\phi_{\xi,m}[\rho]-\Re\big(\varphi_{m}[\rho]\big)\cdot\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho]\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{2a|G|}{M}\cdot\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho]\cdot\left(\overline{\varphi_{m}[\rho]}-\Re\big(\varphi_{m}[\rho]\big)\right)\cdot\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho]

\displaystyle=-\frac{2a|G|}{M}\cdot\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho]\cdot\Im\big(\varphi_{m}[\rho]\big)\cdot\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho]\cdot{i},

where the second equality factors out
\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho]
using
\overline{\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho]}\cdot\phi_{\xi,m}[\rho]=\overline{\varphi_{m}[\rho]}\cdot\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho]
, and the last step uses the identity
\overline{z}-\Re(z)=-{i}\Im(z)
for any
z\in\mathbb{C}
.
The same strategy applies to
\phi_{\xi,m}[\rho]
.
By the product rule and (
C.3b
), we get a first expression:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]

\displaystyle=\frac{2a|G|}{M}\cdot\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho]^{2}\cdot\Re\big(\varphi_{m}[\rho]\big)\cdot\phi_{\xi,m}[\rho]-\frac{2a|G|^{2}}{M}\cdot\Omega_{m}\cdot\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho]\cdot\phi_{\xi,m}[\rho]+\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho]\cdot\partial_{t}\phi_{\xi,m}[\rho].

Substituting the polar forms into (
C.1b
) gives a second expression:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]=\frac{2a|G|}{M}\cdot\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho]^{2}\cdot\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho]^{2}-\frac{2a|G|^{2}}{M}\cdot\Omega_{m}\cdot\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho]\cdot\phi_{\xi,m}[\rho].

Following the same proof strategy, we have

\partial_{t}\phi_{\xi,m}[\rho]=\frac{2a|G|}{M}\cdot\frac{\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho]^{2}}{\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho]}\cdot\Im\big(\varphi_{m}[\rho]\big)\cdot\phi_{\xi,m}[\rho]\cdot{i},

which completes the proof of this lemma.
∎

The next lemma confirms this by showing that
\varphi_{m}[\rho]
satisfies a closed-form ODE that depends only on
\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho],\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho]
and
\varphi_{m}[\rho]
itself.
In other words, the reduced system
(\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho],\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho],\varphi_{m}[\rho])
is closed, and one need not track the individual phases separately.

Lemma C.2
.

The relative phase alignment
\varphi_{m}[\rho]=\overline{\phi_{\xi,m}[\rho]}\cdot\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho]^{2}\in\mathbb{D}
satisfies the ODE:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\varphi_{m}[\rho]=-\frac{2a|G|}{M}\cdot\left(2\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho]+\frac{\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho]^{2}}{\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho]}\right)\cdot\Im\big(\varphi_{m}[\rho]\big)\cdot\varphi_{m}[\rho]\cdot{i}.

(C.7)

Proof of Lemma
C.2
.

Since
\varphi_{m}[\rho]=\overline{\phi_{\xi,m}[\rho]}\cdot\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho]^{2}
, the product rule gives

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\varphi_{m}[\rho]

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}\overline{\phi_{\xi,m}[\rho]}\cdot\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho]^{2}+\overline{\phi_{\xi,m}[\rho]}\cdot 2\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho]\cdot\partial_{t}\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho].

We substitute the phase equations from Lemma
C.1
.
For the first term, conjugating (
C.4b
) gives

\partial_{t}\overline{\phi_{\xi,m}[\rho]}=-\frac{2a|G|}{M}\cdot\frac{\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho]^{2}}{\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho]}\cdot\Im\big(\varphi_{m}[\rho]\big)\cdot\overline{\phi_{\xi,m}[\rho]}\cdot{i}.

Thus, by direct calculation, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\overline{\phi_{\xi,m}[\rho]}\cdot\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho]^{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2a|G|}{M}\cdot\frac{\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho]^{2}}{\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho]}\cdot\Im\big(\varphi_{m}[\rho]\big)\cdot\overline{\phi_{\xi,m}[\rho]}\cdot\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho]^{2}\cdot{i}.

For the second term, substituting (
C.4a
) gives

\displaystyle\overline{\phi_{\xi,m}[\rho]}\cdot 2\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho]\cdot\partial_{t}\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho]

\displaystyle=-\frac{2a|G|}{M}\cdot 2\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho]\cdot\Im\big(\varphi_{m}[\rho]\big)\cdot\overline{\phi_{\xi,m}[\rho]}\cdot\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho]^{2}\cdot{i}.

Adding the two contributions, both terms share the common factor
\Im\big(\varphi_{m}[\rho]\big)\cdot\varphi_{m}[\rho]\cdot{i}
, and the scalar prefactors combine to give

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\varphi_{m}[\rho]

\displaystyle=-\frac{2a|G|}{M}\cdot\left(\frac{\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho]^{2}}{\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho]}+2\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho]\right)\cdot\Im\big(\varphi_{m}[\rho]\big)\cdot\varphi_{m}[\rho]\cdot{i}.

Therefore, we conclude the proof of this lemma.
∎

C.2
Proof of Theorem
5.1
: Verification of (

μ

-PA
) for Abelian Group

The proof proceeds in two steps, corresponding to two intermediate lemmas.
The first step identifies the joint distribution of
(\check{\rho}_{m},u_{m})
in the Fourier space.
By exploiting the permutation inariance of the ODE across irreps and the rotational invariance of the phase dynamics, we show that the limiting measure
\mu
is the pushforward of a product measure on the representation space.

Lemma C.3
.

Let
\pi:=\pi_{\rho}\otimes\pi_{u}={\rm Unif}({\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1})\otimes{\rm Haar}(\mathbb{D})
.
Then the of parameters within the Euclidean space, denoted by
\mu
, is the push-forward of
\pi
under the inverse Fourier map
{\mathscr{T}_{\sf idft}}:{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}\times\mathbb{D}\to(\mathbb{S}^{|G|-1})^{\otimes 2}
:

\mu={\mathscr{T}_{\sf idft}}_{\#}\pi,\qquad\text{where~~}{\mathscr{T}_{\sf idft}}:(\check{\rho},u)\mapsto\sqrt{2/|G|}\cdot\big(\Re(u\check{\rho}(\cdot)),\Re(u^{2}\check{\rho}(\cdot))\big).

Proof of Lemma
C.3
.

Please refer to §
C.2.1
for a detailed proof.
∎

The second step verifies that
\mu
achieves perfect accuracy.
The next lemma shows that the actual mean-field predictor is a
flawed indicator
: the correct label
j=g_{1}\star g_{2}
receives the largest coefficient, but two “ghost” labels
g_{1}^{2}
and
g_{2}^{2}
also receive nonzero weight due to the structural limit.

Lemma C.4
.

The limiting distribution
\mu
in Lemma
C.3
satisfies (

μ

-PA
) with output logit

{f}_{\sf NN}^{\mu}(g_{1},g_{2})_{j}\;\propto\;2\cdot\ind(j=g_{1}\star g_{2})+\ind(j=g_{1}^{2})+\ind(j=g_{2}^{2})-4/|G|,

for all input pairs
(g_{1},g_{2})\in G^{2}
and labels
j\in G
.
In particular, the correct label receives coefficient
2
, the ghost labels
g_{1}^{2}
and
g_{2}^{2}
each receive coefficient
1
, and all remaining labels sit at the baseline
-4/|G|
.

Proof of Lemma
C.4
.

Please refer to §
C.2.2
for a detailed proof.
∎

With both lemmas in hand, Theorem
5.1
follows immediately.

Proof of Theorem
5.1
.

Lemma
C.3
establishes that
\mu={\mathscr{T}_{\sf idft}}_{\#}\pi
is the limiting distribution of parameters, and Lemma
C.4
verifies that this
\mu
satisfies the perfect-accuracy condition (

μ

-PA
).
∎

C.2.1
Proof of Lemma
C.3
: Limiting Distribution

Proof of Lemma
C.3
.

We adapt the proof of Theorem
4.3
to the shared-embedding Abelian setting.
In particular, for each neuron
m
, there exists
\check{\rho}_{m}\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}
such that as
t\to\infty
:

\displaystyle\alpha_{m}[\rho](t)\to 0,\quad\forall\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}\setminus{\rm Orb}(\check{\rho}_{m}),\qquad\text{and}\qquad\varphi_{m}[\check{\rho}_{m}](t)\to 1.

The first property is part (i) of Theorem
4.3
, i.e., single representation, and the second is part (ii), i.e., rank-one rotational alignment.
Consequently, the limiting neuron is completely determined by the pair
(\check{\rho}_{m},u_{m})\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}\times\mathbb{D}
, where
u_{m}:=\phi_{\theta,m}[\check{\rho}_{m}](\infty)\in\mathbb{D}
is the winner’s limiting phasor.
To prove
\mu={\mathscr{T}_{\sf idft}}_{\#}\pi
, it therefore suffices to establish

u_{m}\sim\text{Haar}(\mathbb{D}),\qquad\check{\rho}_{m}\sim\text{Unif}({\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}),\qquad\check{\rho}_{m}\perp\!\!\!\perp u_{m}.

Preparation: Independence at Initialization.

We show that under uniform spherical initialization,
\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho](0)
is Haar-uniform and independent of the magnitudes and relative phases at all representations.
Note that by applying the DFT, each parameter
\theta_{m},\xi_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{|G|}
can be captured by

\big\{\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho],\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho],\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho],\phi_{\xi,m}[\rho]\big\}_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}}.

Recall that
\varphi_{m}[\rho]=\overline{\phi_{\xi,m}[\rho]}\cdot\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho]^{2}\in\mathbb{D}
.
Adopting this definition, we define a set

\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)}:=\bigcup_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}}\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)}[\rho],\qquad\text{where}~~\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)}[\rho]=\big\{\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho](0),\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho](0),\,\varphi_{m}[\rho](0)\big\}.

In the following, we first show that the initial phase is conditionally uniform given
\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)}
, and then propagate this through the dynamics.
Under the uniform spherical initialization
\theta_{m}(0),\xi_{m}(0)\overset{\rm i.i.d.}{\sim}{\rm Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{|G|-1})
, the DFT preserves the rotational symmetry, so at
t=0
we have

\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho](0),\phi_{\xi,m}[\rho](0)\sim\text{Haar}(\mathbb{D}),\quad\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho](0)\perp\!\!\!\perp\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho^{\prime}](0),\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho^{\prime}](0),\quad\forall\rho,\rho^{\prime}\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G).

Moreover, note
\{\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho](0)\}_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}}

and
\{\phi_{\xi,m}[\rho](0)\}_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}}

are mutually independent across
\rho
.
Hence, it is easy to show that

\varphi_{m}[\rho](0)\sim\mathrm{Haar}(\mathbb{D}),\qquad\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho](0)\perp\!\!\!\perp\varphi_{m}[\rho^{\prime}](0),\qquad\forall\rho,\rho^{\prime}\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1},

which jointly establishes that
\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho](0)\perp\!\!\!\perp\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)}
.
Thus, we can obtain that

\displaystyle\big(\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho](0)\mid\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)}\big)\overset{\rm d}{=}\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho](0)\sim\mathrm{Haar}(\mathbb{D}),\qquad\forall\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}.

(C.8)

In summary, at initialization the absolute phase
\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho](0)
is Haar-uniform and independent of all initial magnitudes and relative phases collected in
\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)}
.

Step 1: Uniformity of the Limiting Phase.

As shown in Lemma
C.1
, the phasor satisfies

\partial_{t}\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho]=-\frac{2a|G|}{M}\cdot\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho]\cdot\Im\big(\varphi_{m}[\rho]\big)\cdot\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho]\cdot{i},\qquad\forall\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}.

We define the accumulated rotation angle by

\Phi_{m}[\rho](t):=-\frac{2a|G|}{M}\cdot\int_{0}^{t}\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho](s)\cdot\Im\big(\varphi_{m}[\rho](s)\big)\,{\rm d}s\in\mathbb{R}.

Integrating the above scalar ODE over time yields

\displaystyle\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho](t)=\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho](0)\cdot\exp\big({i}\,\Phi_{m}[\rho](t)\big),\qquad\forall\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}.

(C.9)

Moreover, by Lemma
C.1
and Lemma
C.2
, the evolution of

\big\{(\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho],\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho],\varphi_{m}[\rho])\big\}_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}}
is governed by a closed ODE system. The time derivatives of these variables depend only on the same collection and the energy function
\Omega_{m}
, which can be written equivalently as
