\Omega_{m}=\sum_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}}\overline{\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]}\,\widehat{\theta_{m}}[\rho]^{2}=\sum_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}}\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho]\cdot\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho]^{2}\cdot\varphi_{m}[\rho].

Hence, by the Picard–Lindelöf theorem, for every
t\geq 0
, the solution is uniquely determined by the initial data
\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)}
.
In particular,
\Phi_{m}[\rho](t)
is a measurable function of
\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)}
for every
t\geq 0
.

The key observation is that
\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho](0)
does
not
enter the reduced system.
Thus, conditioned on
\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)}
, the accumulated rotation
\Phi_{m}[\rho](t)
is
deterministic
, and the solution (
C.9
) shows that
\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho](t)
is simply a fixed rotation of the random initial phase
\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho](0)
.
Hence, we have

\displaystyle u_{m}=\phi_{\theta,m}[\check{\rho}_{m}](\infty)=\phi_{\theta,m}[\check{\rho}_{m}](0)\cdot\exp({{i}\Phi_{m}[\check{\rho}_{m}](\infty)}).

Conditioning on
\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)}
fixes both
\check{\rho}_{m}
and
\exp({i}\Phi_{m}[\check{\rho}_{m}](\infty))\in\mathbb{D}
, so by (
C.8
) the conditional law is

\displaystyle u_{m}\mid\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)}\overset{\rm d}{=}\phi_{\theta,m}[\check{\rho}_{m}](0)\cdot\exp({i}\Phi_{m}[\check{\rho}_{m}](\infty))\mid\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)}\overset{\rm d}{=}\phi_{\theta,m}[\check{\rho}_{m}](0)\mid\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)}\sim\text{Haar}(\mathbb{D}),

(C.10)

where the second equality uses the rotational invariance of
\text{Haar}(\mathbb{D})
: multiplying a Haar-uniform variable by a deterministic element of
\mathbb{D}
does not change its distribution.

Step 2: Uniformity of the Winner Representation.

The proof strategy proceeds by symmetry: we first establish that the initialization is invariant under permutations of the non-trivial irreps, and subsequently demonstrate that the ODE dynamics preserve this symmetry.

Let
{\rm Bij}(X)
denote the set of all bijections from a set
X
to itself, and let
\mathfrak{S}
denote the subgroup of
{\rm Bij}({\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1})
consisting of bijections that respect the conjugation pairing:

\mathfrak{S}:=\big\{\pi\in{\rm Bij}({\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}):\pi(\rho^{\vee})=\pi(\rho)^{\vee},\;\forall\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}\big\}.

Each
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}
acts on a family of representation-indexed quantities
{\mathcal{S}}=({\mathcal{S}}[\rho])_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}}
by relabeling:

(\pi\circ{\mathcal{S}})[\rho]:={\mathcal{S}}[\pi(\rho)],\qquad\forall\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}.

First, we show that the initial distribution of Fourier components in
\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)}
is permutation invariant under the actions within
\mathfrak{S}
.
Let
\widehat{\theta_{m}}(0)=\{\widehat{\theta_{m}}[\rho](0)\}_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)}
and
\widehat{\xi_{m}}(0)=\{\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho](0)\}_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)}
.
Since
\theta_{m}(0),\xi_{m}(0)\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{|G|-1})
, their Fourier transform are uniformly distributed on the unit sphere of the real Fourier subspace, defined by

\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{R}}=\Big\{z=(z[\rho])_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)}:z[\rho^{\vee}]=\overline{z[\rho]},\ z[\rho_{\sf triv}]\in\mathbb{R}\Big\}.

Because
\pi
simply reorders the conjugate pairs while respecting
z[\rho^{\vee}]=\overline{z[\rho]}
, it preserves the inner product
\|z\|^{2}=\sum_{\rho}|z[\rho]|^{2}
on
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{R}}
, and therefore acts as an orthogonal transformation.
Moreover, since the uniform measure on the sphere is invariant under orthogonal transformations, we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\theta_{m}}(0)\overset{\rm d}{=}\pi\circ\widehat{\theta_{m}}(0),\qquad\widehat{\xi_{m}}(0)\overset{\rm d}{=}\pi\circ\widehat{\xi_{m}}(0),\qquad\forall\pi\in\mathfrak{S}.

Since
\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)}
is obtained from this joint Fourier components by the coordinate-wise measurable map

\big(\widehat{\theta_{m}}[\rho],\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]\big)\mapsto\big(\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho],\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho],\varphi_{m}[\rho]\big)
,
the permutation invariance passes to
\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)}\overset{\rm d}{=}\pi\circ\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)},\qquad\forall\pi\in\mathfrak{S}.

(C.11)

Next, we show that the ODE dynamics preserves this symmetry.
Let
\Psi_{t}
denote the flow map of the closed ODE system of (
C.3a
), (
C.3b
) and (
C.7
), and thus we have

\Psi_{t}(\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)})=\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(t)}:=\{(\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho](t),\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho](t),\varphi_{m}[\rho](t))\}_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}}.

We claim that the flow
\Psi_{t}
is
\mathfrak{S}
-equivariant. That is, for every
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}
and
t\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\Psi_{t}(\pi\circ\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)})=\pi\circ\Psi_{t}(\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)}).

(C.12)

To prove this, let
\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(t)}=\Psi_{t}(\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)})
denote the original trajectory, and consider the permuted
\pi\circ\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(t)}
:

(\pi\circ\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(t)})[\rho]=\{\alpha_{\theta,m}[\pi(\rho)](t),\alpha_{\xi,m}[\pi(\rho)](t),\varphi_{m}[\pi(\rho)](t)\}.

Recall that the dynamics of
\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(t)}
is governed by (
C.3a
), (
C.3b
), and (
C.7
). It follows that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\pi\circ\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(t)})[\rho]=\pi\circ\partial_{t}\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(t)}[\rho]=\pi\circ{\rm Dyn}(\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(t)}[\rho],\Omega_{m}(\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(t)})),

(C.13)

where
{\rm Dyn}(\cdot)
denotes the right-hand side of the ODE system.
Moreover, the energy satisfies

\displaystyle\Omega_{m}(\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(t)})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}}\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho](t)^{2}\cdot\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho](t)\cdot\Re(\varphi_{m}[\rho](t))

\displaystyle=\sum_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}}\alpha_{\theta,m}[\pi(\rho)](t)^{2}\cdot\alpha_{\xi,m}[\pi(\rho)](t)\cdot\Re(\varphi_{m}[\pi(\rho)](t))=\Omega_{m}(\pi\circ\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(t)}),

(C.14)

where the second equality uses that
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}
is a bijection.
Combining (
C.13
) and (
C.14
), we obtain

\partial_{t}(\pi\circ\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(t)})[\rho]=\pi\circ{\rm Dyn}(\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(t)}[\rho],\Omega_{m}(\pi\circ\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(t)}))={\rm Dyn}(\pi\circ\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(t)}[\rho],\Omega_{m}(\pi\circ\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(t)})).

Thus,
\pi\circ\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(t)}
solves the same ODE with initial condition
\pi\circ\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)}
.
By the Picard–Lindelöf uniqueness theorem, it must coincide with
\Psi_{t}(\pi\circ\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)})
, which proves (
C.12
).

Since
\check{\rho}_{m}
is determined entirely by the long-time behavior of
\Psi_{t}(\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)})
, we can define a
winner-selection map

{\rm Sel}:\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)}\mapsto{\bm{1}}_{{\rm Orb}(\check{\rho}_{m})}={\bm{1}}_{\{\check{\rho}_{m},\check{\rho}_{m}^{\vee}\}}
that maps the initial reduced state to the conjugate pair of the winning representation.
Based on (
C.12
), this map is
\mathfrak{S}
-equivariant:

{\rm Sel}(\pi\circ\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)})=\pi\circ{\rm Sel}(\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)}),\qquad\forall\pi\in\mathfrak{S}.

Combined with the distributional invariance (
C.11
), for any
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}
and
\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big({\rm Sel}(\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)})={\bm{1}}_{{\rm Orb}(\rho)}\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\big({\rm Sel}(\pi\circ\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)})={\bm{1}}_{{\rm Orb}(\rho)}\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\big(\pi\circ{\rm Sel}(\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)})={\bm{1}}_{{\rm Orb}(\rho)}\big)=\mathbb{P}\big({\rm Sel}(\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)})={\bm{1}}_{{\rm Orb}(\pi^{-1}(\rho))}\big).

(C.15)

Since
\mathfrak{S}
acts transitively on the conjugate pairs in
{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}
(i.e., for any
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}
, there exists
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}
with
\pi(\rho_{1})=\rho_{2}
), (
C.15
) implies that

\mathbb{P}\big({\rm Sel}(\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)})={\rm Orb}(\rho)\big)=\mathbb{P}\big({\rm Sel}(\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)})={\rm Orb}(\rho^{\prime})\big),\qquad\forall\rho,\rho^{\prime}\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}.

Since these probabilities are equal across all conjugate pairs and must sum to one, we conclude

\displaystyle\check{\rho}_{m}\sim{\rm Unif}({\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}).

In words, the
\mathfrak{S}
-symmetry of both the initialization and the dynamics forces every non-trivial irrep to be equally likely to win.

Step 3: Independence.

Steps 1 and 2 established the marginal distributions, i.e.,
u_{m}\sim\text{Haar}(\mathbb{D})
and
\check{\rho}_{m}\sim\text{Unif}({\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1})
.
It remains to show that
\check{\rho}_{m}
and
u_{m}
are independent.
Recall
\check{\rho}_{m}={\rm Sel}(\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)})
is a deterministic function of the reduced state
\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)}
, and is therefore
\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)}
-measurable.
On the other hand, the conditional law of
u_{m}
given
\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)}
is
\text{Haar}(\mathbb{D})
by (
C.10
), which does not depend on the realization of
\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)}
.
It follows that for any bounded measurable
f:\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{R}
and any event
A\in\sigma(\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)})
,

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)}}[f(u_{m})\cdot{\bm{1}}_{A}]=\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{E}[f(u_{m})\mid\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)}]\cdot{\bm{1}}_{A}\big]=\mathbb{E}_{\rm Haar}[f]\cdot\mathbb{P}(A),

which shows that
u_{m}\perp\!\!\!\perp\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)}
.
Since
\check{\rho}_{m}
is
\mathcal{G}_{m}^{(0)}
-measurable, this gives
\check{\rho}_{m}\perp\!\!\!\perp u_{m}
.
Combining the two marginals with independence, the joint law is the product measure

(\check{\rho}_{m},u_{m})\sim\text{Unif}({\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1})\otimes\text{Haar}(\mathbb{D})=\pi.

Finally, since the neurons are initialized i.i.d., the pairs
\{(\check{\rho}_{m},u_{m})\}_{m\in[M]}
are i.i.d. with respect to
\pi
, which completes the characterization of the limiting distribution and conclude the proof.
∎

C.2.2
Proof of Lemma
C.4
: The Limiting Distribution Satisfies
\mu
-PA

With the limiting measure now identified, it remains to verify that its mean-field predictor favors the correct label
g_{1}g_{2}
. We do this by expanding the neuron logit under the parameterization induced by
{\mathscr{T}_{\sf idft}}
and then averaging over the phase
u
and the representation
\check{\rho}
.

Proof of Lemma
C.4
.

We begin by expanding the logit expression using the Fourier representation of the parameters.
Let
\check{\rho}\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}
denote the single non-zero representation within each neuron.
Using the conjugacy relation from Lemma
D.4
, we can write:

\displaystyle\big(\theta_{g_{1}}+\theta_{g_{2}})^{2}

\displaystyle=\big(\widehat{\theta}[\check{\rho}]\cdot\check{\rho}(g_{1})+\widehat{\theta}[\check{\rho}^{\vee}]\cdot\check{\rho}^{\vee}(g_{1})+\widehat{\theta}[\check{\rho}]\cdot\check{\rho}(g_{2})+\widehat{\theta}[\check{\rho}^{\vee}]\cdot\check{\rho}^{\vee}(g_{2})\big)^{2}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\big(\widehat{\theta}[\check{\rho}]\cdot\check{\rho}(g_{1})+\overline{\widehat{\theta}[\check{\rho}]\cdot\check{\rho}(g_{1})}\big)^{2}}_{:=S_{1}}+\underbrace{\big(\widehat{\theta}[\check{\rho}]\cdot\check{\rho}(g_{2})+\overline{\widehat{\theta}[\check{\rho}]\cdot\check{\rho}(g_{2})}\big)^{2}}_{:=S_{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad+2\cdot\underbrace{\big(\widehat{\theta}[\check{\rho}]\cdot\check{\rho}(g_{1})+\overline{\widehat{\theta}[\check{\rho}]\cdot\check{\rho}(g_{1})}\big)\cdot\big(\widehat{\theta}[\check{\rho}]\cdot\check{\rho}(g_{2})+\overline{\widehat{\theta}[\check{\rho}]\cdot\check{\rho}(g_{2})}\big)}_{:=S_{12}}.

Moreover, we can expand the auxiliary terms
S_{1},S_{2}
and the cross term
S_{12}
as

\displaystyle S_{\tau}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\theta}[\check{\rho}]^{2}\cdot\check{\rho}(g_{\tau}^{2})+\overline{\widehat{\theta}[\check{\rho}]}^{2}\cdot\check{\rho}(g_{\tau}^{-2})+2|\widehat{\theta}[\check{\rho}]|^{2},

\displaystyle S_{12}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\theta}[\check{\rho}]^{2}\cdot\check{\rho}(g_{1}g_{2})+\overline{\widehat{\theta}[\check{\rho}]}^{2}\cdot\check{\rho}((g_{1}g_{2})^{-1})+|\widehat{\theta}[\check{\rho}]|^{2}\cdot\{\rho(g_{1}g_{2}^{-1})+\rho(g_{1}^{-1}g_{2})\}.

Expanding the product and grouping terms by the Fourier coefficients, we obtain:

\displaystyle\xi_{j}\cdot\big(\theta_{g_{1}}+\theta_{g_{2}}\big)^{2}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\xi}[\check{\rho}]\cdot\widehat{\theta}[\check{\rho}]^{2}\cdot C_{4}+\widehat{\xi}[\check{\rho}]\cdot\big|\widehat{\theta}[\check{\rho}]\big|^{2}\cdot C_{2}+\overline{\widehat{\xi}[\check{\rho}]}\cdot\overline{\widehat{\theta}[\check{\rho}]^{2}}\cdot C_{-4}+\overline{\widehat{\xi}[\check{\rho}]}\cdot\big|\widehat{\theta}[\check{\rho}]\big|^{2}\cdot C_{-2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\widehat{\xi}[\check{\rho}]\cdot\overline{\widehat{\theta}[\check{\rho}]}^{2}\cdot\big\{\check{\rho}(jg_{1}^{-2})+\check{\rho}(jg_{2}^{-2})+2\check{\rho}(j(g_{1}g_{2})^{-1})\big\}

\displaystyle\qquad+\overline{\widehat{\xi}[\check{\rho}]}\cdot\widehat{\theta}[\check{\rho}]^{2}\cdot\big\{\check{\rho}(j^{-1}g_{1}^{2})+\check{\rho}(j^{-1}g_{2}^{2})+2\check{\rho}(j^{-1}g_{1}g_{2})\big\},

(C.16)

where
C_{4},C_{-4},C_{2},C_{-2}
are some complex constants depending on
\check{\rho}\in\widehat{G}_{\neq 1}
and
j,g_{1},g_{2}\in G
.
Recall that
\mu
is the pushforward of the product measure
\pi=\mathrm{Unif}({\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1})\otimes\mathrm{Haar}(\mathbb{T})
via mapping:

{\mathscr{T}_{\sf idft}}:(\check{\rho},u)\mapsto\sqrt{2/|G|}\cdot\big(\Re(u\check{\rho}(\cdot)),\Re(u^{2}\check{\rho}(\cdot))\big),

which assigns to each pair
(\check{\rho},u)
the corresponding Fourier coefficients for
(\theta,\xi)
.
Integrating against
\mu
therefore reduces to integrating over representations and phases.
Equivalently, we have

(\theta_{g},\xi_{g})=\sqrt{2/|G|}\cdot\big(\Re(u\check{\rho}(g)),\Re(u^{2}\check{\rho}(g))\big)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad(\widehat{\theta}[\check{\rho}],\widehat{\xi}[\check{\rho}])=\sqrt{1/2|G|}\cdot(u,u^{2}).

Define function
F_{j}^{g_{1},g_{2}}:(\theta,\xi)\mapsto\xi_{j}\cdot\big(\theta_{g_{1}}+\theta_{g_{2}}\big)^{2}
for each label
j\in G
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\int_{(\mathbb{S}^{|G|-1})^{\otimes 2}}\eqref{eq:neuron_logit_abelian}~{\rm d}\mu(\theta,\xi)=\int_{{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}\times\mathbb{T}}F_{j}^{g_{1},g_{2}}\circ{\mathscr{T}_{\sf idft}}(\check{\rho},u){\rm d}(\pi_{\check{\rho}}\otimes\pi_{u})(\check{\rho},u).

Moreover, the neuron logit can be further written as a polynomial of
u

(
C.16
)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2|G|)^{3/2}}\sum_{\kappa\in\{-4,-2,2,4\}}C_{\kappa}\cdot u^{\kappa}+\frac{1}{(2|G|)^{3/2}}\cdot\big(\check{\rho}(jg_{1}^{-2})+\check{\rho}(jg_{2}^{-2})+2\check{\rho}(j(g_{1}g_{2})^{-1})\big)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{(2|G|)^{3/2}}\cdot\big(\check{\rho}(j^{-1}g_{1}^{2})+\check{\rho}(j^{-1}g_{2}^{2})+2\check{\rho}(j^{-1}g_{1}g_{2})\big).

(C.17)

The key observation is that the phase integrals over the unit circle
\mathbb{T}
vanish for all non-zero powers:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi}[u^{k}]=\int_{\mathbb{T}}u^{k}{\rm d}\pi_{u}(u)=2\pi\cdot\int_{0}^{2\pi}e^{ikt}{\rm d}t=\frac{e^{ikt}}{2\pi ik}\bigg|_{0}^{2\pi}=0,\qquad\forall k\neq 0.

(C.18)

Combining (
C.17
) and (
C.18
) yields that

\displaystyle{f}_{\sf NN}^{\mu}(g_{1},g_{2})_{j}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{(\theta,\xi)\sim\mu}\big[\xi_{j}\cdot\big(\theta_{g_{1}}+\theta_{g_{2}}\big)^{2}\big]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}|G|^{3/2}\cdot(|G|-1)}\sum_{\check{\rho}\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}}\big(\check{\rho}(jg_{1}^{-2})+\check{\rho}(jg_{2}^{-2})+2\check{\rho}(j(g_{1}g_{2})^{-1})\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}|G|^{1/2}\cdot(|G|-1)}\cdot\big(\ind(j=g_{1}^{2})+\ind(j=g_{2}^{2})+2\cdot\ind(j=g_{1}\star g_{2})-4/|G|\big),

where the last equality applies the orthogonality relation for characters
\sum_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)}\rho(g)=|G|\cdot\ind(g={\mathrm{Id}})
.
Thus, the correct label
j=g_{1}\star g_{2}
achieves the largest expected logit, completing the proof.
∎

C.3
Proof of Theorem
5.3
: Convergence Rate for Abelian Groups

We first establish two structural lemmas that facilitate the subsequent analysis of the dynamics.
The first lemma shows that under scale-matching initialization, the magnitudes of input and output Fourier coefficients for any representation
\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)
remain equal throughout training.

Lemma C.5
.

Under the initialization in Definition
5.2
, throughout the training, the following equality holds

|\widehat{\theta_{m}}[\rho](t)|=|\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho](t)|,\qquad\forall(t,\rho)\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times{\mathrm{Irr}}(G).

Proof of Lemma
C.5
.

Combining (
C.3a
) and (
C.3b
) in Lemma
C.1
yields that

\partial_{t}\big(|\widehat{\theta_{m}}[\rho]|^{2}-|\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]|^{2}\big)=-\frac{4a|G|^{2}}{M}\cdot\Omega_{m}\cdot\big(|\widehat{\theta_{m}}[\rho]|^{2}-|\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]|^{2}\big).

By taking integration at both side, it holds that

|\widehat{\theta_{m}}[\rho]|^{2}(t)-|\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]|^{2}(t)=\frac{4a|G|^{2}}{M}\cdot\big(|\widehat{\theta_{m}}[\rho]|^{2}(0)-|\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]|^{2}(0)\big)\cdot\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{t}\Omega_{m}(\tau){\rm d}t\right),\quad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}.

Recall we consider a scale-matching initialization specified in Definition
5.2
such that
|\widehat{\theta_{m}}[\rho]|^{2}(0)-|\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho]|^{2}(0)=0
for all
\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)
.
Combining the results above completes the proof.
∎

Following this lemma, for notational simplicity, we define the common magnitude variable:

\alpha_{m}[\rho](t):=|\widehat{\theta_{m}}[\rho](t)|=|\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho](t)|\in\mathbb{R},\qquad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}.

Recall from Lemma
C.1
that the relative phase alignment
\varphi_{m}[\rho]=\overline{\phi_{\xi,m}[\rho]}\cdot\phi_{\theta,m}[\rho]^{2}\in\mathbb{D}
captures the phase relationship between the input and output Fourier coefficients. Also recall from (
C.2
) that the energy function
\Omega_{m}=\sum_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}}\overline{\widehat{\xi}_{m}[\rho]}\cdot\widehat{\theta}_{m}[\rho]^{2}
governs the strength of the normalization term.
Under scale-matching, the pair
(\alpha_{m}[\rho],\varphi_{m}[\rho])
fully determines the state of each representation, and the following lemma shows that their joint dynamics form a closed system.

Lemma C.6
.

Under the initialization in Definition
5.2
, the dynamics in (
C.1a
) and (
C.1b
) are equivalent to

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\alpha_{m}[\rho]

\displaystyle=\frac{2a|G|}{M}\cdot\alpha_{m}[\rho]^{2}\cdot\Re(\varphi_{m}[\rho])-\frac{2a|G|^{2}}{M}\cdot\Omega_{m}\cdot\alpha_{m}[\rho],

(C.19)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\varphi_{m}[\rho]

\displaystyle=\frac{6a|G|}{M}\cdot\alpha_{m}[\rho]-\frac{6a|G|}{M}\cdot\alpha_{m}[\rho]\cdot\varphi_{m}[\rho]\cdot\Re(\varphi_{m}[\rho]).

(C.20)

In the magnitude ODE (
C.19
), the first term is a
self-reinforcement
that amplifies representations with larger scale and good phase alignment, i.e.,
\Re(\varphi_{m}[\rho])
close to one, while the second is a
competition
term that penalizes all representations through energy
\Omega_{m}
.
The phase-alignment ODE (
C.20
) drives
\varphi_{m}[\rho]\to 1
at a rate proportional to
\alpha_{m}[\rho]
.

Proof of Lemma
C.6
.

We prove the two statements separately.
Throughout, Lemma
C.5
guarantees
\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho]=\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho]=\alpha_{m}[\rho]
, so we write
\alpha_{m}[\rho]
in place of both
\alpha_{\theta,m}[\rho]
and
\alpha_{\xi,m}[\rho]
.

Magnitude Dynamics.

We apply the chain rule to
\alpha_{m}[\rho]^{2}=|\widehat{\theta_{m}}[\rho]|^{2}
, which yields
\partial_{t}(\alpha_{m}[\rho]^{2})=2\alpha_{m}[\rho]\cdot\partial_{t}\alpha_{m}[\rho]
.
Leveraging the ODE in (
C.3a
) from Lemma
C.1
, we have

\displaystyle 2\alpha_{m}[\rho]\cdot\partial_{t}\alpha_{m}[\rho]

\displaystyle=\frac{4a|G|}{M}\cdot\alpha_{m}[\rho]^{3}\cdot\Re(\varphi_{m}[\rho])-\frac{4a|G|^{2}}{M}\cdot\Omega_{m}\cdot\alpha_{m}[\rho]^{2}.

Then, dividing both sides by
2\alpha_{m}[\rho]
yields (
C.19
).

Phase-Alignment Dynamics.

By the phase-alignment ODE in Lemma
C.2
, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\varphi_{m}[\rho]=-\frac{6a|G|}{M}\cdot\alpha_{m}[\rho]\cdot\Im\big(\varphi_{m}[\rho]\big)\cdot\varphi_{m}[\rho]\cdot{i}.

(C.21)

To rewrite this in terms of
\Re(\varphi_{m}[\rho])
, we use the unit-circle identity: for any
\varphi\in\mathbb{D}
, we have

-\Im(\varphi)\cdot\varphi\cdot{i}=1-\varphi\cdot\Re(\varphi)
.
This identity can be verified by writing
\varphi=e^{i\psi}
and expanding both ends of the equation. Combining this identity with (
C.21
) yields (
C.20
).
∎

Now we are ready to prove Theorem
5.3
.

Proof of Theorem
5.3
.

The ODE dynamics in Lemma
C.6
couple
phase alignment
, i.e.,
\varphi_{m}[\rho]\to 1
, and
representation competition
, i.e., one
\alpha_{m}[\rho]
dominates the others.
Theorem
5.3
establishes a separate convergence rate for each mechanism, given that the other mechanism is realized when initialized:

\triangleright

Case 1: Phase Alignment.
A single conjugate pair
{\rm Orb}(\check{\rho}_{m})
is active, so the magnitude is constant and the dynamics reduces to a scalar phase ODE with constant coefficients.

\triangleright

Case 2: Representation Competition.
All phases are perfectly aligned, i.e.,
\varphi_{m}[\rho]\equiv 1
, and the analysis reduces to studying log-ratio dynamics among the magnitudes.

Before deriving the main results, we first provide an alternative representation of the dynamics in Lemma
C.6
.
By taking the real and imaginary part of both sides of (
C.20
) respectively, after some simple transformations, we can obtain that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\log\Im(\varphi_{m}[\rho])=\frac{1}{\Im(\varphi_{m}[\rho])}\cdot\partial_{t}\Im(\varphi_{m}[\rho])=-\frac{6a|G|}{M}\cdot\alpha_{m}[\rho]\cdot\Re(\varphi_{m}[\rho]),

\displaystyle\partial_{t}{\rm arctanh}\big(\Re(\varphi_{m}[\rho])\big)=\frac{1}{1-\Re(\varphi_{m}[\rho])^{2}}\cdot\partial_{t}\Re(\varphi_{m}[\rho])=\frac{6a|G|}{M}\cdot\alpha_{m}[\rho].

(C.22)

Moreover, we can write the dynamics of scale in (
C.19
) as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\log\alpha_{m}[\rho]=\frac{2a|G|}{M}\cdot(\alpha_{m}[\rho]\cdot\Re(\varphi_{m}[\rho])-|G|\cdot\Omega_{m}).

(C.23)

The sphere constraint gives that the scales of representations should satisfy

\sum_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)}\alpha_{m}[\rho]^{2}=\sum_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)}|\widehat{\theta_{m}}[\rho]|^{2}=1/|G|,

where we use Lemma
D.7
.
We now consider two initialization schemes that yield explicit dynamics.

Case 1: Phase Alignment.

In this case, each neuron is initialized with support on a single non-trivial representation
\check{\rho}_{m}\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)
.
Given the single-frequency initialization and the unit sphere constraint, we can show that

\alpha_{m}[\rho](0)=1/\sqrt{2|G|},\quad\forall\rho\in{\rm Orb}(\check{\rho}_{m}),\text{~~and~~}\alpha_{m}[\rho]=0,\quad\forall\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}\backslash{\rm Orb}(\check{\rho}_{m}).

Recall that (
C.19
) in Lemma
C.6
implies that that

\displaystyle\alpha_{m}[\rho](t)=\alpha_{m}[\rho](0)\cdot\exp\left(\frac{2a|G|}{M}\int_{0}^{t}\alpha_{m}[\rho](s)^{2}\cdot\Re(\varphi_{m}[\rho](s)){\rm d}s-\frac{2a|G|^{2}}{M}\int_{0}^{t}\Omega_{m}(s)\cdot\alpha_{m}[\rho](s){\rm d}s\right),

for all time
t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.
For
\rho\neq\check{\rho}
, it apparent that
\alpha_{m}[\rho](t)\equiv 0
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is the solution:

\alpha_{m}[\rho](t)\equiv 0,\qquad\forall(\rho,t)\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}\backslash{\rm Orb}(\check{\rho}_{m})\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}.

Hence, the single-frequency pattern is preserved throughout the training.
(
C.22
) can be written as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}{\rm arctanh}\big(\Re(\varphi_{m}[\rho])\big)=3\sqrt{2}a|G|^{1/2}/M,

which indicated that

\displaystyle\frac{1+\Re(\varphi_{m}[\rho](t))}{1-\Re(\varphi_{m}[\rho](t))}=\frac{1+\Re(\varphi_{m}[\rho](0))}{1-\Re(\varphi_{m}[\rho](0))}\cdot\exp\left(3\sqrt{2}a|G|^{1/2}/M\cdot t\right),\qquad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}.

This shows that the phase alignment
\Re(\varphi_{m}[\rho])
converges to
1
exponentially fast.
Thus, to ensure
\Re(\varphi_{m}[\rho](T))\leq 1-\varepsilon
, the required time
T
must satisfy

T\gtrsim\frac{M}{a|G|^{1/2}}\cdot\log\left(\frac{2-\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}\cdot\frac{1-\Re(\varphi_{m}[\rho](0))}{1+\Re(\varphi_{m}[\rho](0))}\right).

Given that
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
is chosen to be sufficiently small, the following simplified lower bound for
T
is sufficient:

T\gtrsim\frac{M}{a|G|^{1/2}}\cdot\log\left(\frac{2}{\varepsilon}\cdot\frac{1-\Re(\varphi_{m}[\rho](0))}{1+\Re(\varphi_{m}[\rho](0))}\right).

Case 2: Phase-Aligned Initialization.

In this case, neurons are initialized with perfect phase alignment, i.e.,
\varphi_{m}[\rho](0)=1
for all
\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}
.
We show that phase alignment is preserved, reducing the system to a pure magnitude competition, and then derive the convergence rate.

We first show that perfect phase alignment is an invariant of the dynamics.
Note (
C.20
) gives

1-\Re(\varphi_{m}[\rho](t))=-(1-\Re(\varphi_{m}[\rho](0)))\cdot\left(-\frac{6a|G|}{M}\cdot\int_{0}^{t}(1+\Re(\varphi_{m}[\rho](s)))\cdot\alpha_{m}[\rho](s){\rm d}s\right),

where the right-hand side vanishes at
\varphi_{m}[\rho]=1
.
Thus, we have
\Re(\varphi_{m}[\rho](t))\equiv 1
for all
\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}
and
t\geq 0
.
Since
\varphi_{m}[\rho](t)
stays on the unit circle
\mathbb{D}
, we have
\varphi_{m}[\rho](t)\equiv 1
throughout the training.
Under this invariance, substituting
\Re(\varphi_{m}[\rho])=1
into the log-magnitude dynamics (
C.23
) gives

\partial_{t}\log\alpha_{m}[\rho]=\frac{2a|G|}{M}\cdot\big(\alpha_{m}[\rho]-|G|\cdot\Omega_{m}\big).

(C.24)

We next derive the log-ratio ODE that governs the competition between representations.
For any two non-trivial representations
\rho,\rho^{\prime}\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}
, subtracting (
C.24
) for
\rho^{\prime}
from that for
\rho
gives

\partial_{t}\log\left(\frac{\alpha_{m}[\rho]}{\alpha_{m}[\rho^{\prime}]}\right)=\frac{2a|G|}{M}\cdot\big(\alpha_{m}[\rho]-\alpha_{m}[\rho^{\prime}]\big)=\frac{2a|G|}{M}\cdot\alpha_{m}[\rho]\cdot\left(1-\frac{\alpha_{m}[\rho^{\prime}]}{\alpha_{m}[\rho]}\right).

(C.25)

To simplify the notation, we define
r_{\check{\rho}_{m},\rho}(t)=\alpha_{m}[\check{\rho}_{m}](t)/\alpha_{m}[\rho](t)
and
\check{\rho}_{m}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}}\alpha_{m}[\rho](0),

where
\check{\rho}_{m}
is the initially dominant representation.
Hence, by definition, we have
r_{\check{\rho}_{m},\rho}(0)>1
for all
\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}\backslash{\rm Orb}(\check{\rho}_{m})
.
Setting
\rho=\check{\rho}_{m}
in (
C.25
) yields that

\partial_{t}\log r_{\check{\rho}_{m},\rho}=\frac{2a|G|}{M}\cdot\alpha_{m}[\check{\rho}_{m}]\cdot(1-1/r_{\check{\rho}_{m},\rho}),

which further indicates that

\partial_{t}\log(r_{\check{\rho}_{m},\rho}-1)=\frac{\partial_{t}\log r_{\check{\rho}_{m},\rho}}{1-1/r_{\check{\rho}_{m},\rho}}=\frac{2a|G|}{M}\cdot\alpha_{m}[\check{\rho}_{m}].

Integrating this ODE with respect to
t
gives

r_{\check{\rho}_{m},\rho}(t)=1+\big(r_{\check{\rho}_{m},\rho}(0)-1\big)\cdot\exp\!\left(\frac{2a|G|}{M}\cdot\int_{0}^{t}\alpha_{m}[\check{\rho}_{m}](s)\,{\rm d}s\right),\qquad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}.

(C.26)

Since
r_{\check{\rho}_{m},\rho}(0)-1>0
and the exponential is at least
1
, we have
r_{\check{\rho}_{m},\rho}(t)\geq r_{\check{\rho}_{m},\rho}(0)>1
for all
t\geq 0
.
This confirms that
\check{\rho}_{m}
remains the dominant representation throughout training.
Next, we prove that
\alpha_{m}[\check{\rho}_{m}](t)
is monotonically nondecreasing.
From (
C.24
) with
\rho=\check{\rho}_{m}
, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\log\alpha_{m}[\check{\rho}_{m}]

\displaystyle=\frac{2a|G|}{M}\cdot\bigg(\alpha_{m}[\check{\rho}_{m}]-|G|\cdot\sum_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}}\alpha_{m}[\rho]^{3}\bigg)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{2a|G|}{M}\cdot\alpha_{m}[\check{\rho}_{m}]\cdot\bigg(1-|G|\cdot\sum_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}}\alpha_{m}[\rho]^{2}\bigg)\geq 0,

where the second inequality follows from the sphere constraint
\sum_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)}\alpha_{m}[\rho]^{2}=1/|G|
.
We now derive the convergence rate.
We define
r_{\min}=\min_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}\backslash{\rm Orb}(\check{\rho}_{m})}r_{\check{\rho}_{m},\rho}(0)
.
Since
\alpha_{m}[\check{\rho}_{m}](t)
is nondecreasing, we have
\int_{0}^{t}\alpha_{m}[\check{\rho}_{m}](s)\,{\rm d}s\geq\alpha_{m}[\check{\rho}_{m}](0)\cdot t
.
Substituting this into (
C.26
), we obtain

r_{\check{\rho}_{m},\rho}(t)\geq 1+(r_{\min}-1)\cdot\exp\!\left(\frac{2a|G|}{M}\cdot\alpha_{m}[\check{\rho}_{m}](0)\cdot t\right),\qquad\forall\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}\backslash{\rm Orb}(\check{\rho}_{m}).

Thus, as
t
goes to infinity, the ratio
r_{\check{\rho}_{m},\rho}(t)
goes to infinity for all non-trivial representation
\rho
.
To derive the convergence rate, we require
r_{\check{\rho}_{m},\rho}(T)\geq 1/\varepsilon
for a given
\varepsilon>0
.
It suffices to have

\displaystyle T\geq\frac{M}{2a|G|\cdot\alpha_{m}[\check{\rho}_{m}](0)}\cdot\log\!\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{-1}-1}{r_{\min}-1}\right).

(C.27)

Next, we analyze the trivial representation.
For this component, the dynamics simplify significantly as the quadratic growth term vanishes.
Specifically, the evolution is governed by:

\partial_{t}\widehat{\theta_{m}}[\rho_{\sf triv}]=-\frac{2a|G|^{2}}{M}\cdot\Omega_{m}\cdot\widehat{\theta_{m}}[\rho_{\sf triv}],\qquad\partial_{t}\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho_{\sf triv}]=-\frac{2a|G|^{2}}{M}\cdot\Omega_{m}\cdot\widehat{\xi_{m}}[\rho_{\sf triv}].

Solving these ODEs, for each parameter
\nu\in\{\theta_{m},\xi_{m}\}
, we obtain the closed-form expression:

\displaystyle\widehat{\nu}[\rho_{\sf triv}](t)

\displaystyle=\widehat{\nu}[\rho_{\sf triv}](0)\cdot\exp\left(-\frac{2a|G|^{2}}{M}\cdot\int_{0}^{t}\Omega_{m}(\tau){\rm d}t\right),\qquad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}.

(C.28)

Under the initial condition that
\varphi_{m}[\rho](0)=1
for all
\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}
, we can show that

\displaystyle\Omega_{m}(0)=\sum_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}}\alpha_{m}[\rho]^{3}\cdot\Re(\varphi_{m}[\rho](0))=\sum_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)_{\neq 1}}\alpha_{m}[\rho]^{3}\geq 0.

(C.29)

Recall that the dynamics follow a Riemannian gradient (ascent) flow with respect to the energy
\Omega_{m}
(see Lemma
B.3
).
Therefore,
\Omega_{m}(t)
is monotonically non-decreasing. Combining this monotonicity with (
C.28
) and (
C.29
) yields the upper bound:

\displaystyle\widehat{\nu}[\rho_{\sf triv}](t)

\displaystyle\lesssim\widehat{\nu}[\rho_{\sf triv}](0)\cdot\exp\left(-\frac{2a|G|^{2}}{M}\cdot\Omega_{m}(0)\cdot t\right),

which indicates that

r_{\check{\rho}_{m},\rho_{\sf triv}}(t)\gtrsim\frac{M}{a|G|^{2}}\cdot r_{\check{\rho}_{m},\rho_{\sf triv}}(0)\cdot\exp\left(\Omega_{m}(0)\cdot t\right).

To ensure the trivial representation is sufficiently suppressed, it suffices to choose a time:

\displaystyle T\gtrsim\frac{M}{a|G|^{2}\cdot\Omega_{m}(0)}\cdot\log\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{-1}}{r_{\check{\rho}_{m},\rho_{\sf triv}}(0)}\right).

(C.30)

The proof is completed by combining the time requirements from (
C.27
) and (
C.30
).
∎

Appendix D
Technical Lemmas

This appendix provides the technical lemmas supporting our main results.
In §
D.1
, we collect several auxiliary results required for the proofs in §
A.2
.
Next, §
D.2
records standard identities for irreducible representations that are invoked repeatedly throughout §
A.2
through §
C.3
.

D.1
Auxiliary Lemmas for §
A.2

The following three lemmas provide the analytic estimates needed in the proof of the trajectory approximation (Proposition
B.1
).

Lemma D.1
.

Let
G
be a finite set and
\{\kappa_{xy}\}_{(x,y)\in G^{2}}\subset\mathbb{R}
be a sequence of coefficients. Then we have

\bigg\|\sum_{x,y\in G}\kappa_{xy}\cdot e_{x}\bigg\|_{2}\leq|G|^{1/2}\cdot\bigg(\sum_{x,y\in G}\kappa_{xy}^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}.

Proof of Lemma
D.1
.

By expanding the squared
\ell_{2}
-norm coordinate-wise, we obtain that

\bigg\|\sum_{x,y\in G}\kappa_{xy}\cdot e_{x}\bigg\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{s\in G}\bigg(\sum_{x\in G}\kappa_{sx}\bigg)^{2}\leq|G|\cdot\sum_{s\in G}\sum_{x\in G}\kappa_{sx}^{2}=|G|\cdot\sum_{x,y\in G}\kappa_{xy}^{2},

where the inequality uses Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.
Taking square roots completes the proof.
∎

Lemma D.2
.

For each pair
(g_{1},g_{2})\in G^{2}
, let
f_{g_{1}g_{2}}\in\mathbb{R}^{|G|}
be a logit vector and
{p}_{g_{1}g_{2}}={\mathrm{smax}}(f_{g_{1}g_{2}})\in\mathbb{R}^{|G|}
the corresponding softmax distribution.
Define the maximal logit gap

\Delta f_{\max}=\max_{g_{1},g_{2}\in G}\big(\max_{j\in G}(f_{g_{1}g_{2}})_{j}-\min_{j\in G}(f_{g_{1}g_{2}})_{j}\big).

If
\Delta f_{\max}\leq 1
, then the softmax distribution is close to uniform:

\max_{g_{1},g_{2}\in G}\left\|{p}_{g_{1}g_{2}}-{\bm{1}}_{|G|}/|G|\right\|_{\infty}\leq 2|G|^{-1}\cdot\Delta f_{\max}.

Proof of Lemma
D.2
.

We fix an arbitrary pair
(g_{1},g_{2})
and write
f=f_{g_{1}g_{2}}
for brevity.
Let
j_{+}\in\arg\max_{j\in G}f_{j}
and
j_{-}\in\arg\min_{j\in G}f_{j}
, so that
f_{j_{+}}-f_{j_{-}}\leq\Delta f_{\max}
.

Upper Bound.

For any
j\in G
, the softmax probability is maximized when
j
has the largest logit and all other logits are as small as possible.
Since each logit lies within the interval
[f_{j_{-}},\,f_{j_{+}}]
, we have

\displaystyle({p}_{g_{1}g_{2}})_{j}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\exp(f_{j_{+}})}{\exp(f_{j_{+}})+(|G|-1)\cdot\exp(f_{j_{-}})}\leq\frac{\exp(\Delta f_{\max})}{\exp(\Delta f_{\max})+|G|-1}.

Subtracting
1/|G|
and using
\Delta f_{\max}\leq 1
gives

({p}_{g_{1}g_{2}})_{j}-\frac{1}{|G|}\leq\frac{(|G|-1)\cdot(\exp(\Delta f_{\max})-1)}{|G|\cdot(\exp(\Delta f_{\max})+|G|-1)}\leq\frac{\exp(\Delta f_{\max})-1}{|G|}\leq\frac{2\Delta f_{\max}}{|G|},

where the last step uses
\exp(u)-1\leq 2u
for
u\in[0,1]
.

Lower Bound.

By the same reasoning applied to the minimum, we obtain

\frac{1}{|G|}-({p}_{g_{1}g_{2}})_{j}\leq\frac{1}{|G|}-\frac{1}{1+(|G|-1)\cdot\exp(\Delta f_{\max})}\leq\frac{2\,\Delta f_{\max}}{|G|},

where the last inequality follows by the same
\exp(u)-1\leq 2u
bound.
Combining the upper and lower bounds and taking the maximum yields the claimed result.
∎

Lemma D.3
.

Let
\iota\neq 0
denote a non-zero constant and
\zeta:[0,\infty)\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{n}
denote a continuous function.
For any initial condition
x(0)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the unique solution of

\partial_{t}x(t)=\iota x(t)+\zeta(t)

is given by

x(t)=x(0)\cdot\exp(\iota t)+\int_{0}^{t}\zeta(s)\cdot\exp(\iota(t-s)){\rm d}s.

In particular, if
\zeta(t)\equiv\zeta\in\mathbb{R}
is constant, then
x(t)=x(0)\cdot\exp(\iota t)+{\zeta}/{\iota}\cdot(\exp(\iota t)-1)
.

Proof of Lemma
D.3
.

Please refer to Lemma B.5 in
13
for detailed proof.
∎

D.2
Technical Identities for Irreducible Representations

This subsection collects identities for irreps that are used in the spectral dynamics proofs.
All results follow from two properties established in §
E
: the conjugacy relation for dual representations (see Definition
4.1
(ii)) and the Schur orthogonality relations.
We begin with a lemma relating the dual representation to complex conjugation, which follows directly from unitarity.

Lemma D.4

(Conjugate Relation for Dual Representations)
.

For any
\rho\in\widehat{G}
, we have
\rho^{\vee}(g)=\overline{\rho(g)}
for all
g\in G
.
Furthermore, for any real-valued function
\nu:G\mapsto\mathbb{R}
, the Fourier coefficients satisfy
\widehat{\nu}[\rho^{\vee}]=\overline{\widehat{\nu}[\rho]}
.

Proof of Lemma
D.4
.

Since
\rho
is an unitary representation, we have
\rho(g^{-1})=\rho(g)^{-1}=\rho(g)^{*}
.
Recall that the dual representation is defined by
\rho^{\vee}(g)=\rho(g^{-1})^{\top}
.
Then, we obtain that

\rho^{\vee}(g)=(\rho(g)^{*})^{\top}=(\overline{\rho(g)}^{\top})^{\top}=\overline{\rho(g)}.

Applying the definition of the transform and the property derived above gives that

\displaystyle\widehat{\nu}[\rho^{\vee}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{g\in G}\nu(g)\rho^{\vee}(g^{-1})=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{g\in G}\nu(g)\overline{\rho(g^{-1})}=\overline{\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{g\in G}\nu(g)\rho(g^{-1})}=\overline{\widehat{\nu}[\rho]},

which yields the desired results.
∎

The next two lemmas provide trace-level formulations of the Schur orthogonality, which are convenient for manipulating products of representation matrices summed over the group.

Lemma D.5

(Trace-Level Schur Orthogonality, Dual Form)
.

Let
G
be a finite group and let
\widehat{G}
denote its unitary dual.
For any
\rho,\sigma\in\widehat{G}
and any complex matrices

C_{1}\in\mathbb{C}^{d_{\rho}\times d_{\rho}}
,
C_{2}\in\mathbb{C}^{d_{\sigma}\times d_{\sigma}}
, it holds that

\sum_{g\in G}\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\big(C_{1}\rho(g)\big)\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\big(C_{2}\sigma^{\vee}(g)\big)={|G|}/{d_{\rho}}\cdot\ind(\rho=\sigma)\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\big(C_{1}C_{2}^{\top}\big).

Proof of Lemma
D.5
.

By the entry-wise Schur orthogonality (see Definition
4.1
(iii)), for any
\rho,\sigma\in\widehat{G}
and indices
i,j\in[d_{\rho}]
,
k,\ell\in[d_{\sigma}]
, we have

\langle\sqrt{d_{\rho}}\,\rho_{ij},\,\sqrt{d_{\sigma}}\,\sigma_{k\ell}\rangle_{L_{2}(G)}=\delta_{\rho,\sigma}\,\delta_{i,k}\,\delta_{j,\ell},

where
\delta_{\rho,\sigma}
equals
1
if
\rho=\sigma
and
0
otherwise and similarly for
\delta_{i,k}
,
\delta_{j,\ell}
.
Expanding the
L^{2}(G)
inner product
\langle f,h\rangle_{L_{2}(G)}=|G|^{-1}\sum_{g\in G}f(g)\overline{h(g)}
and rearranging gives

\sum_{g\in G}\rho_{ij}(g)\,\overline{\sigma_{k\ell}(g)}={|G|}/{d_{\rho}}\cdot\ind(\rho=\sigma)\cdot\delta_{i,k}\,\delta_{j,\ell}.

(D.1)

Based on Lemma
D.4
, we have

\sigma^{\vee}(g)=\overline{\sigma(g)}
such that
\sigma^{\vee}_{ij}(g)=\overline{\sigma_{ij}(g)}
for all
i,j\in[d_{\sigma}]
.
Following this, (
D.1
) can be rewritten as

\sum_{g\in G}\rho_{ij}(g)\sigma^{\vee}_{k\ell}(g)={|G|}/{d_{\rho}}\cdot\ind(\rho=\sigma)\cdot\delta_{i,k}\delta_{j,\ell}.

(D.2)

Moreover, by the definition of trace and the matrix product rule
(AB)_{ii}=\sum_{j}A_{ij}B_{ji}
, we have

\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(C_{1}\rho(g))=\sum_{i=1}^{d_{\rho}}(C_{1}\rho(g))_{ii}=\sum_{i=1}^{d_{\rho}}\sum_{j=1}^{d_{\rho}}(C_{1})_{ij}\cdot\rho_{ji}(g),\quad\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(C_{2}\sigma^{\vee}(g))=\sum_{k=1}^{d_{\sigma}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{d_{\sigma}}(C_{2})_{k\ell}\cdot\sigma^{\vee}_{\ell k}(g).

Multiplying these two expansions and exchanging the order of summation with
\sum_{g\in G}
gives

\displaystyle\sum_{g\in G}\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(C_{1}\rho(g))\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(C_{2}\sigma^{\vee}(g))

\displaystyle=\sum_{i_{1},j_{1}=1}^{d_{\rho}}\sum_{i_{2},j_{2}=1}^{d_{\sigma}}(C_{1})_{i_{1}j_{1}}(C_{2})_{i_{2}j_{2}}\cdot\sum_{g\in G}\rho_{j_{1}i_{1}}(g)\,\sigma^{\vee}_{j_{2}i_{2}}(g).

(D.3)

Applying (
D.2
) yields that

(
D.3
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i_{1},j_{1}=1}^{d_{\rho}}\sum_{i_{2},j_{2}=1}^{d_{\sigma}}(C_{1})_{i_{1}j_{1}}(C_{2})_{i_{2}j_{2}}\cdot{|G|}/{d_{\rho}}\cdot\ind(\rho=\sigma)\cdot\delta_{j_{1},j_{2}}\delta_{i_{1},i_{2}}

\displaystyle={|G|}/{d_{\rho}}\cdot\ind(\rho=\sigma)\cdot\sum_{i,j=1}^{d_{\rho}}(C_{1})_{ij}(C_{2})_{ij}={|G|}/{d_{\rho}}\cdot\ind(\rho=\sigma)\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(C_{1}C_{2}^{\top}).

Combining the arguments above completes the proof.
∎

Lemma D.6

(Trace-Level Schur Orthogonality, Inverse Form)
.

Let
G
be a finite group and let
\widehat{G}
denote its unitary dual.
Fix
\rho,\sigma\in\widehat{G}
and let
C\in\mathbb{C}^{d_{\rho}\times d_{\rho}}
.
Then the following matrix identity holds:

\sum_{g\in G}\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\big(C\rho(g)\big)\sigma(g^{-1})={|G|}/{d_{\rho}}\cdot\ind(\rho=\sigma)\cdot C.

Proof of Lemma
D.6
.

By the entry-wise Schur orthogonality (Definition
4.1
(iii)) and unitarity
\sigma(g^{-1})=\sigma(g)^{*}
, which gives
\sigma(g^{-1})_{k\ell}=\overline{\sigma(g)_{\ell k}}
, for any

\rho,\sigma\in\widehat{G}
and indices
i,j\in[d_{\rho}]
,
k,\ell\in[d_{\sigma}]
, we have

\sum_{g\in G}\rho_{ij}(g)\sigma(g^{-1})_{k\ell}={|G|}/{d_{\rho}}\cdot\ind(\rho=\sigma)\cdot\delta_{i,\ell}\cdot\delta_{j,k}.

(D.4)

Next, we evaluate the
(k,\ell)
-th entry of the matrix sum. Expanding the trace term gives

\displaystyle\bigg(\sum_{g\in G}\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(C\rho(g))\sigma(g^{-1})\bigg)_{k\ell}

\displaystyle=\sum_{g\in G}\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(C\rho(g))\,\sigma(g^{-1})_{k\ell}=\sum_{j_{1},j_{2}=1}^{d_{\rho}}C_{j_{1}j_{2}}\sum_{g\in G}\rho_{j_{2}j_{1}}(g)\sigma(g^{-1})_{k\ell}.

(D.5)

By applying (
D.4
), we obtain

\eqref{eq:entry_expand}=\sum_{j_{1},j_{2}=1}^{d_{\rho}}C_{j_{1}j_{2}}\cdot{|G|}/{d_{\rho}}\cdot\ind(\rho=\sigma)\cdot\delta_{j_{2},\ell}\cdot\delta_{j_{1},k}=\frac{|G|}{d_{\rho}}\cdot\ind(\rho=\sigma)\cdot C_{k\ell}.

As this relation holds for every pair of indices
(k,\ell)
, the desired result follows.
∎

Lastly, we state the Plancherel theorem for finite groups.

Lemma D.7

(Plancherel Theorem)
.

Let
f,h:G\mapsto\mathbb{R}
.
The inner product on
L^{2}(G)
is given by

\langle f,h\rangle_{L_{2}(G)}=\sum_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)}d_{\rho}\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\big(\widehat{f}[\rho](\widehat{h}[\rho])^{*}\big)=\sum_{\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)}d_{\rho}\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\big((\widehat{f}[\rho])^{*}\widehat{h}[\rho]\big).

Proof of Lemma
D.7
.

Please refer to Theorem 2 in Chapter 15,
37
for a detailed proof.
∎

Appendix E
Background: Group Representation Theory

This appendix provides a self-contained introduction to finite group representation theory on finite groups, following the exposition in
31
and
37
.
The presentation is tailored to readers with a background in linear algebra who may not be familiar with abstract algebra.

Definition E.1

(Group)
.

A
group

(G,\star)
consists of a set
G
and a binary operation
\star:G\times G\to G
satisfying three axioms:
(i)
associativity
:
(a\star b)\star c=a\star(b\star c)
for all
a,b,c\in G
;
(ii)
identity
: there exists an element
{\mathrm{Id}}\in G
such that
a\star{\mathrm{Id}}={\mathrm{Id}}\star a=a
for all
a\in G
;
and (iii)
inverses
: for every
a\in G
there exists
a^{-1}\in G
with
a\star a^{-1}=a^{-1}\star a={\mathrm{Id}}
.
The group is
finite
if
|G|<\infty
, and
Abelian
(or commutative) if
a\star b=b\star a
for all
a,b\in G
.

Several families of finite groups appear throughout this paper.

\triangleright

Cyclic Groups.
The
cyclic group

\mathbb{Z}_{n}=\{0,1,\dots,n-1\}
with addition modulo
n
is the simplest finite group; it is generated by a single element and is always Abelian.

\triangleright

Direct Products of Cyclic Groups.
The
direct product

\mathbb{Z}_{n_{1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{n_{d}}
consists of
d
-tuples with componentwise modular addition, and is the group underlying generalized modular addition (§
3.2
). By the Fundamental Theorem of Finite Abelian Groups
(see, e.g.
31
, Chapter 1)
, every finite Abelian group is isomorphic to such a direct product.

\triangleright

Symmetric and Alternating Groups.
A
permutation
of
\{1,\dots,n\}
is a bijection from this set to itself. For example, the map
1\mapsto 2,2\mapsto 3,3\mapsto 1
is the
3
-cycle
(1\;2\;3)
.
The
symmetric group

S_{n}
is the set of all
n!
such permutations under composition.
It is non-Abelian for
n\geq 3
.
The
alternating group

A_{n}\subset S_{n}
consists of all even permutations and has order
n!/2
and is non-Abelian for
n\geq 4
.

\triangleright

Frobenius Group
C_{7}\rtimes C_{3}
.
This is the non-Abelian group of order 21 used in our experiments (§
4.2
).
It is defined by two
generators

x
and
y
subject to three relations:

\langle x,y\mid x^{7}=1,\;y^{3}=1,\;yxy^{-1}=x^{2}\rangle.

Here
x
has order 7,
y
has order 3, and the relation
yxy^{-1}=x^{2}
specifies that conjugating
x
by
y
squares it.
Every element can be written uniquely as
x^{a}y^{b}
with
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{7}
and
b\in\mathbb{Z}_{3}
, so we identify group elements with pairs
(a,b)
.
To compute the group operation, we multiply
x^{a}y^{b}\cdot x^{a^{\prime}}y^{b^{\prime}}
by moving
y^{b}
past
x^{a^{\prime}}
.
The relation
yx=x^{2}y
, rearranged from
yxy^{-1}=x^{2}
, tells us that each
y
passing over an
x
doubles its exponent, so
y^{b}x^{a^{\prime}}=x^{2^{b}a^{\prime}}y^{b}
. This implies that

x^{a}y^{b}\cdot x^{a^{\prime}}y^{b^{\prime}}=x^{a}\cdot x^{2^{b}a^{\prime}}y^{b}y^{b^{\prime}}=x^{a+2^{b}a^{\prime}\bmod 7}\,y^{b+b^{\prime}\bmod 3}.

Since every element is
x^{a}y^{b}
, we can equivalently regard
\mathbb{Z}_{7}\times\mathbb{Z}_{3}
as the set of group elements, with the group operation
(a,b)\star(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})=(a+2^{b}a^{\prime}\bmod 7,\;b+b^{\prime}\bmod 3)
.
Note that
2^{3}=8\equiv 1\pmod{7}
, so three doublings return to the original value, consistent with
y^{3}=1
.
The group is non-Abelian precisely because of this doubling:
(1,0)\star(0,1)=(1,1)
while
(0,1)\star(1,0)=(2,1)
.

Representations.

The central idea of representation theory is to
linearize
a group: instead of working with abstract group elements, we map them to invertible matrices so that the group structure can be studied using the tools of linear algebra.

Definition E.2

(Representation)
.

A
(linear) representation
of a finite group
G
over the vector space
\mathbb{C}^{d_{\rho}}
is a homomorphism
\rho:G\to{\rm GL}(\mathbb{C}^{d_{\rho}})
, i.e., a map from group elements to
d_{\rho}\times d_{\rho}
invertible complex matrices satisfying

\rho(g_{1}\star g_{2})=\rho(g_{1})\cdot\rho(g_{2}),\qquad\forall g_{1},g_{2}\in G.

The integer
d_{\rho}
is called the
dimension
(or
degree
) of the representation.

It follows immediately that
\rho({\mathrm{Id}})=I_{d_{\rho}}
and
\rho(g^{-1})=\rho(g)^{-1}
.
Two representations
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
of the same dimension are
isomorphic
, denoted by
\rho\cong\rho^{\prime}
, if there exists an invertible matrix
M
such that
M\rho(g)=\rho^{\prime}(g)M
for all
g\in G
. Informally, two isomorphic representations differ only by a change of basis.
Every representation of a finite group is isomorphic to a
unitary
representation, i.e., one satisfying
\rho(g)^{*}\rho(g)=I_{d_{\rho}}
for all
g
.

Throughout this paper, we always work with unitary representations.

Examples of Representations.

We illustrate with the groups introduced above.

\triangleright

Cyclic Group.
The maps
\rho_{k}(g)=\exp(2\pi{i}kg/n)
for
k=0,1,\dots,n-1
are one-dimensional representations.
One can verify the homomorphism property, since

\rho_{k}(g_{1}+g_{2})=\exp(2\pi{i}k(g_{1}+g_{2})/n)=\rho_{k}(g_{1})\rho_{k}(g_{2}).

These are precisely the standard Fourier basis on
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
, where
\rho_{k}
corresponds to frequency
k
.

\triangleright

Direct Product of Cyclic Groups.
For the direct product
\mathbb{Z}_{n_{1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{n_{d}}
with componentwise modular addition, the representations are products of one-dimensional cyclic representations:
\rho_{k_{1},\dots,k_{d}}(g_{1},\dots,g_{d})=\prod_{j=1}^{d}\exp(2\pi{i}k_{j}g_{j}/n_{j})
, since each coordinate acts independently.

\triangleright

Symmetric Group
S_{3}
.
The group
S_{3}
of all 6 permutations of
\{1,2,3\}
has three representations.
Besides the trivial representation, there is also a one-dimensional
sign representation

\rho_{\rm sgn}(\sigma)={\rm sgn}(\sigma)\in\{+1,-1\}
, which maps even permutations to
+1
and odd permutations to
-1
.
Also, there is a two-dimensional representation
\rho_{\rm std}
that maps each permutation to a
2\times 2
matrix. Writing
\omega=e^{2\pi{i}/3}
, the six matrices are

\displaystyle\rho_{\rm std}({\mathrm{Id}})

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\quad

\displaystyle\rho_{\rm std}((1\;2\;3))

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\omega&0\\
0&\bar{\omega}\end{pmatrix},\quad

\displaystyle\rho_{\rm std}((1\;3\;2))

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\bar{\omega}&0\\
0&\omega\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\rho_{\rm std}((1\;2))

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},\quad

\displaystyle\rho_{\rm std}((1\;3))

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&\bar{\omega}\\
\omega&0\end{pmatrix},\quad

\displaystyle\rho_{\rm std}((2\;3))

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&\omega\\
\bar{\omega}&0\end{pmatrix}.

(E.1)

The three even permutations (top row) map to diagonal rotation matrices, while the three odd permutations (bottom row) map to off-diagonal reflection matrices.

\triangleright

Frobenius group
C_{7}\rtimes C_{3}
.
This group has five irreducible representations: three one-dimensional and two three-dimensional.
The
one-dimensional representations
are
\rho_{j}(x^{a}y^{b})=\omega_{3}^{jb}
for
j=0,1,2
, where
\omega_{3}=e^{2\pi{i}/3}
.
These are trivial on
x
and only focus the
C_{3}
-component
b
.
The
three-dimensional representations
are the nontrivial ones that arise from the non-Abelian structure of the group, and are the ones that networks must learn in our experiments.
They are specified by the images of the two generators. Writing
\omega_{7}=e^{2\pi{i}/7}
, we have

\displaystyle\rho_{4}(x)={\rm diag}(\omega_{7},\omega_{7}^{2},\omega_{7}^{4}),\qquad\rho_{5}(x)={\rm diag}(\omega_{7}^{3},\omega_{7}^{6},\omega_{7}^{5}),\qquad\rho_{4}(y)=\rho_{5}(y)=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&1\\
1&0&0\\
0&1&0\end{pmatrix},

One can verify the group relation
\rho(y)\rho(x)\rho(y)^{-1}=\rho(x^{2})
.
Indeed, the cyclic permutation matrix
\rho(y)
shifts the diagonal entries
(\omega_{7},\omega_{7}^{2},\omega_{7}^{4})
to
(\omega_{7}^{2},\omega_{7}^{4},\omega_{7})=(\omega_{7}^{2},\omega_{7}^{4},\omega_{7}^{8})=\rho(x^{2})
, where we used
\omega_{7}^{8}=\omega_{7}
since
8\equiv 1\pmod{7}
.
These
3\times 3
matrix-valued representations are the ones that networks must learn in our non-Abelian experiments (§
4.2
).

Dual Representation.

For each
\rho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)
, the
dual representation

\rho^{\vee}
is defined by

\displaystyle\rho^{\vee}(g)=\rho(g^{-1})^{\top}.

(E.2)

Since we always work with unitary representations, we have
\rho(g)^{-1}=\rho(g)^{*}
. Thus (
E.2
) simplifies to
\rho^{\vee}(g)=(\rho(g)^{*})^{\top}=\overline{\rho(g)}
, i.e., the entrywise complex conjugate.

The dual generalizes the notion of conjugate frequency in the standard DFT: for
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
, the dual of
\rho_{k}(g)=\exp({2\pi{i}kg/n})
is
\rho_{n-k}(g)=\exp(-2\pi{i}kg/n)
.
When
\rho\cong\rho^{\vee}
, i.e.,
\rho
and its conjugate are related by a change of basis, the representation is called
self-dual
or
self-conjugate
.
For example, the two-dimensional representation
\rho_{\rm std}
of
S_{3}
in (
E.1
) is self-conjugate: conjugating the matrices swaps
\omega\leftrightarrow\bar{\omega}
, and one can verify that
\rho_{\rm std}
and
\overline{\rho_{\rm std}}
are related by a
change of basis
.
In contrast, the two
3
-dimensional representations
\rho_{4}
and
\rho_{5}
of
C_{7}\rtimes C_{3}
are duals of each other but not self-conjugate.

Appendix F
Additional Results for Generalized Modular Addition

In §
3.2
, we focused on the cleaner Abelian setting without non-trivial self-conjugate representations, using
G=\mathbb{Z}_{3}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{5}
as the running example.
In this appendix, we record the full Fourier-domain heatmaps of the main-text experiment.
Moreover, we discuss the self-conjugate case, which requires a separate interpretation from the odd-order setting.
Finally, several clarification are made about the relation between our formulation and the cyclic modular-addition analysis of
13
.

F.1
Full Experimental Results in §
3

Figure
1
in the main text visualizes the single-frequency pattern through
\xi_{m}
.
Here, we provide the full Fourier-domain heatmaps for all three learned parameter blocks
(\theta_{m}^{1},\theta_{m}^{2},\xi_{m})
on the same task
G=\mathbb{Z}_{3}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{5}
.
Figure
9
complements the compressed visualization in Figure
1
.
It shows that the same single-frequency structure appears in
\theta_{m}^{1}
,
\theta_{m}^{2}
, and
\xi_{m}
: each neuron concentrates on one shared non-trivial frequency tuple together with its conjugate partner, and Hermitian symmetry forces the two active coefficients to have equal real parts and opposite imaginary parts.
Thus the sparsity pattern is shared across all three parameter families, not only in the output embedding.

Figure 9
:
Heatmap of the learned parameters for the top 20 neurons on the generalized modular addition task over
G=\mathbb{Z}_{3}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{5}
, after applying the Discrete Fourier Transform. Each row corresponds to one neuron, and the three columns of panels correspond to
\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}
,
\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}
, and
\widehat{\xi_{m}}
, respectively. The upper row plots the real parts and the lower row plots the imaginary parts of the Fourier coefficients. Along the horizontal axis, each column is indexed by a frequency tuple
k
, with conjugate frequencies arranged symmetrically. Since
G=\mathbb{Z}_{3}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{5}
has no non-trivial self-conjugate representations, each active neuron shows exactly one conjugate pair of nonzero coefficients at
k
and
k^{\vee}
.

F.2
Modular Addition with Self-Conjugate Irreps

For a product group
G_{\mathcal{N}}=\mathbb{Z}_{n_{1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{n_{d}}
, a frequency tuple is self-conjugate exactly when each even-order coordinate is either
0
or
n_{j}/2
. As a concrete example, we consider

G=\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{3}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{5},

with
29
non-trivial representations. Among them,
k=(1,0,0)
is the unique non-trivial self-conjugate frequency, while the remaining
28
frequencies form
14
conjugate pairs.

Figure 10
:
Heatmap of the learned parameters for the top 20 neurons on the generalized modular addition task over
G=\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{3}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{5}
, after applying the Discrete Fourier Transform. Each row corresponds to one neuron, and the three columns of panels correspond to
\widehat{\theta_{m}^{1}}
,
\widehat{\theta_{m}^{2}}
,
\widehat{\xi_{m}}
, respectively. Along the horizontal axis, each column is indexed by a frequency tuple
k
, and conjugate frequencies are arranged symmetrically. The dashed separators isolate the self-conjugate sector, consisting of the trivial frequency and the unique non-trivial self-conjugate frequency. Neurons that learn a non-self-conjugate representation therefore show two mirrored active coefficients at
k
and
k^{\vee}
, while neurons that learn the self-conjugate mode show a single active real coefficient in the middle sector.

Results.

The dominant mechanism from the odd-order case remains unchanged: most neurons still learn a single non-trivial frequency together with its conjugate partner, so Observations
3.2
and
3.2
continue to describe the learned structure. The only new phenomenon occurs when a neuron selects the self-conjugate frequency
k=(1,0,0)
. Since this representation satisfies
\rho_{k}=\rho_{k}^{\vee}
, its Fourier coefficient has no distinct conjugate partner and must therefore be real. Empirically, such neurons appear as a single active coefficient with vanishing imaginary part in the isolated middle sector.

This changes the interpretation of diversification in exactly one place. For a non-self-conjugate frequency, the neuron still carries a
continuous phase

\exp({i}\phi_{m})
with
\phi_{m}\sim{\rm Unif}(0,2\pi]
.
In this case, the phase space collapses to the
discrete Rademacher
sign set
\{\pm 1\}
. Thus, the self-conjugate case does not represent a new learning mechanism, but rather a boundary case of the general spectral framework where the complex phase reduces to a real-valued sign.

F.3
Comparison with Results in
13

The cyclic-group results of
13
are the one-dimensional special case of our framework. For
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
with odd
n
, each irrep is a character
\rho_{k}(g)=\exp(2\pi{i}kg/n)
, and a sinusoid is simply its real part:

\cos(\omega_{k}g+\phi_{m})=\Re(\exp({{i}\phi_{m}})\cdot\rho_{k}(g)).

Thus the scalar frequencies used in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
are replaced in our framework by irreducible representations, and the cyclic story becomes a special case of the representation-theoretic picture.
For odd
n
, the non-trivial characters of
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
come in conjugate pairs
(k,n-k)
, so any real-valued feature admits the expansion

\nu(g)=\widehat{\nu}[\rho_{0}]+\sum_{k=1}^{(n-1)/2}2|\widehat{\nu}[\rho_{k}]|\cdot\cos(\omega_{k}g+\phi_{k}),\qquad\omega_{k}=\frac{2\pi k}{n}.

Following this, the cyclic phenomena in
13
are exactly our single-frequency sparsity, phase alignment, and diversification specialized to 1D irreps.
In particular, tied embeddings give
\arg(\widehat{\xi}_{m}[\rho_{k}])=2\phi_{m}\bmod 2\pi
, and the ensemble predictor reduces to Lemma
C.4
on
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, with the main peak at
x+y
and ghost peaks at
2x
and
2y
.
What our framework adds is the extension beyond this cyclic, odd-order setting. First, it covers arbitrary finite Abelian products rather than a single cyclic factor. Second, it makes explicit how the picture changes when self-conjugate sectors are present. In particular, prior modular-addition analyses effectively avoid this issue by working with odd
p
, whereas our formulation shows that even-order groups fit into the same spectral mechanism after replacing Haar-random phases on self-conjugate modes by Rademacher signs.
